Acknowledgments
First, I wish to thank Josip Lukin with whom I wrote the second, fourth, and
fifth essay. Josip also reviewed all other essays and made many objections,
suggestions, and corrections.
Besides Josip I also wish to thank Luciano Bazzochi, Boran Berčić,
Stipe Buzar, Carol Caraway, Newton Garver, Russell Goodman, Peter
Hacker, late Wasfi Hijab, Borna Jalšenjak, Jeff Jordan, Jim Klagge, Timm
Lampert, Jonathan Lowe, Winfried Löffler, Ivan Macan, Daniel Miščin,
Damir Mladić, Karel Mom, late Siniša Očuršćak, Michael Parnham, Davor
Pećnjak, Alois Pichler, Alvin Plantinga, Duncan Richter, Nicholas Rescher,
Joachim Schulte, Neven Sesardić, Nenad Smokrović, Ivan Spajić, Thomas
Wallgren, and Anja Weiberg for their helpful critical comments and sugges-
tions regarding these papers during their development and during discus-
sions after paper presentations.
Thirdly, apart from previously mentioned philosophers, I must refer to
Wittgenstein scholars from all over the world that I met during the last
decade in Kirchberg am Wechsel, Austria at the International Ludwig Witt-
genstein Symposium (sometimes referred to as Kirchbergians). Discussing
many topics with them on numerous formal and informal occasions regard-
ing Wittgenstein’s later philosophy was of substantive value for the quality
of these texts.
Next I wish to thank my students and colleagues at my home institutions,
Philosophical Faculty of the Society of Jesus in Zagreb and Zagreb School of
Economics and Management, for their assistance in times when papers were
completed and presented.
For proofreading the text I am very grateful to Luna Polić, Heather L.
Smith, and Ivana Bušljeta Banks.

Sources
The papers collected in this volume originally appeared in journals, sympo-
sia proceedings, or essays collections. However, if a paper was presented as
a lecture on multiple occasions, only the most recent occasions on which a
paper was delivered will be mentioned.
Chapter 1 (sections 2, 3, and 4) was delivered in Zadar, Croatia, at the
DECOS International Conference (Describing Complex Systems), 2008, and
was later published in K. Krkač Illegal Substances and Unnatural Kinds,
Trope Ontology as a Narrative Idealism (2009, www.filozofija.org, but
only sections 2–4). Parts 1, 5, and 6 were delivered as a lecture at IUC in
Dubrovnik, Croatia, during the international course Logical Foundations of
Metaphysics at IUC, Dubrovnik, Croatia, 2009.
Chapter 2 was previously published, K. Krkač, J. Lukin Wittgenstein the
Morphologist I, Synthesis Philosophica, 46, (2/2008) pp. 427–438. Section
2.5 is published in Disputatio Philosophica 1 (2009) pp. 3–23.
Chapter 3 was not previously published. However, the second part was
delivered during the international course Logical Foundations of Metaphysics
at IUC, Dubrovnik, Croatia, 2008, and the third part was delivered at Institut
D’etudes Politique de Lille, University of Lille 2, France, 2009/2010 as a part
of the course Introduction to Ethics, Business Ethics and CSR.
Chapter 4 (sections 1 and 2) was delivered during the ALWS symposium
(2007) and published: Krkač K., Lukin J. Forms of life as forms of culture
in: H. Hrachovec, A. Pichler, J. Wang (eds.) Philosophy and Information
Society, Papers of 30. International Wittgenstein Symposium, Kirchberg am
Wechsel, ALWS, Austria, 2007:112–14. Sections 3–5 are published here
for the first time and were delivered during a Postgraduate studies course in
Analytic Philosophy at the Philosophical Faculty of the Society of Jesus in
Zagreb, Croatia, 2007/2008.

xix
Introduction: Fighting windmills
The valleys of foolishness have more grass growing in them for the phi-
losopher than do the barren heights of cleverness. (CV 92)
On an occasion such as this, it would be appropriate to begin with the note
that Wittgenstein’s philosophy interests me as far as it is one of the finest
examples of a philosophical morphology in twentieth century philosophy,
and this is a pivotal reason why this collection of essays begins with a some-
what detailed philosophical morphology primer, i.e. in order to portray the
background and the rough ground of my vital proposal, namely, that Witt-
genstein’s later philosophy is philosophical morphology at least in terms of
its methods and goals. Nonetheless, Wittgenstein is always my starting point,
but on the other hand, rarely the ending point. Furthermore, I often start with
technical exegesis (mostly summarizing some new or major results with my
small supplement), but end with some notes regarding the matter in question
which are hard to join with the beginning of the text for one who does not see
the goal of philosophy as I indeed do. Occasionally this ratio is on the side of
exegesis, and sometimes on the side of further development and application
of some issues. Exegesis is concentrated on a small number of concepts, i.e.
morphology, perspicuous presentation, forms of life, practice, consciousness,
certainty, and religious belief. This point needs some additional elucidation.
On the philosophical book market, there were and there still are many ex-
cellent works on Wittgenstein, from general introductions, dictionaries, and
collections of essays on his major works, to the brilliant exegetical books and
papers on his crucial ideas published in symposia proceedings and in the finest
philosophical journals. There are even books that summarize and comment on
this huge bibliography, and books that produce a kind of meta-commentary, i.e.
some kind of order in this large amount of works and interpretations, especially


xx
Introduction
regarding different schools (and depths) of interpretation (so much regarding
(over)interpreting Wittgenstein). A lifetime is not enough to study it all. Fortu-
nately, the subject matter of these essays are somewhat constrained and therefore
my primary and secondary bibliography. The first restriction is concerning the
fact that these essays are on Wittgenstein’s later philosophy, mostly on PI and
OC. The second limitation concerns the subject of these essays. Namely, all top-
ics belong to theoretical philosophy for which, absurdly enough, it is claimed
that in Wittgenstein’s case springs from a practice. The third limitation is re-
garding the fact that I understand these essays as critical and developmental in
relation to Wittgenstein’s own ideas. These notions call for further clarification
as well.
The word critical refers to an approach to some of the results of this kind
of philosophy, i.e. high-tech exegesis of Wittgenstein’s texts, especially
regarding MS’s and TS’s (in the last decade or so). On the other hand, and
quite opposite to the results of such hard and honest scholarly work, stand
many papers, chapters in books with different subjects, and books on Witt-
genstein which are loosely connected to his philosophy and are by no means
referred to as a kind of creative continuation and a sort of development of
Wittgenstein’s ideas. However, such works are often regarded as mislead-
ing, confusing, superfluous, and alike regarding correct, often contradictory,
interpretation(s) of Wittgenstein’s ideas by commentators engaged in techni-
cal exegesis, biographers, and historians of philosophy (which nevertheless
casts a shadow of doubt on the use of the word correct). Now, my point is
not to criticise Wittgenstein’s arguments, since in my opinion there are no
arguments. But, there are photographs, pictures and sketches (short poems as
it were), and what I tried to do was to offer an additional picture, or sketch,
or at least to alter a sketch a little bit in order to accentuate what needs to be
highlighted. Closely connected to this, another part of the critical approach is
condensed in the idea that there is at most 0.5 Wittgenstein (CV 16, MS 154
1v: 1931). This point will be clarified later on.
The word developmental on the other hand, refers to a particular approach
to some of the results of this kind of philosophy. Mentioned Wittgenstein’s
pictures and sketches are in my opinion half-finished and what I try to do is to
offer various directions of their ending, if such task seems to be appropriate.
If everyday language or colloquial speech is in fact morphological, then what
I try here is to talk about philosophical morphology using ordinary language
(humble use), and to avoid technical vocabulary as much as it is possible.
For instance, if one agrees with G. Baker that the concept of perspicuous
presentation is not itself so perspicuous, then one should try to make it more
perspicuous using precisely such ordinary language. The expression itself,
which is “a perspicuous presentation,” is in fact clear description or simply



Fighting windmills
xxi
synopsis (surely it is not an abridgement). Conversely, are the words clear,
as well as are the phenomena clearly described, are another issues (PI 24). In
other words, here, I took the middle road between these extreme approaches
since I believe that it reflects situations in which I de facto found myself when
I was writing these papers, i.e. being intensely affected, influenced and above
all inspired by Wittgenstein’s texts. Perhaps it can be of some interest for the
reader to mention that I started as an epistemologist in early 90’s and besides
the textbook “The Philosophy of Knowledge” of my professor Ivan Macan
and classical 20th century epistemology readings my first books on the subject
were R. Rorty’s “Philosophy and The Mirror of Nature,” M. Merleau-Ponty’s
“Phenomenology of Perception,” and L. Wittgenstein’s “On Certainty,” so
one engaged in more traditional analytic epistemology can imagine the odd
context of my starting point in investigating Wittgenstein’s works (the de-
parture partially described by K. Morris). Regarding this point the following
passage from “Culture and Value” (CV 70) can be quoted: “It is not by any
means clear to me that I wish for a continuation of my work by others, more
than a change in a way we live, making all these questions superfluous.”
Nonetheless, regarding the intermediate approach it is sensible to differ be-
tween critical (exegetical, biographical, and historical likewise) understand-
ings of Wittgenstein’s philosophy, and to move on from his own questions,
method, and solutions to some other themes, obstacles, and elucidations (to
some developments); continuing with Wittgenstein’s idea of a conversion
to a new worldview, and in the same time performing as it were some kind
of application, sometimes further in the direction he pointed to, sometimes
regarding topics which are quite new and loosely connected with his paths in
philosophy. However, it seems impossible to do the second job without the
first. In fact, it seems to me that Wittgenstein is, so to speak, an encouraging
(or motivating) philosopher (like Heraclitus, Augustine, de Montaigne, Pas-
cal, Nietzsche, Moore, Austin, Frankfurt, or Rorty in my own case), that is to
say a philosopher who can very easily inspire one to move on without under-
standing what he/she is in fact reading, or should think about, still this simple
idea is clouded by his own search for precision, clarity, esprit de finesse, and
specific style of writing which appears more like a specific poetic style than
what is expected of philosophical style (noted by two German poets). This
is so because it is not easy to differ between these two tasks, and at the same
time, because it is very difficult to understand that these two tasks are one and
the same; as it were, different aspects of a single process of philosophising
(As twentieth century Croatian poet Tin Ujević pointed out in one of his criti-
cal essays, that the poem must be stern and strange in the same time, which
in my opinion nicely summarises Wittgenstein’s style. However, I would like
to add that a poem should be humorous too).


xxii
Introduction
Nevertheless, the second task was always more important to me in view of
the fact that if one wants to know wherefrom the problems of a philosopher
are coming, one should look at his/her life, the way he/she is living it, and
how he/she does things; furthermore, by whom he/she was influenced, who
his/her contemporaries and critics are, and one will surely understand why
these obstacles, philosophical or others, are significant for a philosopher and
his/her ideas (on this point I agree with R. Monk’s ideas regarding the issue
of philosophical biography, Monk 2001:3–16). However, Wittgenstein’s life
and influences seem to be investigated sufficiently enough in order to make a
more or less sound statement, at least regarding certain vital moments of the
overlap of his life struggles and his philosophical pretensions (OC 549) that
are keys for the present inquiry. On the other hand, to accomplish such an
enterprise, I understood that I needed to appreciate Wittgenstein in his own
right, i.e. to add something to the known, sometimes commonly accepted,
and sometimes not so significant or known, interpretations of his ideas. Sub-
sequently, I came up with an idea that nowadays seems to be somewhat con-
troversial, i.e., that Wittgenstein was a pragmatist, in fact a European style
pragmatist, in times when (American) pragmatism was already developed
and suffered basic criticism, and an old-school morphologist in somewhat
unfortunate times and circumstances regarding the position of morphology
in society, culture, science, and philosophy also (mostly due to misinterpre-
tations and misuses of Spengler’s work, and a certain lack of philosophical
interest for Goethe). In addition, his new idea was the one about explicating
philosophical grammar. In last two decades or so Wittgenstein’s philosophi-
cal morphology (he said to Norman Malcolm “What I give is the morphology
of the use of an expression.” Malcolm 1984:43) is fairly investigated in its
own right, as well as in the light of the influences of Goethe and Spengler
(particularly by B. McGuinness, M. W. Rowe, and J. Schulte). Regarding
the morphological approach in Wittgenstein’s philosophy, I owe very much
to communication with P. Hacker and J. Schulte, to works by R. Monk,
and to some morphologists working in other fields (especially psychology,
mathematics, biology, and cultural anthropology and whom I met on various
occasions during the last decade).
On the other hand, the idea of pragmatism (particularly European) needs
some clarification at least for Wittgenstein scholars engaged in some dis-
putes on certain technical topics of Wittgenstein’s philosophy as well as for
some who are engaged in, as previously mentioned, creative continuation
and development. In a way, Wittgenstein came up with an idea that looked
like pragmatism to him but a worldview got in the way. What he had in
mind was probably pragmatism in James’s style, and what he had in mind
by “worldview” will be clarified in the fourth chapter. Nevertheless, he used



Fighting windmills
xxiii
the word pragmatism only once, namely in OC. The influence of W. James
on Wittgenstein, which seems to be the most important, is quite well inves-
tigated and commented upon chiefly regarding pragmatism and philosophy
of psychology (I owe very much to discussions with R. B. Goodman and
his book Wittgenstein and William James, and to H. Putnam’s lecture Was
Wittgenstein a Pragmatist? later published in his book Pragmatism, An open
Question). In addition, the influence of the methods of the Austrian school
reform movement and of his friend and colleague from Cambridge University
P. Sraffa should be equally emphasised regarding this matter. Nevertheless,
what he was saying by his position in OC regarding pragmatism is that his
solution only looks like pragmatism not that it is pragmatism. What I am try-
ing to argue is that it is a pragmatism and in fact it is a certain combination of
American pragmatism (like in James then, or for example, Rorty and Putnam
in last decades) and European pragmatism (very controversial idea in its own
right like in Pascal, Nietzsche, Mill, Kierkegaard, Schiller, as well as French
and Italian schools at the beginning of twentieth century, possibly in Austin
and Habermas later).
A certain blend of these pragmatism(s), morphology, and the idea of a
philosophical grammar is very differently manifested in Wittgenstein’s later
works, particularly in his last writings, and specifically, regarding diverse
series of questions in these periods, i.e. nature of philosophy, method, math-
ematics, language, mind, knowledge, ethics, aesthetics, culture (anthropol-
ogy), religion, etc. This may be a sign of discontinuity among later writings;
alternatively, it could be an expression of finding the right expression.
On the other hand, this manifestation was always so to say half-explicated,
or more positively speaking, open-ended due to various causes, from the sty-
listic ones to the issues that were at stake. By this, I do not imply that there are
two Wittgensteins, or three, or even four Wittgensteins (Wittgenstein himself
thought that he made significant change in his later period compared to the
earlier one, “And this is similar to when a painter makes a transition from one
school to another” (PPO 149, Baker, Hacker Vol. 1, Part I, 2005:257 men-
tioning Kandinsky and Van Gogh, but Picasso seems to be a better example)).
Rather that there is at most one Wittgenstein, maybe 0.5 barely. The question
is where the other half is? The answer is twofold; that is to say, there is the
absent half, and the present one.
•  Therefore, there is the present philosophical part consisting of his philo-
sophical works. In the present part there is constant struggle to say what
needs to be said in the proper, clear, and elegant way. However, this is
often proved to be a difficult task. This particular part consists of allusion
to the other one, namely the absent part, as in the following passage: “Here


xxiv
Introduction
I am inclined to fight windmills, because I cannot yet say the thing I really
want to say” (OC 400). There are two distinct (half) philosophical reasons
why this absent part is in fact “absent”; the first one presented in the TLP
period, and the second one presented in PI and the later period respectively.
•  Namely, in his TLP period it is represented by the idea that some things
cannot be said, that they can only be shown. This particular saying/showing
distinction occupies hundreds of pages of exegesis into which I cannot en-
ter here. However, the point could be illustrated by mentioning his famous,
often cited, remark from his letter to L. von Ficker where he claims that
TLP consists of two parts, the second of which, i.e. the unwritten one (I
suppose, the one which cannot be written down at all), is far more impor-
tant. (FL 10./11.19)
•  The later reason, that is to say in PI and later period, is represented by the
idea of use, actions, practices, techniques, institutions, forms of life, etc.
The point can be illustrated by many quotations mostly from PI and RFM,
yet it is of some importance for my present purpose to quote a part of the
following sections of OC, namely, that a “…[p]ractice has to speak for
itself” (OC 139), “I know all that. And that will come out in the way I act,
and in the way I speak…” (OC 395), and the one from ROC, “Practices
give words their meaning.” (ROC III 320).
Of course, I cannot pin down particular conclusive evidence for such a claim,
to repeat, that there is a far more important absent part (it is more something
like “the spirit of the whole,” CV 8–9), and consequently one should not
put any additional weight to these claims than to any others, including quite
dissimilar ones. On the other hand, there seems to be another source on the
background of which one could illuminate the answer to the question—why
there is at most 0.5 Wittgenstein, i.e. the present (philosophical) half, or in
other words, to answer to the question—where is the other half?
•  This particular source consists of his love of and participation in art—
namely music, architecture, literature, poetry, sculpturing, helping mem-
bers of the art world, etc. These can be categorised as enjoying the beauty
of artwork, reproducing artwork, and creating art. This point would not
be of any relevance whatsoever if it were not intimately connected to his
views regarding philosophical method and aim, but indeed it is, and in that
light the following passages can serve as indications in a pointed direction.
•  “A thinker is very similar to a draughtsman. Who wants to represent all the
interconnections.” “And what I basically am after all is a painter, & often
a very bad painter.” “I believe I summed up where I stand in philosophy
when I said: really one should write philosophy only as one writes a poem.”



Fighting windmills
xxv
(CV 14, 95, 28, see Schulte 2004:146–65, and Gibson and Huemer 2004)
“It is my intention to whistle this theme…” (RPP II 575) “I often think that
the highest I wish to achieve would be to compose a melody” “Style is the
expression of a general human necessity. This holds for a writing style or
a building style (and any other).” (PPO 17, 37; 37 is a paraphrase of Spen-
gler, see Schulte 1990:6, 6; here I must warn the reader to take a closer look
at the original context of these sections, mainly these from CV).
In the light of this interpretative framework, Wittgenstein’s already mentioned
training for teaching in primary schools (particularly regarding the nature of
training, namely Arbeitsschule, work-school, see Savickey 1999:49–77), the
influence of W. James and P. Sraffa (the latter is the only one mentioned in
the Preface of PI, a preface which is clearly written for some earlier version
since there is not much discussion of, for instance, the foundations of math-
ematics, see RFM), his interest in engineering and models, architecture, mu-
sic, literature, poetry, and his quite peculiar attitude toward religion, culture,
civilisation (mostly Anglo-American), politics, even some political systems
and persons, and so on, this fits in agreeably.
Perhaps the decisive point of his later philosophy which in a way summa-
rizes and at the same time is the best example of a paradigm of morphological
method, philosophical grammar, and pragmatism is the very phenomenon
and the concept of form of life which cumulates (condenses) these three as-
pects of the paradigm.
•  “No single sentence can summarize Wittgenstein’s later philosophy. But
the aphorism words have meaning only in the stream of life expresses one
pervasive theme.” (Stroll 2000:113, see Stern 2004)
Forms of life seems to be very important for later Wittgenstein in the way that
forms of life are crucial for the majority of his clarifications and explications,
and it can be summarized in the following way—to perform like humans do
perform, to use things, to act, to live a life of a human being, no matter in
which aspect (everyday action, language, knowledge, consciousness, technol-
ogy, history, art, and religion) means to have as a context and as an interpreta-
tive background (which is a rough ground as a valley of foolishness), to be a
part of, and to co-create and co-invent particular forms of life.
I start the first part of the present collection of essays with a development
of philosophical morphology without substantive relation to Wittgenstein’s
philosophy (Chapter 1). In fact, I mention his ideas in only a few places.
In that light, the first chapter, which in fact tries to be a primer and a kind
of introduction, should be understood as a covering text, to which all other


xxvi
Introduction
chapters more or less serve as detailed explications in order to clarify basic
elements of the mentioned paradigm. It consists of many examples which
should introduce the reader into a different way of seeing. Subsequent chap-
ters examine elements in Wittgenstein’s work which were introduced in the
first chapter (Chapters 2, 3, and 4). Many examples from the first part and
particularly from the first chapter will be repeated and presented in more de-
tail in the chapters of the second part. Also, some more or less precise theses
will be introduced in the second part, i.e. that Wittgenstein is not a behaviour-
ist regarding consciousness (Chapter 5), that he was not a foundationalist or
a coherentist regarding epistemic justification (Chapters 6 and 7), and that he
was not a fideist regarding religious belief (Chapter 8). Therefore, chapters
of the first part are to be considered as an introduction to and summary of the
topic, while chapters of the second part are concerned with quite minute and
tiny issues of his philosophy, essentially as applications of these three general
ideas to particular issues. The issue of method is discussed in essays from the
second to the fourth chapters. Thus, in a way all topics and elucidations of
his later philosophy can be interpreted as examples of different forms of life,
language-games, grammars, overviews, and morphologies.
When I developed this idea in various directions I tried to use the mor-
phological method (perspicuous presentation) by which I tried to achieve “a
philosophical grammar,” or at least to point out the direction of a philosophi-
cal grammar of a particular phenomenon in question, such as consciousness,
perception, and certainty (writing a particular chapter on a particular topic
of the philosophical grammar was and still is a distant ideal). In doing so,
the problem was how to understand and practice morphological method ap-
propriately, and on the other hand, regarding the mentioned shift from Witt-
genstein’s own ideas on, how not to utter anything which the grammar does
not allow to be said. These different issues, in fact my struggle with stating
the obvious, are discussed in the second part, namely in essays from fifth to
the eight chapters. By this division of topics, I did not want to suggest any
kind of sharp distinction between say principles and cases, since principles
are comprehensible only in virtue of cases and examples (perhaps the best
examples are not examples at all, rather, via consisting solely of grammatical
remarks, they are paradigm cases that de facto manifest implicit but already
present principles).
Nonetheless, one could claim, something must be taught us as a founda-
tion (OC 449) and at the same time one does not need to believe in real
foundations (to be a fundament in a course of teaching and learning is not
the same as to be a fundament in the philosophical grammar). In other words,
the words foundations, basics, and the similar that are instantiated in the first
four chapters are used as educational and pedagogical concepts. Nothing is



Fighting windmills
xxvii
fundamental, essential, obvious, given, clear, or evident per se; it is such only
within a certain framework, certain context, language-game, world-picture,
and a form of life. The context of these chapters can be regarded as a kind
of training for a certain way of seeing (self-training as well), i.e. seeing that
morphology is the philosophical method and that philosophical grammar is
the distinctive aim of philosophical investigations.
On the other hand, there is no shallow end to the philosophical pool
(Strawson 1992), therefore, even the first part is as it were overcrowded with
examples (and overcrowded with an absence of a philosophical substance
(and as being overcrowded no matter if the presence or the absence is in ques-
tion), which surely is a kind of kitsch, see Eco 1989:180–217). One does not
need to be a philosopher in order to understand the first chapter. However,
chapters 2, 3, and 4 get into exegesis of Wittgenstein’s text somewhat deeper
in order to demonstrate that he was engaged in morphology and philosophi-
cal grammar presented in the first chapter. Consequently, examples in the
second part are far more complicated compared to the examples in the first
part. However, the distinction is in intensity, not in category. The present
volume can be studied from the beginning as well as from the end depending
on one’s interests. These examples are chosen partly because of personal idio-
syncratic reasons, and partly because I strongly believe that they demonstrate
that the morphology and the philosophical grammar of various phenomena
are undoubtedly possible. Therefore, the morphology and the grammar of
consciousness, certainty, and religion are presented as a series of more com-
plicated examples.
Conversely, my final point is not to stop at these after examining all spe-
cific philosophical issues, rather to point to issues that are morphological and
grammatical in sciences, arts, crafts, skills or techniques, and in everyday life
too. My hidden assumption (which obviously is not hidden any more) is that
humans are natural born morphologists and grammarians. Take for instance
emotions (feelings) as a language, which are initially manifested and mastered
in early stages of child development. Infants are capable not only of manifest-
ing (at least basic) emotions, but of recognising them quite precisely as well
(face recognition as a morphological procedure), and even further, to using
them to communicate more complicated messages. Even newborn infants
exhibit a great variety of postural and even facial expressions. By the end of
the third year of life, a full range of adult emotions can be said to exist (Lewis
2000:265). Now, we know that emotions are very complicated for an infant
(and for an adult) to manifest, but to recognise as well. The question then is
how is this possible, since infants do not posses any kind of sufficiently de-
veloped analytical powers? Yet they are capable of recognising a great variety
of facial expressions of their parents and to manifest certain expressions as


xxviii
Introduction
responses. My guess is that infants recognise small differences (they can be
fooled but they are rarely wrong) in facial expressions and other emotional
manifestations and at the same time they see the whole picture of a particular
emotion, and this is an example of morphological method par excellence.
However, let me return to rough ground. Forms of life can be described.
Such descriptions should be clear to one to whom they are presented for a
particular purpose. The idea of philosophical grammar seems to be the core of
perspicuous presentation since it is manifested in and throughout grammati-
cal remarks. I learned much from books like Beth Savickey’s Wittgenstein’s
Art of Investigation (1999), i.e. from books that highlight the very examples
of the method of grammatical investigation, as well as from various excellent
scholarly works on Wittgenstein’s concept of philosophical grammar and
morphology which were indispensable to me as well (like for example works
by Baker and Hacker, Forster, Garver, Monk, Schulte, and others).
All of the papers presented in this collection, if it is possible and conve-
nient, should be understood in the light of the morphology as a method, the
philosophical grammar as a goal, and the idea of pragmatism which empha-
sizes not so much consequences of actions, rather the very fact that we act in
certain way, that we have certain forms of life, and that we as humans have
mastered certain techniques, routines or standard practices.


Part One
WITTGENSTEIN AND
PHILOSOPHICAL MORPHOLOGY


3
Chapter One
Philosophical Morphology Primer
What I give is the morphology of the use of an expression. (Wittgenstein,
in Malcolm 1984:43) But the essence of language is a picture of the es-
sence of the world; and philosophy as custodian of grammar can in fact
grasp the essence of the world, only not in the propositions of language,
but in rules for this language, which exclude nonsensical combinations of
signs. (PR 54) Wittgenstein’s method of grammatical investigation is com-
plex and detailed. It is also very natural and playful. […] However, if we
are attentive to his methodological remarks and demonstrations, and if we
put his grammatical methods into practice, we will not be spared the trou-
ble of thinking but stimulated to thoughts of our own. (Savickey 1999:6)
1.1. INTRODUCTION
This chapter will present the basics of philosophical morphology and philo-
sophical grammar through a series of examples of visual phenomena. None-
theless, Wittgenstein’s ideas are still not the prime subject matter in this chap-
ter; nonetheless, there are some hints, concepts, and phenomena mentioned
that would be discussed in more detail in the subsequent chapters. Besides
this introduction and concluding remarks, which summarises basic ideas of
philosophical morphology, this chapter is divided into six sections.
1.  The first part explores the very possibility of philosophical morphology
and grammar, by means of perhaps the simplest case of all, that is—the
case of a dot (and some more complex phenomena such as lines, basic
geometrical figures, and solids, and via these cases the very possibility of
identification of phenomena).


4
Chapter One
2.  The second part investigates basic morphological assumptions, that is to
say the assumption that all phenomena are more or less directly mutually
similar or dissimilar, and that any given pair of phenomena cannot be
completely identical, or completely different regarding all of their features
or characteristics.
3.  In the third part some morphological relations are explicated: relations
such as similarity, dissimilarity, analogy, disanalogy, pattern, and network.
4.  The fourth part focuses on the question—which one of the relations expli-
cated in the previous section is the basic (morphological) relation? In order
to answer the question some distinctions are made, i.e. between logical,
morphological, and phenomenological views, and in addition, further anal-
ysis of the relation of being more/less similar/dissimilar is presented also.
5.  The fifth part includes discussion about some elements of the purpose and
the goal of philosophical morphology, that is to say clear descriptions or
perspicuous (re)presentations that consists of grammatical remarks (GP,
philosophical grammar of the use of certain words, or of certain groups of
phenomena, or actions). This part includes the only non-visual example in
the whole chapter, that is, the case of a daily cultural pattern.
6.  Finally, the last part explicates overall relativity as inherent to all morpho-
logical investigations and findings. In a way, it represents the broadening
of particular cases of relativity explicated in the first section by making
them no more than particular cases of as it were overall morphological and
grammatical relativity.
This chapter provides a place for philosophical morphology as a method,
and a place for philosophical grammar as an aim of philosophical inquiry
(which is instantiated in perspicuous presentations or clear descriptions of
phenomena). In this particular sense, the investigation is conceptual, and
overcrowded with examples. On the other hand, these examples are not just
examples. Most of the time they serve as instantiations of grammar too, since
explicating / pointing to the obvious is not an easy job to do, and therefore
the reader herself / himself should try to explicate the grammar too (“Phi-
losophy simply puts everything before us, and neither explains, nor deduces
anything. Since everything lies open to view there is nothing to explain.” PI
126) Nonetheless, these examples are mostly of visual phenomena because
they are the simplest among all examples. Compare for instance examples
including something that can be seen with an example of something that
can be smelled, heard, tasted, or touched, or compare perceptual phenomena
with for instance motivation, reasoning, or emotions, or even with economic,
social, or cultural phenomena (or “possibility of phenomena” PI 90). There
is the issue of simplicity also, since, an example of a clear description of a



Philosophical Morphology Primer
5
dot is clearly simpler than an example of clear description of a painting (or,
compare whistling a single tone with whistling a whole composition). In
addition, this is so because morphology is all about looking-at, looking-for,
seeing-as, and describing phenomena. In that light, some prerequisites for the
reader are clearly needed, such as some basic knowledge of Wittgenstein’s
later philosophy, some knowledge of morphology (for instance at least in
some scientific, cultural, or art fields), and some basics of visual literacy (see
for instance Dondis 1990 or any other primer in the field).
•  The essence of philosophical morphology as a philosophical method is
condensed in Wittgenstein’s remark “Don’t think, but look!” (PI 66), and
•  the essence of philosophical grammar as the goal of the application of
morphological method is condensed in the claim—state nothing but the
obvious and “leave everything as it is” (PI 124, see also 126), and “Don’t
explain!—Describe!” (PPO 191).
1.2. DOTS, SPOTS, SPECKS, LINES, AND SOLIDS
1.2.1. I’m seeing spots—The Case of a ●
Philosophical morphology welcomes, observes, and describes phenomena
of all kinds regarding all of their characteristics (that is to say that, nothing
is left out, explained away, or reduced). The aim of morphology is clear
description, or to put it to some extent odd “a perspicuous (re)presentation”
(PI 122). At the beginning of creating any clear description is looking-
at a phenomena, and looking-for connections between them, or between
their parts (similarities, analogies, patterns, networks), and ultimately
seeing—(them)—as wholes, or as nets of connections (different kinds of
regularities). It would be appropriate to start with some linguistic phe-
nomena; still, one should move on to non-linguistic phenomena too (since
LGs are parts of FOLs). Presenting the basic morphological procedures as
looking-at a phenomenon and seeing-it-as can be somewhat tricky since
most phenomena can be seen in many different ways. Therefore, the ques-
tion of the identification and relativity of a phenomenon is often the hard
one. Further in this section this point will be illustrated by means of quite
a simple example. Imagine that what is given, what one sees, is this [
 ●.
It is of some importance that at least in this early stage of inquiry one does
not give a name to it (and to any investigated phenomenon connected to it),
or a description, and the reason for this should be obvious in light of fur-
ther explications. To provide an unorganised series of features (something


6
Chapter One
like primordial description) is sufficient for the moment (meaning without
counting them in some particular order, or connecting them with any kind
of a conjunction, taken simply as a heap of characteristics). Now, let us
look at it. The first likely impression is that it is quite easy to say what one
sees. Nonetheless, it will be shown that it is dreadfully difficult if not al-
most impossible, to say what it is without relevant ambiguity, which plays
a major role in overall morphological relativity (here it should be pointed
out that relativity of a description presents a serious issue only if one dis-
connects a description from the activity of describing, and describing from
its practical purpose). The first obvious candidate for a description of the
mentioned phenomenon could be a small coloured round since what is seen
is by no means small, coloured, and round, i.e. a small black round. Other,
as it will turn out later on, in fact much worse candidates, would be a dot
(or spot, or speck), or a hole, because one confuses what one sees with the
background of it by making the background in a way an essential part of
it. In view of the fact that indeed it is printed on a white paper this by no
means creates the particular context, that is the background-context, by use
of which one could without a shred of doubt claim to see a black dot, or a
spot on a white piece of paper. This would be the case represented on the
left side of Figure 1.1.
Now, one could claim that one sees the black hole on a white or light grey
piece of paper like it is represented on the right side of the Figure 1.1. Say
Figure 1.1. A dot and a hole.



Philosophical Morphology Primer
7
that ● we call α, a dot β, a hole γ, seeing S, and as A. Then we would have
the following:
1. Seeing α as β, or SαAβ, and
2. Seeing α as γ, or SαAγ.
The cases of SαAβ and SαAγ are cases of seeing-as, or seeing aspects. It
seems that there is one more possibility, that is to say, the possibility of see-
ing α as α. In fact it would be the case that a ● is not seen as a ●, rather one
would claim that a ● in fact is, say, small black round. Therefore, one would
not claim:
3. Seeing ● as a small black round, or SαAδ, rather
4. This ● is small black round, α = δ, which comes down to the following,
5.  This ● is this ●, or α = α in which “a small black round” is claimed to be
the complete and correct description of what is seen.
Now, to be completely clear, here I do not claim that α ≠ α because surely
α = α. What I claim is that to say what α really is, is not easy to do, because
if one claims that ● is a small, black, and coloured round, the questions is—
compared to what? Is ● a small black round compared to a big white back-
ground, and if it is, then—does one really see a big white background? If yes,
then it must be the case that one sees a small black round “on” the big white
background. However, one could hardly see that a small black round is on
the big white background, since being on something is a relation, and in this
particular case supposedly a physical relation, like for instance a book being
on the desk (or is it like a layer of black colour being on the white paper?).
Leaving this issue aside, one could also say that (1) and (2) could be claimed
without claiming (4) or even (5), since (4) and (5) claim that:
6.  ● is this ● is the same as to claim: This ● is a small black round, which
is not the fact.
Nevertheless, what one sees can be described as being small, coloured, and
round under particular circumstances which are completely implicit or pre-
supposed in our imagining, thinking, seeing, and talking. The proposition (6)
seems to be wrong because there is no special reason why one should give
more weight to some characteristics than to others, i.e. to being small, black,
and round, than to being a dot, or to being a hole. There is no reason to sup-
pose that on the one hand this ● is a small black round, while on the other
hand it only looks as if it is a dot, or as a hole. It is not the issue of something


8
Chapter One
that looks like or is seen as something else, but that something seems to be
as seeing and as something else at the same time. In order to show that, since
there are no special reasons to consider (3) in any way relevantly different, in
terms of being more clearer than (1) and (2), one must show that (3) is rela-
tive too as (1) and (2). The point is that (3) and (4) are inconsistent, and that
there is no reason whatsoever to consider (3) as more basic than (1) and (2), at
least perceptually speaking. There is a slight difference between the utterance
I see something small, round, and coloured (black) and the utterance I see
small black round. The first utterance seems to be closer to the description of
what one sees, that is to say, particular characteristics observed solely (as an
unorganised series of features), while the second utterance seems to be closer
to a description of the whole phenomenon, almost as a definition or a name of
it. However, let us consider these characteristics one by one. There are three
characteristics mentioned in proposition (3), all of which can be questioned
based on simple cases of relativity.
Now, let us take an example of colour, i.e. black (as shown in Figure 1.2 as
a small grey round), still, the case can be applied to any other colour (gram-
matically, and aesthetically perhaps, black is by no means a colour, while
physically it is not).In Figure 1.2 the round of the same shade of grey appears
lighter on the darker background and darker on the lighter background. If this
is so, then the colour of ●, i.e. a property of being grey, is in fact background
colour relative because on different backgrounds it could appear very dark
grey but not black.
The next issue to consider is the size since one claims that what one sees is
small. The issue of a size is also relative compared to the size of background
(as shown in Figure 1.3). Both rounds in the illustration are in fact of the same
size. Conversely, on the bigger background a round appears smaller than on
the smaller background on which it appears bigger. Therefore, the property
of size is in fact background size relative.
The third characteristic to be examined is the feature of roundness (as
shown in Figure 1.4). The round on the right side of the Figure 1.4 appears to
be more round (roundish) than the one on the left, and this is so because of
Figure 1.2. A dot regarding its color.



Philosophical Morphology Primer
9
the shape and size of the background. Therefore, the characteristic of being
round (as well as other shape characteristics) is background shape relative.
If this example and examples of background size, and background colour
also, are suitably convincing, then there is no reason whatsoever to give pri-
ority to (3) compared to (1) and (2). My point here is that if this is so, then it
is wrong to say:
7.  ● is a small black round, and which can be seen as a dot or as a hole
because still there is no special reason to give priority to such utterance com-
pared to say the following one: ● is a dot, which can be seen as a small black
Figure 1.3. A dot regarding its size.
Figure 1.4. A dot regarding its shape.


10
Chapter One
round or as a hole. On the other hand, it seems that there are some differences
between (1), (2), and (3). In order to see ● as a small round (say that this is the
sub-description 3.1, that is without colour characteristic) one needs only two
dimensions since round is a part of a plane (at least geometrically speaking),
while to see it as (2), or (3) one needs additional dimension, i.e. depth since
a dot is on a paper (a layer of colour being on the paper), and a hole is in the
paper (essentially being a certain lack of paper). Perhaps the difference can be
made between dots and spots as visual phenomena and points as geometrical
phenomena visually represented by dots or spots. Dots and spots must have
colour, i.e. they cannot be colourless, while points cannot have colour, al-
though their representations must have it. Nevertheless, this difference does
not seem to be sufficient for the claim that (3) is not aspect-seeing while (1)
and (2) are. All of them seem to be cases of aspect seeing, perhaps of differ-
ent levels of aspects due to the mentioned distinctions. Therefore, what one
sees depends on so to say the background or more loosely, on the context,
and what is claimed are three quite different things, i.e. that ● is a small black
round, a dot, and a hole. What we have is the following:
(a) ●
(b) SαAβ,
(c) SαAγ, and
(d) SαAδ.
What could be of some importance is the question—is this (●) more similar
to a small black round, to a dot, or to a hole? If it is impossible to give a
straight answer, then it seems that at least some simple visual phenomena
are background-relative (colour, size, and shape background relative). On the
other hand, if it is possible to say that this (●) is more similar (further abbrevi-
ated as ↑σ) to a dot than to a hole (less similar further on abbreviated as ↑σ),
then one should indicate regarding which characteristic is this more similar
to a dot than to a hole. In the case of a dot and a hole, the issue seems to be
a difficult one to decide. Namely, there seems to be no additional reason by
which one could decide between:
8. This (●) is more similar to a dot than to a hole, or α ↑σ β • ↓σ γ, and
9. This (●) is more similar to a hole than to a dot, or α ↑σ γ • ↓σ β.
Perhaps some indication toward the decision could be found in the simple fact
that many people see this (●) as a dot before they see it as a hole, or simply
that it is easier to see it as a dot than to see it as a hole (but in fact it is easier
because we see dots more often than holes). However, the following case is
more complicated.



Philosophical Morphology Primer
11
10.  This (●) is more similar to a small black round than to a dot, or to a hole,
or α ↑σ, δ • ↓σ γ, β.
This raises the problem of the difference between (3) and (3.1) since a dot
seems to be a small round. Now, as such it can still be coloured or colourless.
The other way around, small rounds can be dots or points. As such, they are
coloured, still colour is unimportant if a small round represents a geometrical
point, while if it represents a dot, then colour is essential, not representation
functional. In addition, something can be seen as a hole only in relation to the
colour of its background, which should be of the same colour (as it is sug-
gested in Figure 1.1). Perhaps it is possible to suggest grammatical remarks
such as:
11.  Dots (spots, specks) are small coloured rounds (circles, but not as an-
nular lines).
12. Points are places where two lines cross in the same geometrical plane.
13. Points are represented by dots.
Proposition (12) looks like a definition, proposition (13) looks like a gram-
matical remark, while proposition (11) looks like something in between. The
problem is that there are phenomena that appear to us as small coloured rounds
and at the same time not as dots, but as holes. However, in order for something
to be seen as a small coloured round it needs to be surrounded by a different
colour, or at least by the same colour but of a different shade compared to its
surroundings. Therefore, surroundings different from the round in respect of
colour are essential for seeing a small coloured round, but in order to see it as a
dot this contrast between an item and its surroundings needs to be relevant. Ad-
ditionally, in order to see it as a hole (puncture, or lack of colour, in fact a lack of
material on which it should be if it would be a dot), the colour of the item should
be relevantly similar to the colour of the background of the item, and relevantly
dissimilar to the colour of its surroundings. Consequently, it seems that quite
different background and contrast conditions are needed for this (●) in order to
be seen as a small coloured round, or as a dot (as shown in Figure 1.5).
Namely, the basic condition for this (●)
•  to be seen as a small coloured round is just a little bit of contrast of it to
its surroundings, since then it appears to be on the same plane with its sur-
roundings (on the left side of Figure 1.5);
•  to be seen as a dot a somewhat edgy contrast is needed, since then it appears
to be on its background (in the middle of the Figure 1.5);
•  while to be seen as a hole, besides at least mild contrast with the surround-
ing material, additional background of the same colour as the (lacking) item


12
Chapter One
is needed, since then it appears as a lack of surroundings, i.e. as a round
hole (on the right side of Figure 1.5).
Now, it seems wrong to say that holes are small coloured rounds, yet it
seems right to say that small coloured rounds sometimes appear as dots,
and sometimes as holes depending on their relation to their background/
surroundings. If this is correct, then this (●) is in fact a small coloured
round which can be seen-as a dot or as a hole. Perhaps characteristics of
size, colour, and shape are, if taken individually, completely relative, but if
taken together, they seem to compose a description, or a definition of this
(●) which clearly describes its pattern. Further consequence is that small
coloured round is not an aspect of this (●), i.e. this (●) cannot be seen-as
small coloured round, rather, small coloured round is a definition or a
description of this (●), which, in other words, “is” a small coloured round
(as shown in Table 1.1).
According to the explication of differences given in Table 1.1, further
grammatical propositions would be something like the following:
Figure 1.5. Three interpretations of a ●.
Table 1.1. The description and aspect of a dot.



Philosophical Morphology Primer
13
14. Single small coloured rounds can be seen-as dots.
15. Single small coloured rounds can be seen-as holes.
Now, propositions (13) and (14) seem to be close to propositions like: This
drawing (where a combination of lines function as small coloured rounds
and curves as in our basic example) can be seen-as a duck, as a rabbit, or
as a one-eyed monster with both ears on the left side of its head, but also to
propositions like the following: A complex is composed of its parts, Four
metres is a length, Every rod has a length, Hands do not disappear when we
do not pay attention to them, and An order orders its own execution. (All of
these are Wittgenstein’s examples of grammatical or perhaps axis remarks
from OC, PR, and PG, for further examples see PI 142, 357, 429, 472, 583,
621, and for comment Kenny 2006:xvii) Therefore, this (●) can be described,
and some aspects can be mentioned, but all of the possibilities, in the man-
ner of survey, cannot be represented easily. The last point is obvious because
there are various, almost numerous kinds of contexts of this (●). Consider for
example a case that is two times more knotty than the previous one, that is
[
● ●. One could see it as representing two points in geometrical plane, or as
a part of a symbol of weight, or as eyes, or as a side of a die, etc. If one is not
satisfied with the present description of this (●), being perhaps the simplest
case of all as in a way and at least for some practical purposes, a complete and
quite clear description, then perhaps a complete and clear description is not
something that we should aim at in our inquiry in philosophical morphology.
However, one could be satisfied with this, and one should try to develop the
idea a little bit further. Here we should move from dots to more difficult items
like lines. The easiest thing to do is to combine dots themselves (as shown in
Figure 1.6, representing Indiana Jones from the movie Indiana Jones and the
Temple of Doom, Paramount Pictures, 1984). Figure 1.6 introduces new is-
sues that from the beginning make minute problems much bigger in the cases
of lines, geometrical forms, solids, and even quite complex curved lines. The
first one of these is the case of a line.
Figure 1.6. From a dot to The Temple of Doom (Indiana Jones, author’s illustration).


14
Chapter One
1.2.2. La Linea—The case of a line and some other strange creatures
Let us move from dots, spots, specks, and points to somewhat complex cases
such as lines. Geometrically speaking, a line is a series of geometrical points
(as shown in Figure 1.7).
Morphologically speaking, the most famous line is perhaps the one that
shapes the character in Osvaldo Cavandoli’s cartoon La Linea (as shown in
Figure 1.8).
Here, a few examples will be considered in order to broaden the discus-
sion on forms and their aspects in order to broaden the concept of mor-
phological relativity. Nevertheless, a line seems to be a somewhat simpler
example than a dot because lines are not so ambiguous. We have straight
and curved lines, and they can be combined in various ways. By using
parallel lines, one can for instance, achieve roundness and depth (as shown
in Figure 1.9).
In addition, by using straight lines for instance, one can achieve the circular
aspect (as shown in Figure 1.10 in which one can see a white circle, or a hole,
or a disc).
On the other hand the curved line (as showed in Figure 1.11) can be seen
in quite different ways, that is as a snake, hills, dunes, or as sea waves. Nev-
ertheless, unlike the case of a dot, in this case there is no direct, clear, and
unambiguous answer to the question which is the correct description of the
drawing, and which are aspects of it. For instance, the description a rhyth-
mically curved black line is not such description, or at least it is not clearer
than descriptions like sea waves, dunes, or hills. However, one should not
forget neither movement nor so to speak, the dynamism of a line like the one
manifested in the famous Edvard Munch painting Scream (especially in the
lithographical version).
Therefore, grammatical sentences regarding Figure 1.11 would only in-
clude aspects of it. To show that this is not an exception to the rule, we can
Figure 1.7. From a dot to a line.

15
Figure 1.8. Osvaldo Cavandoli—“La Linea” (The line, author’s drawing).

use a more difficult case (as shown in Figure 1.12 that for this occasion can
be called Moon C, and 7–4). The lines in the middle of Figure 1.12 can be
seen on the left side as the moon (up), or as number seven (down), or on the
right side as letter c (up), or number four (down). In the case of the upper
illustration, the left side can be called the moon aspect, and the right side
the c aspect, while in the case of the lower illustration, the left side can be
called the seven aspect, and the right side the four aspect. Now, there is no
particular reason in the drawing itself why one should consider the middle
illustration, the moon aspect, as primary, and then the letter c aspect as
secondary. As in the previous case, we can describe the illustration in 1.12
geometrically, but there is not much use in this. However, a slight change
in the drawing can give priority to one aspect over another. Let us take the
Figure 1.9. Roundness and depth by parallel lines.



Philosophical Morphology Primer

upper middle drawing in 1.12 as a further example with a slight change of
its background (as shown in Figure 1.13).
Regarding 1.13, on the left side it seems to be easier to see the moon aspect,
and on the right side the letter c aspect due to the difference in background.
The context can be even more specified as shown in Figure 1.14, where on the
left side there is also a symbol of a star added, while on the right side there is
a letter “a” added. There are many similar and more famous examples. One
can describe quite complex drawings, for instance Van Gogh’s drawing of a
chair, without any reference to or use of geometrical terms, and in fact their

Figure 1.14. Moon—C with inverted colours plus a star and a letter “a”.
use would be quite inappropriate in the description of a drawing (even in
terms of basic art-theoretical analysis of such a work of art, except if a work
of art flirts exactly with geometrical phenomena like in the case of Escher’s
woodcuts, lithographs, and mezzotints).
To return to the issue of a line, some such combinations of lines can in fact
be seen not as figures but as solids. For instance, if one combines straight
lines in a particular way, an interesting thing happens (like is shown in Figure
1.15). In Figure 1.15 drawings a and b differ substantially from drawings c, d,
and e. The difference is that a and b represent geometrical shapes, that is, a is
a hexagonal shape, while b consists of two rhombuses in two distinct planes
(or at least it can be seen as such in the context of the whole illustration). On
the other hand c represents a solid, to be exact a cube, while d could represent
an open box, and e could represent a die. The difference between examples
a and e for instance is demonstrated in the following illustration as the key
which makes the difference, so to speak the crucial characteristic (perhaps
pattern), i.e., the addition of vertical lines on the points where transversal
lines meet (as shown in Figure 1.16).
Regarding this point, it should be mentioned that a can be seen as c, d, or
e; and that all of them are so to speak aspects of it. Now, to move to curved
lines, the difference between an annular line/circle, a round/disc, and a
sphere/ball is somewhat different, as shown in Figure 1.17.
To represent a circle one just needs to draw an approximately annular
closed line. In order to represent a disc it is enough that the area of a circle is a
little bit different from its surroundings, and for it to be the same as its annular
line. Next, to represent a sphere at least one additional curve line is needed,
and finally, to represent a ball a light and shade are needed. Therefore, it is
a substantially different thing to represent a sphere and to represent a ball.


20
Chapter One
In order to represent a sphere one needs only to add one curved line, but to
represent a ball one needs to add shadow in accordance with the presupposed
light source (which is called the chiaroscuro or clair-obscur technique which
can be used for various purposes). All of these illustrations have numerous
aspects. Once again, it is quite difficult to claim a grammatical remark or
proposition (GP) regarding these aspects.
To show such difficulty one must use a somewhat more difficult but
still common and quite known example, for instance Caravaggio’s painting
Conversion on the way to Damascus, which is famous for application of the
chiaroscuro technique. One could say that:
(a) Caravaggio’s painting Conversion on the way to Damascus is beautiful.
Figure 1.15. From a line to a solid.



Philosophical Morphology Primer
21
Furthermore, one could continue in order to explicate the use of the word
“beautiful” in this particular context by saying something like the following:
(b) This painting is beautiful because it is dramatic.
One could continue with an even more particular explication:
(c)  This painting is dramatic because of the particular application of the
chiaroscuro technique.
Nevertheless, this is not the final (basic) reason, since there are applica-
tions and uses of the chiaroscuro technique, which result with completely
Figure 1.16. The pattern of a cube.


22
Chapter One
non-dramatic paintings. Therefore, one could perhaps conclude the chain of
explications with the following statement:
(d)  Particular application of the chiaroscuro technique consists of particu-
lar use of tension of light and shadow as an expression of the dramatic
situation in which Saint Paul was at the moment of his conversion, and
Caravaggio accomplished precisely that by careful placing the source of
light in order to produce precisely such a dramatic situation.
Explication (d) seems to be circular, but it is not the case. The point regard-
ing (d) is that the painting would be technically exceptional by all means
(in terms of application of the chiaroscuro technique), if for example, the
source of light was placed differently, but it would not be so dramatic,
which makes it artistically exceptional. Of course, one could add, in terms
of avoiding alleged circularity that the painting is painted in oil, and oil
colours are particularly suitable for expressing light reflections and shad-
ows because of the density and clarity of oil colours. In other words, in oil
painting technique colour serves light but not vice versa like for instance
in tone painting, etc. (and all of these can be demonstrated by mentioning
Figure 1.17. From an annular line to a ball.



Philosophical Morphology Primer
23
many technical details regarding oil colours, and by use of a bit of canvas
and oil colours in order to demonstrate these and many other effects of
uses of the technique compared with some other techniques, say the water
colours technique).
Now, which of these claims (a-d) should we consider as a grammatical re-
mark? If at all there is a grammar of artistic beauty (contrary to say naturally
beautiful, or technically beautiful), then proposition (a) is a grammatical one.
On the other hand, proposition (d) is not grammatical at all. It seems to be
experiential; the issue of technical and historical knowledge, as well as the
issue of so to say, looking at paintings (as it is claimed by R. Atkinson in his
1997 movie Bean—The Ultimate Disaster Movie, when the leading character
Mr. Bean says “Hello, my name is doctor Bean, apparently, and my job is
to sit and look at paintings.,” Universal Studios, 1997). One should not be
tempted to consider for example proposition (d) as a strict explanation, since
it includes much of personal experience, which is not explanatory regarding
(a). On the other hand, proposition (a) does not solve a simple question of
liking or disliking the particular painting, the painter, or his opus in the first
place, but also substantial aesthetic judgement which implies something more
(like for instance b, c, and d). All of these propositions are just descriptions of
different kinds or levels of knowledge and of experience too. However, one
should not be tempted to consider more detailed descriptions as explanations
and simpler ones as explications of what is seen or felt, or as pure expres-
sions of aesthetical experience. They are after all just descriptions suitable
for this or that particular occasion. Now, in my amateur opinion proposition
(a) is a grammatical remark (GP) and by surely a remark about some of Pi-
casso’s paintings for example, or other masterpieces of art, and if it is not so,
then perhaps the grammar of artistic beauty is overall impossible (noted by
Josip Lukin). However, the point is not that this is, or is not possible, rather
that grammatical sentences regarding visual phenomena sometimes refer
only to aspects, and that neutral or pure description of what is seen is often
completely inappropriate if possible at all. Precisely this point was exposed
regarding simple cases like the case of lines, and regarding more complicated
cases like the case of Caravaggio’s painting.
1.3. BASIC MORPHOLOGICAL ASSUMPTION
Philosophical morphology as it was already mentioned, welcomes, and ex-
plores phenomena of all kinds in the way that one:
(a)  observes phenomena (tries to “see” whole phenomena and all of their
features),


24
Chapter One
(b)  compares phenomena (in all possible combinations and aspects finding
connections and disconnections in terms of similarities, analogies, pat-
terns, and networks),
(c)  and describes clearly or presents perspicuously all phenomena (by means
of grammatical remarks).
All of these procedures (a-c) are very similar in all other morphologies like in
art theory, biology, psychology, cultural anthropology, history, logic, math-
ematics, etc. and precisely this net of similarities is what makes this kind of
investigation “morphological” too.
On the other hand, “philosophical” morphology does not use clear descrip-
tions as means to some other ends like scientific explanations or historical
or aesthetical interpretations for instance, but it regards clear descriptions as
ends in themselves because “clarity is an end in itself” (Wittgenstein CV 9)
and clarity is manifested by the use of “grammatical remarks,” which makes
this kind of investigation “grammatical” (conceptual and eo ipso philosophi-
cal). In addition, (philosophical) morphology can be regarded as a method
of inquiry and as explored structure of phenomena. Regarding this point and
procedures (a-c), it should be discriminated between:
•  two phenomena being similar for instance (morphology as a structure of
phenomena) (an ontological issue, which completely collapses into a gram-
matical one),
•  one’s own “looking-for” a similarity and identifying a similarity between
two phenomena (morphology as a method of “looking for” similarities,
analogies, patterns, and networks) (methodological issue, see Chapter 2,
section 2.5),
•  one’s own “seeing” particular similarity within an analogy, pattern or net-
work, and “seeing-it-as” a more or less important similarity (morphology
as a method of “seeing,” and “seeing-as”) (an issue in epistemology and
philosophy of psychology, which collapses into a methodological one, see
Krkač 2010),
•  and one’s own “clearly describing” a particular similarity and its place
within a network also (morphology as a method of making perspicuous
presentation) (logical or in fact grammatical issue since for Wittgenstein in
“Philosophical Investigations” TLP logic is a part of, or perhaps a chapter
in, philosophical grammar, PR 83, regarding these distinctions see Lukin
2011).
On the other hand if one wants to know which “tool” (procedure like “see-
ing similarity,” methodologically speaking) or “relation” (relation like “…



Philosophical Morphology Primer
25
being similar to …,” grammatically speaking) among morphological tools /
relations is the basic one, then one has to investigate some obvious solutions.
• The first obvious solution is that “being similar” is the basic relation / tool.
• The second solution is that “being networked” is the basic relation / tool.
•  Thus, since both of these relations / tools can be regarded as basic only in
special circumstances, none of them is basic overall. What one should look
for is a relation or a tool, which is basic independently of circumstances of
any kind (as shown in Table 1.2).
The set of examples in the two following two sections, accompanied with
definitions and illustrations of basic morphological concepts, will present the
idea that “being similar” is only formally or logically the simplest relation,
but not morphologically the simplest one since nothing can be done with
simple similarity, and that “being networked” is phenomenologically the
simplest relation, since what is given and observed in most cases are networks
of phenomena. Series of examples will illustrate that the relation of “being
more/less similar/dissimilar” is the basic relation. Regarding the very con-
cept of phenomena, it should be noted that they could not be identified with
things, events, processes, or their parts (they are part of previously mentioned
grammatical issue). A grammatical remark (GP), such as “Every process has
stages or sequences” is connected with the half-empirical and half-axis re-
marks (AP), for instance “In moderate climatic zones a year has four seasons,
that is, spring, summer, fall, and winter,” and with empirical remarks such as
“The spring is quite warm this year.” On the other hand, mentioned categories
could be parts of phenomena (but only conceptually).
•  A phenomenon is anything that can be “experienced” (particularly ob-
served, or seen regarding the majority of examples in this chapter) and, if
it is de facto observed, then it is observed from a particular perspective, by
a particular observer(s), and in a particular situation.
Table 1.2. Morphological relations.


26
Chapter One
Still, a phenomenon may be composed of parts (of different items) like for
example treetops moving in the wind, the sky, and the flight of an airplane.
It can be composed of parts, which are colours, movements, shapes, sounds,
smells, etc. In addition, a phenomenon can be presented in quite strange per-
spectives. We are used to standard perspectives; yet, almost completely com-
mon phenomena can appear to us completely unknown if they are presented
from an unknown perspective. Let us take an example. What is represented
in Figure 1.18?
Figure 1.18. Mexican military officer making eggs.



Philosophical Morphology Primer
27
Figure 1.18 represents a Mexican military officer making eggs (form “the
bird’s-eye view”). This point could be made regarding identification, but
regarding identity also. However, all of the following examples involve a
particular assumption, which can be considered as the basic morphological
assumption (of say, non-identity).
1.  Basic morphological assumption: any (at least) two given phenomena can-
not be completely similar (identical) or completely dissimilar (different)
regarding all of their features (in general or in particular, in large or in
small scale, and internally or externally), i.e. they must share at least one
characteristic in the case of maximum dissimilarity, or they must differ
regarding at least one characteristic in the case of maximum similarity.
Complete identity and complete difference are particular (only theo-
retically possible) cases of similarity and dissimilarity where a number of
phenomena share all characteristics or do not share any of them.
Although one should provide a few examples of this assumption, one must
be cautious while providing such illustrations, since it is obvious that there
cannot be “good” examples of this assumption. Regarding simple identity of
any object to itself (x = x), consider a single light grey object A on a white
background and on a background consisting of a combination of two darker
shades of grey (as shown in Figure 1.19).
The first instance of an A appears to be composed of only one shade of
grey which is obviously a lighter shade than both of those composing the
background of the second instance. However, the second instance of A ap-
pears to be composed of two shades of grey, i.e. lighter on the right side
and darker on the left side. Regardless of questions like, is this the same
object A or not, what seems to be noteworthy regarding our issue here is
that A is completely similar to itself in both instances and at the same time,
Figure 1.19. The shade of an object depending on its background.


28
Chapter One
this is not since in the first one A looks as if it is composed of one shade
of grey, while in the second it looks as if it is composed of two shades of
grey. Therefore, it seems to be wrong to claim necessity of self-identity at
least for phenomena, i.e. that everything is necessarily identical to itself, or
in symbolic fashion:
A
x □ (x = x). Self-identity could be claimed regarding
any particular aspect of a phenomenon, or
A
x □ (x = x) where x is an aspect
of a phenomenon.
Suppose for instance that A is a cloud moving on a background consist-
ing of other clouds more or less dark than the observed one. On the back-
ground consisting of darker grey clouds, it would appear lighter than on
the background consisting of somewhat lighter grey clouds (as shown in
Figure 1.20). However, clouds do not just have features of colour, but also
of shape, they move relatively on the background consisting of movements
of other clouds, etc. (regarding this point interesting notes by Wittgenstein
could be mentioned: “The classification of philosophers and psycholo-
gists: they classify clouds by their shape.” Z 462; or “You can’t construct
clouds…” CV 48)
However, the example in Figure 1.20 can serve only as a metaphor for
something else, that is to say that a network should be considered as the “spe-
cific background” on which any particular phenomenon, similarity, analogy,
and pattern appears in one instance while it could appear completely different
on the background of some different network.
Another point to consider is that the same phenomenon can appear in one
way at one place in a network while it could appear in another way at another
place in the same network. Further issues regarding this problem of identity
are connected with overall relativity of morphological structure and of mor-
phological observations. These issues will be addressed in the last section.
Consider now for example the two following phenomena A and B (as shown
in Figure 1.21). Are they completely similar?
Figure 1.20. A cloud on two different backgrounds.



Philosophical Morphology Primer
29
If one observes and compares their characteristics of colour, shape, and size,
for instance, one would find them to be completely similar. However, if one
compares them regarding their place, then B would be considered to be on the
right side and A to be on the left from the position of the observer or in some
other way defined regarding their position in the network (Wittgenstein intro-
duces the difference between internal and external similarities, see LW I 154–8).
Therefore, A and B are not completely similar since they are observer-position
relative or network-position relative. Consider for example the following phe-
nomena A, B, and C (as shown in Figure 1.22). Are they completely different?
Figure 1.21. Observer-position and network-position relativity.
Figure 1.22. Completely dissimilar objects or not


30
Chapter One
They obviously differ in shape and colour. Nevertheless A and B can be
regarded as simple (B) and complex (A) geometrical forms, but then (C) is
not a regular geometrical form at all. Therefore, they seem to be completely
different. But if one observes them in detail and with utmost precision
regarding their size one would find out that they are quite similar (almost
identical) regarding their size. Therefore A, B, and C are not completely
dissimilar; they only appear to be so. Detailed and complete observation,
which is included here, is not something special that mystically results with
seeing an aspect for example; rather it is ordinary looking-at with ordinary
caution. Nevertheless, complete similarity or identity and complete dissimi-
larity or complete difference, regardless of the fact that they are theoretical
possibilities, are in fact methodological tools; that is to say reductions of
maximum similarity and maximum dissimilarity. This is a case where a
small number of dissimilar characteristics in the case of maximum similar-
ity, or a small number of similar characteristics in the case of maximum dis-
similarity are regarded as somehow irrelevant (statistically for example) for
a particular practical purpose. This is one of the vital points of morphology,
since it does not get behind phenomena (because there is nothing behind
them, nothing is hidden, PI 126–9), but it does get to the whole of phenom-
ena and to all of their characteristics and relations without eliminating or
reducing any of them. However, the basic morphological assumption can be
put to the test if some morphological relations are explicated, particularly,
similarity and dissimilarity. Nevertheless, in order to do so, one needs to
understand their nature.
1.4. SEVERAL BASIC MORPHOLOGICAL RELATIONS
Here is an incomplete list of morphological relations accompanied with ex-
amples, illustrations, and definitions. Sometimes opposite relations are also
described since their description is necessary for easier understanding of op-
posites of more complex relations.
1.4.1. Similarity and dissimilarity (as shown in Figure 1.23)

(as shown in Figure 1.25)
