Definitions:
To be more similar to one phenomenon than to
another means to share a majority (more) of char-
acteristics with one phenomenon and a minority
(less) with the other.
Figure 1.25. Being more/less similar.



Philosophical Morphology Primer
33
Figure 1.26. Analogy.
To be less similar to one phenomenon than to another
means to share a minority of characteristics with
one than with the other.
Corollary:
The relations of being more dissimilar and being
less dissimilar are directly opposite to the defined
relations.
1.4.3. Analogy / disanalogy (as shown in Figure 1.26)
Simple analogy / simple disanalogy
α(A, C) σ (B, D) r f2
“Pairs A, C and B, D are analogous since pair A,
C is similar to pair B, D regarding being grey.”
α(A, C) σ (B, D) r f4
“Pairs A, C and B, D are analogous since pair
A, C is similar to pair B, D regarding being a
geometrical figure.”
~α(A, C) ~σ (B, D) r f1, 3
“Pairs A, B and C, D are simply disanalogous
since they are dissimilar regarding a majority
of features, that is size and shape, and since
they are similar regarding a minority of fea-
tures, that is colour.”
Definitions:
Two pairs of phenomena are simply analogous
if they are similar regarding a majority of fea-
tures (as shown in Figure 1.27).
Two pairs of phenomena are simply disanalogous
if they are dissimilar regarding a majority of fea-
tures, i.e. if they are similar regarding a minority
of features. Objects in this illustration are similar
only regarding the feature of being grey.
Complex or pure analogy / complex or pure disanalogy


34
Chapter One
(AσC) α (BσD) r (f5σf6)
“Pairs of similar phenomena A, C and B, D are
analogous in respect of similarity between their
characteristics, i.e. being light grey and being
dark grey.”
Definitions:
Two pairs of phenomena are complexly analogous
if similarities within each pair are also mutually
similar.
Two pairs of phenomena are complexly disanalo-
gous if similarities within each pair are not
mutually similar.
Corollary:
If two pairs of phenomena are complexly dis-
analogous, they must be simply analogous or
simply disanalogous.

Simple pattern (as shown in Figure 1.28)
π (AσB) • (AσB) … r f1

itself.”

A simple pattern is any recurrence of similar-

A simple pattern is in fact any recurrence of at
least one pair of phenomena. Nonetheless,

π (AσB) α (CσD)… r (f5σf6)

plex or pure analogy repeating themselves

A network (as shown in Figure 1.32)
ν (π1 (A-D) ω π2 (F-H)) r P1
“Two patterns A-D and E-H form network (or:

A network is any kind of pattern (at least two)
sharing at least one phenomenon in at least

Figure 1.32 and its characteristic of being

Figure 1.31. A complex or pure pattern consisting of disanalogies.
Figure 1.32. A network.



Philosophical Morphology Primer
37
A network (or web or net) is a series of mutually connected patterns. Patterns
can be parallel, crossed, circular, overlapped, etc. Consider the following
example (taken from BB 121, as shown in Figure 1.33) regarding the issue of
identification of an individual pattern or the issue of counting patterns.
Here we have two patterns, that is, two rows of dots; one with large inter-
vals succeeds a row of dots with small intervals. Which is the last dot in the
first sequence, or in other words, which is first in the second sequence? The
eleventh dot is the tricky one and it seems to be impossible to decide on this
issue. The possible solution is that these are two patterns, which overlap at
the eleventh dot. Networks are often rather complex like the following one
in which it is quite difficult to notice patterns, analogies, and similarities (as
shown in Figure 1.34).
Figure 1.33. Overlap of two patterns (BB 121).
Figure 1.34. A complicated network.


38
Chapter One
One can categorize all of the mentioned relations in various ways. The
most important way is to differ between connective and disconnective rela-
tions. Technical distinctions are the ones between least number of compared
phenomena needed for a relation to obtain, and least number of compared
features needed for a relation to obtain (as shown in Table 1.3).
1.5. Which morphological relation is the basic one?
Now, it seems obvious that networks consist of patterns, that patterns
consist of analogies, and that analogies consist of similarities. Therefore,
similarities seem to be the simplest relations. However, this is possible since
morphologically speaking there is vital relation, i.e. being more / less similar
(i.e. being more / less dissimilar). This relation is the core of observing and
seeing phenomena as similar / dissimilar, analogous / disanalogous, patterned
/ not-patterned and networked / not-networked. In order to show this one
should observe phenomena A, B, and C again (as shown in Figure 1.35).
First, it seems obvious that the relations “being more similar” and “being
less similar” imply similarity and dissimilarity, because “being similar” is a
special or reduced case of the relation of “being more similar.” On the other
hand, being more / less similar also constitutes being similar since the first
thing one observes is that some phenomena are more similar and that some
are less similar compared to some other phenomena.
(A ↑σ B • ↓σ C r f1–3) →
A σ B, A σ C • B ~σ C
“If A is more similar to B since they share colour
and size and less similar to C since they do
not share colour and size (however they share
shape, namely the characteristic of angularity),
then A is similar with B and with C, and B is
dissimilar with C.” In other words A and B are
more similar than B and C.
Table 1.3. A categorisation of morphological relations.



Philosophical Morphology Primer
39
Precisely this constitution of similarity by means of being more / less similar
permits the construction of pairs of similar phenomena which can be com-
pared as pairs rather than as individual phenomena. Specifically, between
phenomena A, B, and C: if A is more similar to B (and B to A) than to C (and
C to A), then A is similar to B and similar to C; if C is less similar to B (and
B to C) than to A (and A to C), then C is dissimilar with B. Therefore, A is
more similar to B (and B to A) than to C (and C to A) and B is less similar
to C (and C to B) than to A (and A to C), or to put it in symbolic fashion:
(A ↑σ B • ↓σ C r f1, f2) • (A↑σ C • ↓σ B r f3) → A ↑σ B • ↓σ C r f1–3
(A ↑σ B • ↓σ C r f1–3) → A σ B, A σ C
If one construes A, B, and C in pairs, then one can see that this relation also
composes analogy and disanalogy. The pairs are (A, B), (A, C), and (B, C)
and let us view the number of characteristics they share (among listed ones).
(A, B), (A, C), (B, C): (A, B) share two characteristics, (A, C) share one
characteristic, and (B, C) share no characteristics. Some conclusions can be
explicated from this like the following:
(A, B) ↑σ (A, C) ↓σ (B, C)
r f1–3
“Pair (A, B) is more similar to pair (A, C)
than to pair (B, C) regarding colour, size
and shape.”
((A, B) • (A, C)) ↑σ ((A, C)
• (B, C)) r f1–3
“Pairs (A, B) and (A, C) are more similar than
pairs (A, C) and (B, C) regarding colour,
size and shape.”
Figure 1.35. Being more/less similar (see figure 1.28).


40
Chapter One
((A, B) • (A, C)) ↑σ ((A, C)
• (B, C)) r f1–3 → α(A, B)
σ(A, C) r f2
“If pairs (A, B) and (A, C) are more similar
than pairs (A, C) and (B, C) regarding co-
lour, shape and size, then pairs (A, B) and
(A, C) are analogous regarding at least one
feature.”
((A, B) • (A, C)) ↑σ ((A, C)
• (B, C)) r f1–3 → ~α(A,
C)~σ(B, C) r f1–3
“If pairs (A, B) and (A, C) are more similar
than pairs (A, C) and (B, C) regarding co-
lour, shape and size, then pairs (A, C) and
(B, C) are disanalogous regarding at least
one feature.”
This last possibility explicates the relation of analogy since A, B, and C can
be arranged in pairs in order to form simple and complex analogies, and
through this, simple patterns and simple networks also. If this is so, then we
can formulate the following argument.
(d)  If a morphological relation constitutes (implies and/or construes) all other
morphological relations, then it is the basic morphological relation.
(e)  The relation of being more / less similar / dissimilar implies and/or con-
strues relations of similarity / dissimilarity, analogy / disanalogy and by
way of this, also the relations of being patterned / not-patterned and being
networked / not -networked.
(e1)  It does so because it consists of three phenomena and three observed
features, which are necessary and sufficient to imply similarity and to
construe analogy, pattern, and network. Similarity between A and B, or
between A and C is just a special case, for instance, of A being more
similar to B than to C. Therefore, “being similar” is a special case of “be-
ing more similar than” and “being dissimilar” is a special case of “being
more dissimilar than.”
(e2)  Analogy is construed by creating pairs (A, B), (A, C), and (B, C) and
by comparing them regarding their features. Therefore, “being analo-
gous” is also a special case of “being more similar than” and “being
disanalogous” is a special case of “being more dissimilar than.” There-
fore, relations of “being similar / dissimilar” and “being analogous /
disanalogous” are constituted (implied / construed) by the relation of
“being more similar than” / “being less similar than.”
(f)  Therefore, the morphological relation of being more / less similar /
dissimilar is the basic morphological relation.
Premise (d) is obvious. However, to be “a morphologically basic relation”
means to be the “basis for producing perspicuous presentation,” not just what
is first in an observation of phenomena, but what is a basic tool. Premise (e)



Philosophical Morphology Primer
41
is explicated by the set of these examples and interpretations previous to the
argument. Finally, conclusion (f) follows from the premises. The question
is—in which way is the relation of ↑σ, ↑σ the basic one? It is morphologically
basic in a way in which morphology is a method of philosophical inquiry. On
the other hand, it is not basic in a formal (logical) way or in a phenomeno-
logical way. We can show these three views as hierarchies where the highest
relation is basic regarding the view in question (as shown in Table 1.4).
2.  Regarding hierarchies in Table 1.4 it should be noted that according to
the morphological view “similarity” for example is a conceptual or ideal
point constructed on the basis of at least a few observed relations of “A
being more similar to B, than to C” and the same case is with analogy,
pattern, and network. What one does when one observes many phenom-
ena or many parts of a single phenomenon is that one notices that some
phenomena are more or less similar to others than… Another aspect is
Table 1.4. Three views on morphological relations.


42
Chapter One
that observation, e.g. A is more similar to B, than to C, permits one to
conceptualize not just similarity between say A and B, but permits one to
“see” that some pairs are mutually more analogous than other pairs, and
to conceptualize analogy between pairs of these phenomena, say between
A-C and A-B, and so on. Therefore, it seems that, morphologically speak-
ing, the observation of “more / less similarity / dissimilarity” is the basic
one. In addition, it should be emphasised that “conceptualization” is not a
real “in-time” process since one already has concepts that are an essential
part of an “observation” say of similarity (this Wittgensteinian point will
be clarified later regarding his understanding of interpretation of multi-
aspectual phenomena).
To be morphologically the simplest relation does not mean to be merely
implied in more knotty morphological relations like in a logical view, or to
be firstly observed like in a phenomenological view, or that all of the other
relations are “constituted” by such relation. Previous to these it means
to be used as a “basis” of a complete, clear, and perspicuous description
of any given pile (bundle) of phenomena. Surely, what we “see” first is
a network or one or two of its details (which does not mean particular
similarity, rather a particular part of network) and in this way networks
are phenomenologically basic. Another case is what one “thinks of” when
one sees a network and this is surely formally the simplest relation that
is particular similarity (which could turn out to be completely irrelevant
when one produces perspicuous presentation of a whole net for a particular
purpose). But morphologically basic relation is a way in which one “sees”
the network of phenomena as ↑σ, ↑σ and not just particular relations of this
kind but the whole net of them, and their pattern. Such a network of ↑σ
and ↓σ in the course of making a clear perspicuous description becomes a
net in which some of these ↑σ and ↑σ are identified as similarities, some
of them as analogies, and some of them as patterns. The relation of being
more / less similar / dissimilar is the core relation for perspicuous presen-
tation (clear description) of any amount of given phenomena (as shown in
Table 1.5).
Therefore, what we have here is the difference between “concepts” and
“percepts” (experiences of phenomena, as shown in Table 1.6). Namely,
similarity between A and B, dissimilarity between B and C, analogy and
disanalogy between pairs A-B, A-C, and B-C, the possible pattern A-B-
A-B, or network of two patterns A-B-A-B, and B-C-B-C, are all concepts
based on “observations” of these phenomena. However, observations
themselves on which the very concepts are based on are somewhat differ-
ent. Specifically, what one observes is that these phenomena are more or



Philosophical Morphology Primer
43
less similar, more or less analogous, more or less patterned, and more or
less networked.
If one observes phenomena A, B, and C, then what one needs is not just the
complete observation of all of their characteristics, but also of all their actual
and possible combinations and these are observable only in the broader back-
ground/context. This broader background in its most restricted form is “more
similar” which is a three-phenomenon relation since one needs A, B, and C
like in the previous example in order to observe that “A is more similar to B,
than to C.” From such an observation, one could explicate relation-concepts
like “A is similar to B, A is similar to C, B is dissimilar to C” (as shown in
Table 1.7).
Table 1.5. Relationships between basic morphological relations.
Table 1.6. Morphological percepts and concepts.


44
Chapter One
1.6. A CLEAR DESCRIPTION AS THE GOAL OF
PHILOSOPHICAL MORPHOLOGY
Now let us return to the first section of the chapter and to the unanswered
question—what is “a clear description” or “a perspicuous presentation”
(PI 122)? The important difference between these two goals is that:
•  a “clear description” if it is clear, then must be clear to anyone who knows
the language in which it is formulated or uttered (it smells like one of the
“Tractarian goals” of philosophy),
•  while “perspicuous presentation” if it is perspicuous, then is such to one to
whom it is presented for particular purposes.
•  However, one can object to this distinction if one tries to ground it in Witt-
genstein’s texts, and therefore it can be used only as general remark regard-
ing the philosophical goal. On the other hand, interpretation of clarity as a
continuous goal of Wittgenstein’s philosophy in his early and later periods
can include so to speak an aspect-shift, i.e. from the clear as “being pure”
to the clear as “being perspicuous.” Perhaps this is an important distinc-
tion that plays some role in twentieth century philosophy and that can be
traced back to Hegel, Kant, Leibniz, Spinoza, and even Descartes in terms
of historical evidence.
Say that the following simple phenomena are given (as shown in Figure 1.36).
One can perspicuously present these simple phenomena in quite different
ways for quite different purposes. Let us see four different ways of perspicu-
ous presentation of these six phenomena (as shown in Figure 1.37).
These relations can be written in symbolic fashion, which was suggested
in the previous sections.
Table 1.7. Simple and complicated morphological concepts and experiences.



Philosophical Morphology Primer
45
A σ E r f1
B σ C σ D σ F r f1
B σ C σ E σ F r f2
E σ C • E σ D r f3 (roundness)
B σ E σ F r f3 (angularity)
D ~σ C r f2, 3
B σ F r f1–3 (the most similar pair)
A ~σ D r f1–3 (the most dissimilar pair)
Therefore, all other pairs are less similar than the pair B, F and more similar than
the pair A, D. Let us see some different possibilities (as shown in Figure 1.38).
Now, one would be tempted to regard the first illustration as good per-
spicuous presentation and the second, third, and fourth as the bad ones. On
the other hand, one should resist such temptation since all illustrations are
morphologically correct since in the first, only the features of size, shape, and
colour are considered, while in the second, only the symbolizing features are
considered. These are just possible combinations and aspects of relations of
Figure 1.36. A set of objects.


46
Chapter One
these objects. In the first illustration objects D and C for example are similar
only regarding size, while in the second one they are almost completely simi-
lar since they represent eyes (on the left side). In the second illustration, it is
possible to combine faces and a house with a tree with the first illustration by
adding the similarities and dissimilarities regarding colour, size, and shape,
but this would not be perspicuous any more because it would mix two kinds
of features. The first presentation can serve for various practical purposes, for
example for art education in a primary school, as can the second one also, for
example in the course of the investigation of symbolism.
Figure 1.37. A possible perspicuous presentation.
Figure 1.38. Another three possibilities.



Philosophical Morphology Primer
47
3. Therefore, it is equally important that the description is:
(g)  complete (presenting all of the possibilities, not actually listing them,
since lists are not so perspicuous),
(h)  clear to the one to whom it is presented (not clear as such),
(i)  and formulated for a particular purpose.
If illustrations in Figure 1.41 present complete and clear presentation of
these phenomena and if these two presentations have a particular purpose,
then they nicely represent clear descriptions or perspicuous presentations of
these phenomena. These are simple presentations since they consist of simple
phenomena with just a few features considered to be all of their features.
On the other hand, most phenomena are complex, that is to say as any
particular phenomenon, for example some landscape, includes many kinds
of phenomena like shapes, colours, sounds, movements, traffic, social
interactions within a group, daily movements of particular people, etc.
If all of the particularly involved phenomena are explicitly mentioned,
then the (re)presentation would not be perspicuous at all. Completeness is
visible because all of the possibilities are visible, grammatical, and non-
grammatical. Although the colour case seems to be quite an easy one com-
pared to cases like landscapes, urban places, traffic, social interactions,
daily movements of people, etc., precisely because it shows this feature
of completeness. In such cases, it is hard even to observe all phenomena
and all of their features, even harder to compare them in order to establish
similarities, analogies, patterns, and networks, and it is hardest to make
a clear description of these phenomena. Completeness of description and
perspicuity (clarity) are difficult to achieve since they are property of what
Wittgenstein calls “grammatical remarks” regarding the observed phe-
nomena. However, in some cases this is possible, like in Wittgenstein’s
example with colour octahedron. It is easy to claim that for example, the
proposition “A process is composed of stages” is a grammatical remark re-
garding any procedural or proceeding phenomena, but can one claim gram-
matical propositions regarding traffic, or social interactions of people?
It is questionable whether clear descriptions of such complex networks
of phenomena are possible at all. In some sense such clear descriptions
consisting of grammatical (hinge) remarks (perhaps some empirical, and
some axis remarks too, OC 152, see Chapter 7) of any phenomena must be
possible, because such descriptions are necessary as the basis of any prac-
tice, technique, art, or science. Even if this is impossible in many cases, it
seems praiseworthy to consider such attempts as prime subject matter of
philosophical investigations.


48
Chapter One
1.6.1. MORPHOLOGY AND GRAMMAR OF CULTURE—
A CASE OF SPENDING SUNDAY AFTERNOON IN A PARK
Let us take an example of a rather difficult pattern regarding the last point
in the previous section. Humans have a variety of habits, customs, traditions,
and cultures (Price 1989). Besides the fact that these are difficult and expen-
sive to obtain, maintain, adjust, abandon, or completely change for others,
they are almost “necessary” for humans in order to survive culturally and
socially speaking. Each and every one of them constitute what Wittgenstein
called “natural history” (Naturgeschichte, PI 25, 415, p. 230) since their basic
pattern (say for instance that this
 is a symbolic presentation of the
structure of its basic pattern) repeats itself not only in all of their particular
instances of standard practice or routine, but in each lower, higher or more
complex level also (as shown in Table 1.8).
Therefore, survival seems to be somewhat difficult and expensive, and
more to that, cultures and civilisations (worldview, Weltanschauung) are at
their core nothing but striving to make it a little less difficult and expensive.
Nevertheless, culture and civilizations are a source of great puzzlement and
amazement on one hand due to their great variety, and on the other hand, due
to their sometimes expected and sometimes unexpected similarities and dis-
similarities, which on the whole create a complex net of cultures of humans.
If members of particular culture and society do not want to adjust to some
Table 1.8. Repeating of a pattern.



Philosophical Morphology Primer
49
different cultural patterns, do not want to change, and if, furthermore, they
want to preserve their habits, customs, and traditions, but they are at the same
time unable to do so because of circumstances beyond their reach, others
ought to help them if the previously mentioned other horn of the dilemma is
ethically unacceptable. Nevertheless, in order to help, one must know a par-
ticular culture, and in order to know it, one must be able to describe it, and the
prerequisite of a description is an observation. In fact, any observation of any
culture is always a clash of cultures, except in a situation in which an observer
herself/himself belongs to the observed culture and accepts it (then the issue
of “objectivity” is at stake). Besides the fact that it is almost impossible to ap-
proach any culture from the standpoint of an (ideal) observer, i.e., lesser than
in the fashion of mild ethnocentric (standpoint so convincingly defended by
R. Rorty) no matter how much this approach “scientific” could be, it is almost
crucial for understanding of a process of approaching a culture.
•  To understand a different culture means to look-for, look-at, see-as, and
know-how its patterns “work” (which are condensed in its worldview as a
whole of its “basic” beliefs manifested in grammatical remarks, on Witt-
genstein’s concept of know-how and certainty see Chapters 6 and 7). To
reveal these patterns, the whole net of them in a variety of human traditions,
customs, and habits, is the task of morphology and grammar of culture.
Morphologists often claim that the pattern is of utmost importance regarding
of understanding culture (Benedict 1934:49–56, she was strongly influenced
by Goethe and Spengler as well as by Wittgenstein). This seems to be cor-
rect. On the other hand, when one observes a culture, one does not observe
it as a whole, but only partially, for instance a single practice performed by
particular members at particular time, place, and situation.
•  If by looking at a particular practice one “sees” its place in the whole of
the culture as a net of many practices, then one sees it morphologically, be-
cause one sees the pattern. The only thing left to do then is to make (create
or invent) a clear description of the pattern (in a way that such a description
reveals all of the possibilities and enables one to see a particular actualised
possibility and its place within the whole).
Let me take an example of interactions between children and parents while
they are in a park. In Table 1.9 we have a schematic representation of interac-
tions in a park. In both situations, a and b, children live with both parents who
are married and have only one child, but only one parent is in a park with a
child. Specifically, a father is with his daughter and a mother is with her son


50
Chapter One
(as shown in Table 1.9 where standard kinship symbols and biological kin
type notations are used, see Kottak 1999:112).
What can be seen from the representation (which could be regarded as the
result of actual observation of morphological relations regarding the phenom-
enon in question) is that situations a and b represent two different patterns. In
situation a, the parent interacts with his/her child more intensively than with
the other parent, and the children interact with their parents more intensively
than with each other. In situation b, the parents interact more intensively with
each other than with their children, and the children interact with each other
more than with their parents. If all other “pattern-conditions” are satisfied,
one could regard situations a and b as two different patterns. Now, one needs
more information in order to classify these patterns as habits, customs, tradi-
tions, or even as a cultural difference. For the sake of the argument, let us say
that these situations represent cultural differences. If this is the case, then we
have two distinct cultural patterns.
Table 1.9. A case of spending Sunday afternoon in a park.



Philosophical Morphology Primer
51
(3.1)  Therefore, in culture a, members of a family tend to interact with each
other more intensively than with members of other families, while in
culture b they tend to interact more with members of other families than
with members of their own family.
Could (3.1) be regarded as a grammatical remark about interactions between
members of a family? Of course, but one could investigate these differences
more deeply and find out that, for instance, these are only habits of different
classes of the same culture, or even that they are different due to particular
differences between families of the same class within a particular culture.
(3.2a)  On the other hand, say that this difference is only an instance of a
much deeper difference between two cultures, culture b in which
members are more interactive, communicative, pro-socially oriented,
helpful, etc., beyond the basic social group they belong to (that is a
family), and culture a in which its members are less interactive, etc.,
beyond the social group that they belong to.
If, on the other hand, this is the case, then indeed there are two different
cultures here, and this is a good example of a cultural difference. However,
the situation could be the same in these cultures regarding the case of par-
ents spending time with their children in a park, and then this would not be
a good example of differences between particular cultures, but it could be a
good example of a similarity between them. This similarity can be irrelevant
if it is a consequence of different cultural patterns, yet it could be relevant if
both cultures share a variety of specific attitudes and practices regarding their
actions while their members are in a park with their children even if their cul-
tural patterns, worldviews, and standard procedures are, generally speaking,
relevantly different. Now, imagine that the illustrated situation includes dif-
ferent games as basic interactions. If so, then we could get some more precise
grammatical remarks such as the following.
(3.2b)  In some cultures, while visiting a park on Sunday afternoon children
play mostly with their parents (culture a), while in other cultures
children play with each other no matter if they are not kin, while the
parents talk about their kids (culture b).
Now, compare (3.2a) and (3.2b). (3.2a) seems to be like a grammatical
remark regarding the morphology of cultures a and b, while (3.2b) seems
to be more like an experiential remark. However, cultures a and b could be
completely the same regarding a particular practice, say spending Sunday


52
Chapter One
afternoon in a park with their children, while completely different regard-
ing basic social orientation. If so, then the case of comparison of situa-
tions a and b would be a nice illustration of such overall dissimilarity. On
the other hand, cultures a and b could be completely different regarding
mentioned practice, while completely similar regarding basic social orien-
tation. If so, then this would be a nice case of dissimilar practices while
maximum similarity still exists. However, if all other pattern-conditions
are satisfied, then these and similar possibilities become irrelevant. In
other words, then the case of situations a and b represents, and is a good
example of, basic and overall dissimilarity between two cultures, between
two different cultural patterns (practices and value systems, or world-
views). Propositions (3.2) and (3.2a, b) are not different in a way that
(3.2) is experiential, while (3.2a, b) are grammatical, since their borders
are vague. Namely, (3.2a, b) are not “explanations” of (3.2), instead, they
are no more than more precise “explications,” more detailed “descrip-
tions” of (3.2). Grammatical remarks are as relative as are the phenomena
from which they are explicated. This is so because all of a phenomenon’s
characteristics are to be taken into account. “Reduction” and “explanation”
are not allowed as kinds of “magical procedures” of “transubstantiation”
of some more particular descriptions into phenomena that ought to be ex-
plained, and some more general and ambiguous descriptions into law-like
principles via which phenomena ought to be explained too. In descriptions,
there are no “causes” that should be mentioned; only patterns that are a
type of “non-causal regularity.” In addition, “an adjustment” of (results of)
observations is forbidden, as well as an adjustment of descriptions.
1.7. MORPHOLOGY, ASPECTS AND
OVERALL RELATIVITY
Morphology as a structure as well as a method is sensitive or relative in many
ways, as was already explicitly claimed in the first section and implicitly in
the following sections. To begin with, an observation of a phenomenon can
be relative to the level of perspicuity one wants to achieve. The next point
is relativity of a phenomenon, similarity, analogy, or pattern regarding its
position in the network. Let us take an example. How many patterns do you
recognize in the following illustration (as shown in Figure 1.39)?
Did you recognize all of the following six patterns (as shown in Figure 1.40)?
Therefore, the global pattern would be the following (as shown in Figure
1.41).



Philosophical Morphology Primer
53
Figure 1.39. A complicated network.
Figure 1.40. Six patterns in a complicated network.


54
Chapter One
Now, imagine that this pattern represented in Figure 1.41 creates a com-
plicated network like the following one where we have networks within net-
works, and patterns within patterns (as shown in Figure 1.42).
If one regards the whole of the illustration in Figure 1.42 like one pat-
tern, it would not be easy to notice that the patterns on right side of the
illustration are rotated by 180 degrees. This shows at least two interesting
points. The first is that morphological relations or structures are relative.
Something can be a pattern in one context and an analogy in the other one.
Something can be a pattern in one context and a network in the other one
due to its function in the context of bigger unit. Another point is that some-
thing can be “seen” as a simple similarity from a direction from which one
sees only a detail of a network, and as a part of a pattern in different posi-
tion when one sees the whole network connected with other networks due
to aspects which one sees and due to position from which one is observing
phenomena, like in the following duck-rabbit drawing (see PI pp. 193–229
as shown in Figure 1.43).
More to that, a particular phenomenon can have a different identity re-
garding its place in the network it belongs to. Let us consider a dark grey
square from the beginning of the paper and the two different roles it could
play.
Specifically, A has a function of being a side of a cube in A1, and a func-
tion of representing the front of a house in A2. These two functions are (at
least geometrically) incompatible, but the point is that the same phenomenon
has many possible identities due to the number of possible roles it could play
in a pattern or in a network.
Figure 1.41. A global pattern of a complicated network.



Philosophical Morphology Primer
55
4.  Therefore, morphology as a method is relative to the aspect seeing. Some-
thing can appear as a similarity from one aspect and as dissimilarity from
another aspect, or as an analogy in one aspect and as a pattern in another
aspect. Morphology as a structure of phenomena is structure and border
sensitive. This means that some relations between phenomena in a net-
work are more important regarding their role in the structure than the other
relations, and that due to for example spatial and temporal continuity of
phenomena, their borders are frequently not clear due to their relations of,
for example, overlap, crossing or similar.
Figure 1.42. Very complicated network of networks.


56
Chapter One
1.8. CONCLUDING REMARKS
Here some major claims from the previous sections will be summarized
(perhaps not as perspicuously as one expects them to be). On the other hand,
something must be wrong with the very idea of philosophical morphology
and philosophical grammar also, if one cannot represent them perspicuously
as well as any other phenomenon (never mind the fact that what is presented
here is the procedure itself).
5.  There is no complete identity or complete difference in the phenomenal
world. There is no phenomenon which would be the same regarding its
different positions in a network, regarding the way in which it is observed,
and regarding its relevance. There are no two completely similar or com-
pletely different phenomena. Nothing is hidden. All phenomena in daily
experiences are open to view as complex networks consisting of many
patterns, analogies, and similarities between them. There are no hidden
characteristics of phenomena. If a particular phenomenon is given in an
experience, then all of its characteristics are observable and comparable
since they are visible and apparent.
6.  Every technique, skill, or standard procedure begins with morphology.
Morphology is at the beginning of all sciences. Morphology is a method
Figure 1.43. The duck-rabbit head (author’s drawing).



Philosophical Morphology Primer
57
of philosophical inquiry. Morphology deals with phenomena in respect
of their “organic” unity, proceeding, and change (regarding natural phe-
nomena, which are continuous, while non-natural are mostly discrete,
especially their “procedural” nature). Humans are morphologists by nature
and by nurture (culture) too. However, the morphological point of view re-
quires a kind of “conversion” to the new worldview. The “converted” one
“sees” the world very easily from many “perspectives” and what seemed
to be the only possible perspective “before,” seems to be just one among
many perspectives “now.” The converted one “sees” not just isolated anal-
ogies and disanalogies, similarities and dissimilarities and patterns, but a
whole net of them and is capable of combining and perspicuously present-
ing many perspectives in different ways. Of course, this “conversion” is
and is not like a religious conversion; it is since it looks like a conversion
from one religion to another, without the possibility of atheism, and it is
not like a religious conversion since there is no salvation element (nor
atheistic salvation either, i.e. by political system, science, industry, or art).
7.  Morphological investigation does not include “explanations” or “conclu-
sions” rather one can come more or less close to the “grammar” (clear
description) of a phenomenon, which is, as well as clarity, surveyability,
and perspicuity the philosophical goal in itself. (Explication is often
contrasted to explanation, which by itself constitutes an issue for sci-
ence. However, this issue should not interest us here. On the other hand,
similarity or even complementarity between understanding (which is a
result of explication) and feeling (so to speak morphological sensitivity)
is of utmost importance since one is inseparable from the other in case of
morphology and philosophical grammar. If they are separated, then the re-
sult is “the pedant who understands without feeling and the mondain who
enjoys without understanding” to use Bourdieu’s expression so precisely
echoing B. Pascal’s distinction between esprit de finesse and esprit de
géométrie, see Bourdieu 1984:11). Observation of any pair of phenomena
as wholes, and particularly in all their details, and comparison of all of
their characteristics can be concluded with a clear description consisting
of “grammatical remarks” which must be perspicuous to the one to whom
they are presented for a particular purpose. Proper overview as a clear
description of a network presupposes that all of the characteristics are de
facto observed and mutually compared and that none of them are reduced,
eliminated or left out (methodically, systematically, or by mistake, over-
sight, negligence, or the similar).
8.  Morphological education includes training, exercises, and years of drill
and experience in some field. Morphology is about identifying existing
similarities and dissimilarities, analogies and disanalogies in our language,


58
Chapter One
Figure 1.44. Two functions of the same object.



Philosophical Morphology Primer
59
world and practices, and about explicating these identified similarities and
analogies and producing clear descriptions of phenomena (especially hu-
man practices) in order to get “philosophical grammar” which “perspicu-
ously presents the phenomena” (FOL) for various practical purposes.
•  “Is an indistinct photography a picture of a person at all? Is it even always
an advantage to replace an indistinct picture by a sharp one? Isn’t the indis-
tinct one often exactly what we need?” (PI 71)


60
Chapter Two
Wittgenstein the Morphologist I:
Perspicuous presentation
(with Josip Lukin)
But the prototype must just be presented for what it is; as characterizing
the whole approach and determining its form. (CV 21)
2.1. INTRODUCTION
In two parts of the present chapter we will investigate Wittgenstein’s
philosophical morphology. In other words, we will try to identify some
elements of philosophical morphology in his later philosophy. In order
to do so, in the first part we will affirmatively answer the question—is
perspicuous presentation in fact morphology? In the second part we will
affirmatively answer the questions—what is morphology as a method and
why it is better than other (philosophical) methods? The general argument
is the following:
•  If the world is not given ideally but as it is given in our daily experiences,
then Wittgenstein’s philosophical morphology is the best method of repre-
senting the world (the second section).
•  The world is not given ideally in our daily experience; it is not flat as is
suggested by sciences. What is more, it is corrugated in a way (the second
section).
•  Therefore, Wittgenstein’s philosophical morphology, as a method of mak-
ing an overview of “use of our words” of a certain FOL and as a structure
of an FOL (phenomena) by investigating analogies and disanalogies is the
best method of representing the world (the first section).



Wittgenstein the Morphologist I
61
2.2. ANALYSIS OF PI 122, AND GB 133
In the passage relevant for our present purpose, i.e. in PI 122 (and in GB 133)
Wittgenstein writes:
1.  “A main source of our failure to understand is that we do not command
a clear view [übersehen, Glock’s translation is “survey” 1996:279,
also PI 125] of the use of our words.—Our grammar is lacking in this
sort of perspicuity. A perspicuous representation [Die übersichtliche
Darstellung, J. Floyd following S. Cavell suggests that “presentation”
is better translation] produces just that understanding which consists in
‘seeing connections’ [Zusammenhänge sehen]. Hence, the importance
of finding and inventing intermediate cases. [connecting links GB 133]
The concept of a perspicuous representation is of fundamental signifi-
cance for us. It earmarks the form of account we give, the way we look
at things. (Is this a Weltanschauung?)” (PI 122, trans. By Anscombe)
“… (A kind of “World-view” as it is apparently typical of our time.
Spengler.)” (GB 133)
It should be noticed that the adjective “perspicuous” is not used attributively,
i.e. it is not a feature of representation, but is rather a characterisation of its
function. (Baker 2004:42)
•  Therefore, a representation of X makes X perspicuous for someone to
whom it is represented.
It can be claimed that, regarding “us” and “them,” when Wittgenstein says
“us” he probably means “a certain cultural tradition” or a Weltanschauung
he belongs to (Baker, Hacker 2005:320). However, when he criticises that
indexical “we” and “in our time” lack perspicuity, he in fact criticises West-
ern civilisation (European and American civilisations, CV 8). Therefore, by
“our” and “we” he is probably referring to our civilization. Philosophy is a
consequence of non-surveyability of our grammar. Therefore, before asking
about “we” at the end of PI 122 we must ask about “we” at the beginning.
What does it mean that “our grammar is lacking in ... perspicuity”: “we” do
not have it (and “they” have it), or “we” need it (and “they” do not)?
PI 122 is unclear; “we” are different because we lack what “they” have, or
we lack what “they” neither have nor need.
Setting this issue aside, there are some remarks that are accepted as useful
commentary on (1). Here are some of them.


62
Chapter Two
(1.1)  What is of our interest is not scientific data or experiment (espe-
cially developmental hypothesis), or the essence of things. Rather
phenomena, like words and their grammar. “Don’t look for anything
behind the phenomena; they themselves are the theory.” (Goethe
1998: § 575, Spengler 1932:156, RPP I 889)
(1.2)  “Crystalline purity of logic” is replaced with “perspicuous (re)
presentation” as the new method that should be applied to language-
games, grammar, and even to actions and FOLs (RPP and OC re-
spectively). Perspicuous representation need not be a representation
of our grammar; it can also be a representation of religious ceremo-
nies, magical rites (Baker 2004:42–4), and the whole of culture as
we will suggest later (this in combination with Goethe’s quotation
above in 1.1 gives rise to the new method regarding investigating
cultures in the sense of ethnology or cultural anthropology).
(1.3)  The result of the application of this new method is that we can “see
connections,” i.e. “analogies and disanalogies” (PI 66). One clear
example of the perspicuous representation is that of colours (PR
51–2, ROC), but there are also other attempts (perhaps of proof in
RFM, of certainty and belief in OC, and of aspect-seeing as the core
of perspicuous representation in PI and RPP, see Chapter 6).
(1.4)  The notion of surveyability is prominent in all of Wittgenstein’s
later philosophy (PR, GB, BT 89, concerning the very nature of
philosophy as its main goal, RFM, etc. regarding the translation and
the basic analysis see Baker, Hacker 2005:307–34, and regarding
the “archaeology” of texts see Baker 2004, these points are shown
in Table 2.1).
It seems to be important, at least in some cases, to acknowledge that per-
spicuous representation includes a kind of conversion to the new world-view,
or world-picture. As R. Monk explicates it, “The understanding that consists
in seeing connections, one might say, is the understanding that results from
a change of aspect.” (Monk 1991:508) In short, perspicuous representation
(Übersicht, survey, overview) is in fact a kind of new:
Table 2.1. From phenomena (mostly linguistic ones) to perspicuous presentations.



Wittgenstein the Morphologist I
63
2.  clear description of phenomena (“clear” here means “perspicuous,” not
“pure”).
In this new method the logic of TLP is somehow “substituted” with grammar
of PI, but clarity/perspicuity/surveyability is still requested (“For me on the
contrary, clarity, perspicuity is an end in itself.” MS 109 200 5. 11. 1930,
CV 9). Wittgenstein introduced the term in the context of methodological
reflections on anthropology (GB 130–3), namely by contrasting the morpho-
logical method with “a scientific” method applied by J. G. Frazer. Therefore,
it seems that with introducing the notion of perspicuous representation Witt-
genstein tried to replace:
(2.1)  In other words, perspicuous representation seems to be a conceptual
investigation which consists of finding similarities and analogies
between many, and at first glance completely different and discon-
nected, cases (of usage of words), which is in fact morphology as a
method. “Philosophy simply puts everything before us, and neither
explains, nor deduces anything. Since everything lies open to view
there is nothing to explain.” (PI 126)
Baker and Hacker give a clear-cut description of the overview:
3.  “When one has an overview of a conceptual field, one knows one’s way
around. Knowing ones’ way around a conceptual field consists in ability
to specify connections, exclusions, analogies and disanalogies (PI 130)
that make it possible to dissolve and resolve philosophical problems.”
(Baker, Hacker 2005:309) The method essentially consists of “observing”
the phenomena and finding analogies and disanalogies, and the result of
application of this method is “seeing connections.” “Consider for example
the proceedings that we call ‘games’. […] What is common to them
Table 2.2. Descriptions vs. explanations.


64
Chapter Two
all?—Don’t say: “There must be something common, or they would not be
called ‘games’.” […] For if you look at them you will not see something
that is common to all, but similarities, relationships, and a whole series of
them at that. To repeat: don’t think, but look! […] And we can go through
the many, many other groups of games in the same way; can see how simi-
larities crop up and disappear.” (PI 66) (This paragraph is in our opinion
of utmost importance and will be the subject of another investigation.)
The idea of a morphology as a method fits in properly between language-
games and forms of life only when it is recognised that Wittgenstein bor-
rowed these ideas from Goethe, Spengler, Boltzmann, and Hertz. There are
many places in which this influence is obvious, but it seems to us that the
following quotations show it suitably regarding morphology (at least in the
case of the influence of Goethe and Spengler).
(3.1)  “What is it that a conceptual investigation does? Does it belong in
the natural history of human concepts?—Well, natural history, we
say, describes plants and beasts. But might it not be that plants had
been described in full detail, and then for the first time someone real-
ized the analogies in their structure, analogies which had never been
seen before? And so, that he establishes a new order among these
descriptions. He says, e.g., ‘compare this part, not with this one, but
rather with that’ (Goethe wanted to do something of the sort) and in
so doing he is not necessarily speaking of derivation; nonetheless
the new arrangement might also give a new direction to scientific
investigation. He is saying ‘Look at it like this’—and that may have
advantages and consequences of various kinds.” (RPP I: 950)
Consequently, morphology is not something just opposite to the scientific
method or methods of scientific investigation; rather it is something that is in
a way (conceptually) prior to scientific investigation, since it can “give direc-
tion to it” by suggesting different perspectives to investigated phenomena.
(3.2)  “Spengler could be better understood if he said: I am comparing dif-
ferent periods of culture with the lives of families; within the family
there is a family resemblance, while you will also find a resem-
blance between members of different families; family resemblance
differs from the other sort of resemblance in such & such ways etc.
What I mean is: We have to be told the object of comparison, the
object from which this approach is derived, so that prejudices do
not constantly slip into the discussion. Because then we shall willy



Wittgenstein the Morphologist I
65
nilly ascribe what is true of the prototype of the approach to the
object to which we are applying the approach as well; & we claim
‘it must always be...’ This comes about because we want to give the
prototype’s characteristics a foothold in the approach. But since we
confuse prototype & object we find ourselves dogmatically confer-
ring on the object properties which only the prototype necessarily
possesses. On the other hand we think the approach will lack the
generality we want to give it if it really holds only of the one case.
But the prototype must just be presented for what it is; as character-
izing the whole approach and determining its form.” (MS 111 119:
19.8.1931, CV 21–2, for some of Spengler’s influences on Wittgen-
stein regarding culture, and for some early (Von Wright) and later
(Cavell) interpretations see DeAngelis 2007)
Moreover, Wittgenstein once said in a lecture (lectures 1946/7):
(3.3)  “What I give is the morphology of the use of an expression.” (Mal-
colm 1984:43) Goethe was interested in “recognizing living forms as
such to see in context their visible parts, to perceive them as “mani-
festations” of something within […] not by looking for mathemati-
cal regularities and causal laws, but by seeing the connections…”
(Monk 2005:66–67) His idea of morphology was applied to cultures
by Spengler who wrote, “The means whereby to identify dead forms
is Mathematical Law. The means to identify living forms is Anal-
ogy.” (Monk 2005:66–7) According to J. Schulte (1990, 2002) and
related to Wittgenstein’s known quotation of Goethe “And so the
chorus points to a secret law” (GB: 133) it must be stressed that,
(3.4)  “What appeared to be a chorus of facts turns out to embody a law.”
(Schulte 2002:63) Here, law is not a particular natural law like in
natural sciences; rather it is conceived as some kind of abstract law-
like regularity. (Schulte 2002)
The fact that Wittgenstein was strongly influenced by Goethe and Spengler is
not something we are going to investigate here. We will presuppose it on the
basis of known investigations (by Klagge, Monk, Nordmann, Rowe, Schulte,
and others). What is of our particular interest is that he was influenced by the
method which he applied not to plants and animals like Goethe, or to cultures
like Spengler, but to language, grammar, and maybe even to practices. None-
theless, in order to understand the proceeding of the method it is necessary to
explicate some of Wittgenstein’s interesting examples, such as PI: 66 or BB:
87, in order to “see” the morphological method at work. Besides that, one


66
Chapter Two
must also investigate how the method is applied, and of course its advantages
and disadvantages in comparison to other methods. In such an investigation
we must bear in mind that morphology is applied to a philosophical field,
not to a scientific one, and that the result is not a theory, or a prediction, but
grammar, perspicuous presentation, and overview. Nevertheless, this is a task
for a different paper.
2.3. WITTGENSTEIN’S MORPHOLOGY
While in the first part of the paper we explicated some of Wittgenstein’s pas-
sages (mainly from PI and GB) regarding the idea of morphology and men-
tioned some of the relevant and standard commentaries, in this part we will try
to expand Wittgenstein’s morphology following his examples from the obvi-
ous ones (like language-games), to the not so obvious ones (like colour, rites,
certainty). To continue with the previous explications, it seems to be interest-
ing to look at the proclamation that there is nothing “behind the phenomenon”
(RPP I 889, here 1.1.), and that the “chorus points to a secret law” (GB 133).
From the combination of these two quotations (from Goethe and also used by
Wittgenstein), it can be concluded that the law isn’t “hidden,” but still it is
“secret” since there is no “depth” and that the secret law can only be situated in
“organisation” of the phenomenon. (Breithaupt 2002:73–89). In other words,
the issue is about “organisation” or “structure” (perhaps even about grammar)
of phenomena, not about something “hidden” within them or behind them. We
will illustrate this distinction with a story which should not be considered an
evolutionary “explanation,” but as a language-game for a particular purpose.
•  Humans want to survive and in order to succeed in that they need pictures
(world-view), “rough ground” so to speak. They survive when they “see” the
world-view (to “see” here is like “but don’t you see?” in PI 231), and use
language to describe the world, but that picture is wrinkled and rumpled. As
such, the picture stops them in their ontological attempts, so they try over
and over again to flatten the picture, to make it smooth and what they see
then is that it is a drawing, a sketch (certain geometry, law-like regularity).
They then have a model, but there is no world-view any more. In such a flat
drawing they no longer recognise things, facts, and events. What they see is
only a pile of lines on the map which doesn’t make sense to “them” any more.
In this sense TLP is like walking on ice, but what humans need is rough
ground (PI 107), because then they can see the picture, their world-view, and
they need that since pictures are, although wrinkled, also practical, useful,



Wittgenstein the Morphologist I
67
and above all, used in certain ways in their “wrinkled world.” However, if
a change of ecological configuration occurs in the life of a certain tribe (or
culture or society), morphologists are precisely the people who are capable
of “saving the tribe” from an inefficient theory. Only morphologists see
the theory just as it is: only a theory. They do so by means of overview or
perspicuous representation and they achieve that without a theory, without a
hypothesis, because they know when the new theory is needed. They know
that causality is quite appropriate for survival most of the time, but also that
on occasions such as this, i.e. the severe change of ecological configuration,
there are no causes and effects that can be known and used. Nonetheless,
something must stay constant and that which is constant is form (pattern).
4.  Morphology is contrary to the theory, because form is contrary to the
cause. Form is abstention (refraining) from the very idea of a cause, or of
a theory (hypothesis). Forms are models in which certain variables can be
fixed for practical purposes. (Praktische Zwecke, RFM: I, 139, p. 42.) We
talk about good descriptions which are often used as “explanations,” but a
good description which is not used as an explanation is in fact morphology.
Whatever it is, morphology cannot be something simple or something easily
explainable, for then it could be called by other names. Therefore, the ques-
tion is, is morphology something complicated or is it something difficult to
explain. Is it something like a poem in PI 531? But on the other hand, mor-
phology is similar to a theory because both “form” and “cause” are related to
it. Maybe morphology is also a certain “perspective” on things, since in the
previously mentioned unfortunate state of a tribe their theories became ruins,
that is to say useless for practical purposes, and by means of overview a mor-
phologist can say that x is a theory and y isn’t, or that x is more useful than z.
5.  Form (as a pattern) is a certain ruffle, corrugation, (folded, creased), or
a perspective (aspect). That which is corrugated cannot be stated ideally.
Moreover, what is ideal doesn’t need perspective or maybe it has a kind
of “view from nowhere.” Nonetheless, morphology is appropriate for
everyday life, for practical purposes. Our ability of face-recognition, for
example, is amazing. No wonder that we want to expand it to other fields
of forms because face recognition, face-reading, and physiognomy is mor-
phology par excellence. (In spite of W. Shakespeare’s words: “There’s no
art to find the mind’s construction in the face.,” Macbeth, 1.4.)
How do we know that “the world” is ruffled, wrinkled, corrugated? The only
way for a world to be, if we are going to set any kind of rules, is in a ruffled


68
Chapter Two
way. Therefore, the world cannot be flattened, and we “know” that the world
is ruffled, but this is a kind of know-how since there is no rule outside of a
case, or out of a particular practice. That our world is ruffled Wittgenstein
suggests in PI: 125:
6.  “The fundamental fact here is that we lay down rules, a technique, for a
game, and that then when we follow the rules, things do not turn out as
we had assumed. That we are therefore as it were entangled in our own
rules. This entanglement in our own rules is what we want to understand
(i.e. get a clear view of).”
There are at least four types of morphology (including those which influenced
Wittgenstein):
 7.  regarding living forms, i.e. plants and animals (Goethe, see Rowe 1991,
Schulte 2002),
8.  regarding scientific investigation (Hertz, Boltzmann, see Janik 1994/95),
9.  regarding myth, history and culture (Ernst, Spengler, see von Wright
1982),
10.  regarding:
(10.1)  grammar and surveyable representations (Wittgenstein, all anal-
ysed in: Baker, Hacker 2005: 320–334),
(10.2)  and everyday practices, customs, institutions, and FOLs (Wittgen-
stein GB, Baker 2004).
Here it seems worthy to mention that there is no such thing as the morphology
of grammar, of surveyable representations, or of forms of life since they al-
ready are forms i.e. their morphology manifests itself through phenomena and
as such can be appropriately investigated only by morphology as a method.
Since the first meaning of morphology applied by Wittgenstein is well in-
vestigated, we will try to make some sense of the second one, namely of the
morphology of human practices, customs, institutions, cultures, and FOLs by
making a few notes. Maybe there is a difference between:
11.  morphology as a method of making overview of “use of our words” of a
certain FOL and especially its language-games, and
12.  morphology as a structure of the FOL (phenomena) which manifests it-
self, and can be clearly explicated by applying the method of morphology
(from the first case), (suggested by Baker 2004).
We can summarize these aspects of morphology (10–12) in Table 2.3.



Wittgenstein the Morphologist I
69
Therefore, by making an overview of a certain language-game we in fact
produce an overview of one part of a certain FOL which in principle includes
more than just certain language-games, but also certain routines, practices,
customs, institutions, etc. (“institution of language and all its surroundings”
PI 540). Take as an example language-game fifty-eight from the BRB where
Wittgenstein writes:
•  In a certain tribe contests are held in running, putting the weight, etc., and
the spectators stake possessions on the competitors. The pictures of all the
competitors are placed in a row, and what I called the spectator’s staking
property on one of the competitors consists in laying this property (pieces
of gold) under one of the pictures. If a man has placed his gold under the
picture of the winner in the competition he gets back his stake doubled.
Otherwise he loses his stake. Such a custom we should undoubtedly call
betting, even if we observed it in a society whose language held no scheme
for stating “degrees of probability,” chances and the like. I assume that the
behaviour of the spectators expresses great keenness and excitement before
and after the outcome of the bet is known. I further imagine that on examin-
ing the placing of the bets I can understand “why” they were thus placed.
I mean: In a competition between two wrestlers, mostly the bigger man is
the favourite; or if the smaller, I find that he has shown greater strength on
previous occasions, or that the bigger had recently been ill, or had neglected
his training, etc. Now this may be so although the language of the tribe does
not express reasons for the placing of the bets. (BB 58)
Is it the core of morphology of a certain custom, practice, or “standard pro-
cedure” that it is the “background” and the “rough ground” of morphology
of one of its language-games? We must bear in mind that language-games
already are morphology. After all, what do we consider to be the confirmation
Table 2.3. Morphology as a method and as a structure.


70
Chapter Two
of a child, for example, correctly responding to an order such as “Sit down!”
if not that the child sits down? Wittgenstein’s morphology (together with
Goethe’s and Spengler’s), as M. W. Rowe pointed out, tried to “make vivid
and immediate what has become stale and abstract” … and … “inquiry ends
when the pattern is seen” (Rowe 1991:289, 302). But if all humans are so to
speak “morphologists by nature” (since we are “ordinary men living in the
natural world,” Rowe, op. cit.), then all of us are striving for precisely that.
2.4. OBJECTIONS AND REPLIES
It seems impossible to differ between these kinds of morphology (11, 12) in spite
of the fact which says that language-games are (proper) parts of forms of life.
The core of morphology is the analogy between parts of phenomena (we look at
phenomena, but we see only parts of it). Here we will end this section by stating
a few interesting questions and give possible answers by quoting Wittgenstein.
13.  How do we know that this analogy is better than that one?
(13.1)  “We are able to look at this custom in the light of that one. This
may serve, e.g. as a heuristic principle.” (RPP I 321)
(13.2)  “But the prototype must just be presented for what it is; as charac-
terizing the whole approach and determining its form.” (CV 21–2
and here 3.2)
14.  What is the criterion for being a “better analogy?”
(14.1)  “Through custom these forms become a paradigm; they acquire
so to speak the force of law. (The power of custom?)” (RPP I 343)
15.  Why are analogies and overviews (namely as descriptions) better than
hypotheses and developmental theories in any respect?
(15.1)  “But then can the rule not be replaced by an empirical proposition
(EP) saying that rulers are made in such and such ways, that people
do this with them? One might give an ethnological account of this
human institution.” (RFM VII 2)
(15.2)  They are better since they can establish “new order among descrip-
tions,” and because “the new arrangement might also give a new
direction to scientific investigation.” (RPP I 950)
If we cannot answer these questions differently or even better, or if we take
Wittgenstein’s answers (i.e. what we present as “his answers”) to be uncon-
vincing, then we cannot advance morphology over theory and science, holism
over reductionism and elimination, at least regarding Wittgenstein’s own
standpoint. Naturalists dislike Wittgenstein and not for his “linguistic turn”



Wittgenstein the Morphologist I
71
but for his “morphological turn.” By affirming morphology Wittgenstein
dissolves two things at the same time; what he considers to be “metaphys-
ics” on one hand, and “natural philosophy” on the other. An affirmation of
morphology means affirmation of “natural history.” This implies “distinctive
conception of philosophy” which is condensed in PI: 122 (Baker 2004:22),
but “Though clearly important, Wittgenstein’s concept of a perspicuous
representation is not itself perspicuous.” (Baker 2004:23) Nevertheless, this
investigation can be understood as part of a greater project of showing not
only how acknowledging the influence of Goethe’s idea of morphology on
Wittgenstein helps us to better understand Wittgenstein’s later philosophy (an
idea propounded most notably by Joachim Schulte), but, more radically, of
showing how some ideas implicit in the notion of scientific and philosophical
morphology (as developed from Goethe’s premises) can be put into the ser-
vice of explicating philosophy of later Wittgenstein. Even more, by studying
Wittgenstein’s notion of morphology, we can better understand the recent de-
velopments of scientific and non-scientific morphology. The next thing to do
would be to explicate some applications and results of morphological method
in Wittgenstein, and generally as a philosophical method.
2.5. ANNEX: MORPHOLOGY AS A METHOD
Morphology as a method (μορφή + λόγος) examines forms, structures, and
patterns, and as a form/pattern recognition technique is all about the activi-
ties of seeing exhaustively and describing synoptically. J. Lukin in the article
“Morphology” claims that morphology is used in at least four different mean-
ings, namely, “[…] as a form and a structure of something, as a description
of a form and structure, as a scientific investigation of a form and structure
independently of its function, and as a scientific method” (in the third and
fourth meaning Lukin mentions Aristotle and Goethe, see Lukin 2010). Here,
the last meaning in terms of philosophical method and technique will be es-
poused generally (remarks 16–19), and in a few interesting details as basic
steps of the procedure (remarks 16.01–19.1).
16.  One engaged in (philosophical) morphology needs to explore (look at
and look for) forms of phenomena in order to understand (see-as) their
patterns. Once a pattern (often explicated as a symbol, a sketch, or a
drawing) is seen and eo ipso understood, which means that it can be
clearly described in summary fashion (“clear” here means “clear to one
to whom it is described”) for a particular practical purpose, an inquiry
has ended.


72
Chapter Two
Then again, one can raise many questions regarding this preliminary descrip-
tion of morphology (16).
(16.01)  For instance, one can object that there is a matter or a content
of phenomena too and consequently that there is no particular
reason why it should not be investigated as well. That forms of
phenomena are vital for the understanding of their patterns is no
easy thing to explicate. However, there are some examples which
could be used as metaphors for the claim that forms are necessary
and sufficient for understanding (not explaining) phenomena. In
terms of drastic examples, it should be mentioned that there are
phenomena that are morphologically speaking matterless, for
instance clouds. They could be compared solely by comparison
of their forms, in this particular case their shapes (since a form
should not be understood in terms of shapes only, but in terms of
rules, regularities, and processes as well). From an observation
of their forms, and from their comparisons in various ways, one
could understand their patterns in terms of rules, regularities, and
proceedings.
(16.02)  Another objection can be that there must be some special kind of
observation included here, since a scientist observes as well. To
this objection one can reply that scientists observe with so to say
a presupposed agenda, namely a hypothesis or the model which
directs them toward seeing what they want to find in order to
explain, and nothing else. Say that one observes various games
(as described in PI 66) and sees a great variety of them which is
by no means revealed, but not perspicuous, and that one wants
to make it perspicuous. Now, scientists produce a hypothesis say
that all games must have something in common, and therefore
they see only features of games which are almost identical to all
games (we can name it as “scientific methodological reductionist
essentialism”). Finally, those games that do not have the feature
(common to all or at least to the relevant majority) they proclaim
to be something different from games. On the other hand, a
morphologist observes each one regarding all of their features
and operates without a hypothesis or a model whatsoever (PI
109). What one sees then is a complicated net of more or less
important similarities, and finally a game-pattern. Now, one will
hardly point to the very game-pattern since there is nothing com-
mon to all games. However, since such a game-pattern manifests
itself differently in all games, if one is presented with such a



Wittgenstein the Morphologist I
73
pattern and with two candidates for games, then one will clearly
see whether both of these candidates fit into the net (system) or
not. A completely different issue is which concept of “seeing,”
namely “seeing as interpreting,” is implied in morphology.
(16.03)  Then again, if forms are indispensable to such a degree, then
why the morphology as a form and a structure of something and
as a method, cannot be formalised and as such be considered as
a part of say formal ontology? The answer is somewhat compli-
cated since formal ontology is a part of logic, while philosophi-
cal grammar is not (philosophical grammar is “the descriptive
science of speaking,” BT 87). Namely, there is a grammar of
negation or conjunction for instance (in fact the grammar of use
of signs such as “¬” or “→”), and a grammar of logic in general,
in the same sense as a grammar of colour or of shape, but there is
no logic of morphology since morphology is already a method of
investigation, however, somewhat informal and qualitative. For
instance, if one observes that red colour is warmer on a lighter
than on a darker colour as its background or its surroundings,
then “being warm” is an analogy, not “a property” of red colour
which depends on its background. A metaphysician can say that
for red colour the property of being warm is differently actualised
in different surroundings. Well, what if there are no surroundings
at all (just slight difference in a tone of colour, or the difference
between painting technique say between water colours and oil
colours), or, what if someone says that red on black surround-
ings seems to be cold and bloodcurdling, and not warm at all? A
metaphysician seems to have no answer to this question, at least
not a metaphysical one, not because he dislikes metaphors and
analogies (as well as a naturalist, which de facto is the case), but
because he has no use of them in his system. On the other hand,
a logician cannot say much of interest regarding red being warm
and cold at the same time except perhaps that it is an example
of a contradiction, and as good as any. A morphologist can use
symbols instead of expressions for observed phenomena and
their features because of complexity of observed phenomena but
this does not count as formalisation. More to that, the difference
between logical and morphological emphasis of “formal” is like
the difference between explaining red colour in terms of its light
wavelength and describing a procedure of using a certain type of
dust (powder) or a type of a worm in order to get red painting
colour. Speaking in general and in the words of B. Pascal once


74
Chapter Two
again, what one needs is not esprit de géométrie, but esprit de
finesse, and finesse sometimes consists of implementing detailed
examination, and sometimes of pointing to a particular technique
or practice as a particular know-how (on Wittgenstein’s notion of
knowledge as know-how see Chapter 7).
Replies to these three objections guide us to the following explication of the
remark (16).
(16.1)  In morphology a form of a phenomenon reveals its content. In
other words, there is a primacy of phenomenon’s form over its
matter or content (in a way its form is its content), and there is a
primacy of say “pre-scientific observation” over “scientific obser-
vation.” There is a morphological agenda as well, but it does not
concern the phenomena, rather the very procedure, for instance,
“take everything what you see into account, nothing should be
left out” (which is anti-essentialist and anti-reductionist in all
respects).
Morphology is a method of investigating phenomena, their forms and struc-
ture without meddling into their substance (for instance a reductionism in
sciences is an example of precisely the opposite procedure), a method of
revealing their forms (sometimes even their whole patterns) as wholes (dy-
namic wholes, or processes, see Lukin 2011). Let me take another example,
and here we are using one of Wittgenstein’s metaphors, which was already
used by J. Good. It reads, “When we construct a theory or a definition to
capture its essential nature, we divest the artichoke of its leaves, trying to find
the real artichoke hidden within. Nevertheless, the artichoke is its leaves.”
Therefore, a morphological method “offers us a technique for exploring the
whole artichoke, for getting to the heart without losing the whole.” (Good
2006:2, PI 164) Phenomena have their content like an artichoke has its leaves.
Consequently, in the case of an artichoke, a morphologist is stuck with its
leaves, their organisation, and their pattern. The only thing possible to do is to
observe leaves, to describe their relations within a system as a larger whole,
and to reveal their structure, their form, and eventually their overall pattern.
(16.2)  In morphology there is a primacy of a pattern of a whole phenom-
enon over the structure of its parts (Wittgenstein himself seems to
have thought that the morphology of history of philosophy is one
of the finest examples of what philosophical morphology is not or
should not be, see BT pp. 300–18, PI 89–133).



Wittgenstein the Morphologist I
75
Investigation of this kind includes investigating many if not all similarities and
dissimilarities, analogies and disanalogies, most of which are of little or no use
at all. Loads of useless materials, tonnes of garbage as it were, and leftovers
of “wrong turns” during the course of morphological investigation are proper
parts of results of the inquiry. However, some of the results of such investiga-
tion which are useful regularly contain nothing more than stating the obvious
(perspicuous, transparent, manifested, revealed, or that which is not hidden), for
instance, “this
 is the pattern of this cloud” (say the one of a cirrocu-
mulus compared to the one of an altocumulus), or “this
is the pattern of an
artichoke,” pointing to a certain scheme or sketch of a particular type of cloud,
or to the artichoke pattern in comparison with the pattern of some similar plant.
Regarding the difference between revealed and perspicuous a note should
be supplied. Old philosophers of science (of the Vienna Circle, especially R.
Carnap) thought that “the revealed” or “the uncovered” is the same as “per-
spicuous” and vice versa. Namely, if one reveals something, then by being
revealed it is eo ipso perspicuous. Later on, (starting perhaps with W. V. O.
Quine), some came with the idea that this is not so, more to that, it became
obvious that there are many phenomena which are “revealed” but in the same
time “not perspicuous” at all, as for example two almost the same drawings
with say ten differences which should be detected (as the one which could be
found in crossword puzzle magazines, as shown in Figure 2.1). Now, what is
interesting is the opposite, namely that there are phenomena which are “not
revealed,” as it were, “hidden” but nevertheless “perspicuous,” as for instance
a pattern of a heap, i.e. a cone is hidden but perspicuous to a child who
knows-how to gather its toys into a heap when its mother commands it. Now,
the opposite can be of some interest also, since in some phenomena nothing is
hidden, and everything is revealed. However, not much that which is revealed
is eo ipso perspicuous, but most of it could be made perspicuous (PI 435,
regarding “hidden” see also the following sections: 91, 102, 126, 129, 153,
and 164, and regarding the expression “open to view” see 92, 126, for two
meanings of “hidden” see Luntley 2003:50–8). The change from hidden to
revealed is not the same as the change from not-perspicuous to perspicuous,
because some phenomena could be (made to be) revealed but at the same time
not be perspicuous at all. One needs to make them perspicuous.
On the other hand, the opposite idea is of paramount importance, since if
only phenomena are “open to view,” then nothing is hidden, but they are not
perspicuous, because they are too close, too familiar, too everyday, however
they could be made so for practical purposes. Precisely this possibility is the
possibility of philosophy as morphology and grammar (as shown in Table
2.4, regarding differences between Carnap, Quine, and Wittgenstein see
Hookway 1996). Morphology ends when a pattern is seen and described, and


Figure 2.1. Ten differences: the case of a revealed but not perspicuous phenomenon.



Wittgenstein the Morphologist I
77
such a description, if possible, should also be clear and as such, it simply
explicates a pattern.
(16.3)  In morphology there is a primacy of description and explication
(understanding) over hypothesis and explanation because noth-
ing (of any importance for philosophy) is hidden, everything is
revealed, and it should be made perspicuous.
One describes phenomena by describing all of their relations, and explicates
them in a survey, overview, summary, or perspicuous presentation (PI 122,
GB 133, see Breithaupt et. al. 2002) which consists of (propositions serving
as) grammatical remarks regarding the phenomena in question, and produces
understanding (because understanding is like having an overview, BT p. 3).
Now, a kind of practical objection can be provided against morphology.
On the other hand, no matter how persuasive such a description could be,
one should be aware of many difficulties which are integral parts of a mor-
phological technique. Morphology generally speaking, neither is a method of
our times, nor is a method for our times (culturally and practically speaking).
First of all, it is too expensive and too risky to be broadly applied nowadays.
That is so since it includes years, as it were a lifetime, of education, training,
and reflection of trainer and trainee over the very training process which at
the end could turn out to be completely unsuccessful. Namely, the probabil-
ity of success is extremely low. In addition, it implies a kind of (reflective
and self-conscious) conversion of a trainee to a new worldview, namely the
morphological one (one can try to compare Goethe’s approach to science, or
Spengler’s approach to history with opposite approaches, or Wittgenstein’s
approach to anthropology and culture in his criticism of Frazer (GB) in order
to see the morphological particular approach). In other words, it requires
working on a subject as well as working on oneself in similar proportions,
since it is of the essence for the success of training, which on the other hand
Table 2.4. Revealed and hidden phenomena.


78
Chapter Two
