it does not consist of anything fabulous. In addition, a morphological investi-
gation comprises elements of crafts, arts, sciences, and even religions, which
makes it somewhat unpopular these days.
17.  Applying morphological method is industrially and economically speak-
ing quite a risky deal; educationally speaking it is by no means too dif-
ficult for a trainer and a trainee in order to be applied among the general
population during the course of regular education, no matter how “Dew-
eyian” it could look like. Socially, politically, and culturally speaking it
is too dangerous, since it results with extreme “clarity” of an issue upon
which the investigation was conducted, and in many issues in mentioned
spheres “clarity” is neither needed, nor wanted. Perhaps philosophically
it could be applied without such fear, because of overall social and cul-
tural irrelevance of philosophy, then again why should it be if it increases
its irrelevance and unpopularity.
In terms of reply to this objection (17), one can say that morphology is a
method which humans apply on a daily basis, which is more to that a part
of various common practices (like face or emotion recognition among small
children), techniques, crafts, customs, traditions of ours, even of our sciences
(for instance mathematics, or biology), arts, humanities (for instance art
theory, cultural anthropology), and among the last group of our philosophy
as well.
(17.1)  Various techniques include the most primordial morphological
procedures, as it were, pre-conceptual activities. The morphologi-
cal procedure is pre-scientific in a sense in which a daily technique
is pre-industrial, and the daily industry is pre-scientific. Say that a
member of the group (Neanderthals or) Homo Sapiens Sapiens’s
(that is to say anatomically modern humans) invented a primitive
pot made out of clay by looking at his/her footsteps in mud and
by extracting a footstep together with the surrounding mud and by
drying it by the fire in a cave and then compare it to a pot made
of leaves or of wood. He or she made it because he or she saw an
analogy between a pot made of leaves and his/her deep footstep
in mud during rain. Simple analogy is sufficient for one to try to
make a primitive pot, and a motive, investigation, and invention
could be the need for hot water which could not be boiled in a pot
made of leaves or of wood. Of course, afterwards, a procedure
can be made more sophisticated (industry), and even scientifically
investigated (science), but at the beginning the morphology was



Wittgenstein the Morphologist I
79
sufficient. An important difference between a daily technique, an
industrial production, and a scientific investigation is that analogy
or simple similarity in any such case is in fact pre-conceptual in a
sense in which industry and science use pure concepts (in terms of
scientific models). What is sufficient in morphological procedure
is a kind of load of personal and communal experiences which can
be used in actualising such similarity or analogy for a particular
practical purpose. Such load is never conceptualised since there is
no need for a conceptualisation, in a way it is “moulded” (like a
pot) for a particular practical purpose.
Here, morphology would not be presented in all of the mentioned areas,
except the morphology in philosophy and therefore we turn now to this
particular subject matter. Philosophical morphology comes into view in two
approaches,
(17.2)  as a non-morphological but still philosophical investigation of
forms, structures, and morphological approaches in other sciences,
arts, and crafts (by E. Husserl, E. Cassirer and M. Merleau-Ponty),
(17.3)  and as a philosophical morphological investigation of phenomena,
linguistic and non-linguistic as well (for instance by L. Wittgen-
stein in terms of investigating language-games, and forms of life,
creating “perspicuous presentations,” and in the light of his claim
“What I give is the morphology of use of an expression,” should be
repeated once again, see Malcolm 2001:43, Lukin 2011).
The second approach will be of our interest here. In short, morphology as a
philosophical method consists of two general commandments, namely, look-
for, look-at and see-as, and describe perspicuously what you see. These two
commandments could be further divided into more particular sub-command-
ments; however, this will suffice for the present purpose of introducing the
very idea of the morphological technique during the course of philosophical
investigation (for detailed examination of these commandments see Chapter 3).
2.5.1. First commandment of morphology: see exhaustively!
Here, some elements of general description of morphology will be advanced,
namely two basic commandments of morphological procedure. However, one
should start by telling a story about phenomena. Phenomena are like dead
shells, there is nothing inside them, and there is no “deep reality” behind
them. One could imagine a child inventing a story about living beings that


80
Chapter Two
once occupied these strange “shell-caves,” tombs in fact, since thousands
of years ago; an evil tyrant won the battle against these innocent creatures
and consumed them eventually. However, they are empty now in the age of
humans. Nonetheless, a serious philosopher, a morphologist indeed, while
being like a child in some aspects, and like a shrewd crime scene investigator
in others, would rely completely on investigating not “their hidden essence,”
rather the very “structure of their surfaces,” and use her/his creativity and
imagination precisely in that direction. We do not want to mislead you, since
surely, a serious philosopher will try to tell you a story as well, but of a dif-
ferent kind (it would not be a legend; it would have to be a myth in terms of
a series of metaphors and parables, OC 95), like for instance the following
one. Imagine that you are presented with two masks and asked for a com-
ment. You make a remark that one is quite good for Earth, while the other
one is much better for a masquerade of “Deep Space 9” (DS9, Star Trek SF
TV series) for instance. What do you want to say, that the second one is too
bizarre for “Earthlings,” or that you really know how one should dress for a
masquerade for DS9? The first one of course, how should I know, for God’s
sake, in which fashion should one dress for such a peculiar occasion as a
masquerade at DS9? As far as I am concerned, all of them can be masked as
humans. It would be strange enough for me as a human. On the other hand,
what did you mean by “is quite good for Earth,” compared to what? Yes, I
know something about DS9; however, it seems to be insufficient to know
how to dress for a masquerade. Is this essential? In a way it is. If you know
typical masks for a typical masquerade in a particular culture, then you know
a great deal about it. It concerns being overwhelmed with harsh realities of a
cultural ontic place. Now, this last point concerns the difference between an
essence and a prototype—the first is believed to be found, while the second
is believed to be created. The difference between foundation and creation, or
within these, for instance, between finding, founding, and building founda-
tions on one hand, and giving existence, creating existence, and existence by
mistake, or as a side-effect of building (quite often phenomenon in a culture)
on the other hand is of some importance. In addition, the one who created a
prototype will never ask a masked man—who are you, and this point could
be quite illustrative in occasions such as this, namely a masquerade at DS9.
Now, imagine one looking-for, finding, and taking good looking-at dead
shells. In order to see the mistake one should go back to the beginning of an
inquiry and come to the conclusion—that what I was looking-for is precisely
what I will eventually find (PI 66). If one is looking-for an essence, interpret-
ing it as an essence before one even started to observe the surface (making
it perspicuous), then one will eventually interpret the results as an essence.
Now, if one is just looking-at without such rash presumptions, then one sees



Wittgenstein the Morphologist I
81
what one sees, there is nothing behind, no deep essence, no need for a reduc-
tion, just the structure of a surface, and its complicated system of relations.
18.  Look-for, look-at, and see-as! This is the first commandment of mor-
phology.
However, one must not forget that when one “sees,” for instance, a particular
practice (practiced by particular members of a group, at certain time, and in
a particular situation, say a member of an early Homo sapiens group making
pottery), within a larger unit, perhaps a habit, a tradition, or even a culture,
that what one really “sees” is “why,” no more, no less. Now, one can invent
a story regarding his/her own observation of a practice, write it down in a
diary, as cultural anthropologists sometimes indeed do (and this is important
part of the investigation process in contemporary cultural anthropology, see
Kottak 1999), but one should not forget that such a story is written for a par-
ticular practical purpose, say for an educational one, or in order to tell his/her
children what he/she has been doing during their field work. Everything ad-
ditional is bullshit (as it is used by H. G. Frankfurt) especially in philosophy.
“Why they are doing this?” one could ask, and your answer at first should
always be—“Go there and see for yourself, and afterwards perhaps we can
discuss it.” It is the same with all humanities, especially with irrelevant ones,
primarily with philosophy, because while bearing heavy burden of overall ir-
relevance, philosophers often struggle to accomplish a kind of relevant burden
of overall heaviness (as it were substantiveness), and precisely this was meant
by bullshit. Now, a fortunate side effect of the morphological method is that
one elegantly avoids bullshitting simply by refraining from a reduction on
one hand, and from an abstraction and a universalism on the other hand. Uni-
versalism can be a quite good test for rules in general; however, it is an awful
test for their exceptions. Morphology is all about exceptions, since patterns
are exceptional (if one reduces them or tries to make them abstract nothing
remains, one is just left with a lump of artichoke leaves). Indeed, morphologi-
cal relations (similarity, analogy, pattern, network, etc.) can be formalised,
but they are not formalised because of nowadays somewhat bizarre presump-
tions regarding language, truth, and logic, but because of purely educational
and pedagogical drives. If something, which at first appears to be a rule-like
phenomenon, has no exceptions, then it is not a rule at all, God knows what it
is. One could be easily misled. For instance, the following description (in D.
Adams’ style): It is raining. I see people walking around. From above, they
all look like a bunch of crazy space invaders consisting of umbrellas drilled
into their elegantly decapitated bodies. Just walking around pointlessly,
drifting. It is raining. Who knows what will be revealed when sunshine comes


82
Chapter Two
through the clouds? When umbrellas are closed? Suddenly, by direct divine
intervention, they will be given a direction? Obscured by clouds, obscured
by rain. Say that rain never stops, what should I conclude then if anything at
all? Basic morphological relations are:
(18.1)  being webbed—not-webbed, being patterned—not-patterned, be-
ing analogous—not-analogous (simple and complex analogies),
and being similar—not-similar.
Of course, these are not pure opposites; they are in more complex relations of
(18.2)  “being more … than… regarding…,” and “being less … than…
regarding….”
If one observes two parts or features of a phenomenon, or one feature in two
phenomena, then one investigates a similarity. Say that one of our previ-
ously mentioned early Homo Sapiens Sapiens’s observes clouds in order to
detect rainy clouds because he/she needs clay (types of clouds are showed
in Figure 2.2).
Figure 2.2. Types of clouds.



Wittgenstein the Morphologist I
83
There are two types of clouds in the sky on the day of the observation,
namely A and B types (as shown in Figure 2.3).
One compares them regarding all characteristics one can observe. First,
they both have a cloudy shape and regarding their basic pattern, they are obvi-
ously similar, but one dismisses this as irrelevant since one can easily differ a
cloud from a rain or a flock of birds. Now, one compares them regarding size,
shape, and colour. They seem to differ regarding all observed characteristics
and therefore they are overall dissimilar. The very observation runs as fol-
lows: A is bigger than B (size), A is much darker grey than A (colour), and
B is kind of flat, while A is taller (shape); therefore, A and B are different
regarding all of the observed characteristics, or in another words, A and B are
dissimilar. One observes that A is a rainy cloud, while B is not. Now, this is a
simple situation which presents the very procedure, but in an actual situation
an observation is far more difficult. Namely, actual situations are closer to the
following circumstances (as shown in Figure 2.4).
Here one observes three types of clouds in the sky. Obviously, one needs
to observe some further characteristics than in the previous case since colour,
shape, and size are not enough, especially if there are only clouds of type
B in the sky. One observes for instance their changes, and movement, and
say, concludes that clouds type B in most cases change into clouds type A,
then into clouds type C, and therefore this becomes the decisive similarity in
drawing the conclusion that B is more similar to A than to B regarding colour,
shape, and size change. One then concludes that cloud type B most probably
will turn into a pure rainy cloud type A, because the pair A, B is more similar
compared to the pair B, C regarding colour, shape, and size change. However,
such observations are relative in many different ways. Say that one observes
clouds of type B on various “backgrounds” and compares them with clouds
of type A and C (as shown in Figure 2.5).
Figure 2.3. Rainy and not-rainy clouds I.


84
Chapter Two
Obviously, cloud type B of the same shade of grey appears lighter grey on
the darker background than on the lighter background on which it appears
darker. Therefore, in the previous situation one could easily make a mistake
if one does not consider the background and concludes that say B is more
similar to A on the lighter background, or that it is more similar to C on the
darker background regarding colour. Such relativity, namely background or
surroundings relativity is possible regarding colour as shown in the present
case, and in others cases regarding size, shape, change, and especially move-
ment, and it is an integral part of any (morphological) observation.
Seeing as one of the basic morphological methods does not differ from ordi-
nary seeing in any significant respect whatsoever. However, a morphologist, as
being contrary to a scientist regarding this particular matter, does not have any
pretensions toward pure seeing; he/she is satisfied with the fact that it is always
half-seeing and half-interpreting (and “seeing as interpreting,” PI p. 193). In
order to see this ● as a dot, or as a hole, one needs concepts of a colour, of a
surface, or of a lack of surface and of a background or surroundings (see Chap-
ter 1, Section 1.2). In short, there are no perceptions without concepts (see for
instance examples and interpretations of a double cross and a duck rabbit head in
PI). However, it is not the interpretative aspect of seeing that is puzzling, rather
the very change from interpretative to the creative aspect of it (regarding PI p.
193, the point in which one slides for instance from an interpretation of a draw-
ing as a duck to an interpretation of the same drawing as a one-eyed monster).
Figure 2.4. Rainy and not-rainy clouds II.
Figure 2.5. Relativity of observation.



Wittgenstein the Morphologist I
85
When one sees the pattern, say, the one of a carpet, then one sees something, a
picture in one’s mind, as a kind of key for understanding the whole of a carpet,
its structure, its pattern. On the other hand, “picture,” “mind,” and “key” are
still metaphors. A football player (or a coach, or a conductor of a symphonic
orchestra), a midfielder especially, automatically sees the ball with his feet (there
is a physiological and a training-method explanation here) and at the same time
positions of all players on the pitch (his team and rival team, including their mid-
fielder as well). In addition, he sees the pattern of an action before it is played,
the pattern of a particular match before it has even started (he is the manager’s
relation to the team during a match, as it were the most important relation in the
system and crucial for the pattern). The match starts. The rival team leads by
0:1. The midfielder adapts to new circumstances better than the one of the rival
team and the final result is 1:1. How is this possible? Precisely because what he
sees is a whole and he see its pattern better than the midfielder of the rival team
(of course there are other factors, but this is or should be the crucial one). This
kind of football basic strategy belongs to the basic “organisational culture” of a
team; it constitutes a culture of this particular practice.
(18.4)  Seeing a pattern or even a network is the final result of the process,
while during the very process one sees many similarities and dis-
similarities (e.g. “This wing of this bird is similar to that foreleg of
that mammal,” “The pattern of this butterfly wing is similar to the
pattern of this tree bark”), analogies and dis-analogies, patterns, and
networks. While seeing and comparing all of a phenomenon’s char-
acteristics some similarities appear to be important at the beginning,
but they end up as quite irrelevant or even as dissimilarities, and the
situation is the same with analogies, more complicated similarities,
pattern-aspects, and network-aspects. Some characteristic, say A,
can accentuate itself, in simple relation of similarity of form such as
“A is more similar to B than to C,” while this particular similarity A
may be irrelevant in analogy of the form such as “Pairs A, C and B,
D are dis-analogous since their similarities are not mutually similar.”
This procedure goes on as far as it is needed in order for one to see
the overall pattern or the network of a phenomenon.
2.5.2. Second commandment of morphology:
describe synoptically!
Morphology as a (practical) technique / procedure is far more important than
morphology as a (theoretical) method. During the process of seeing, some
similarities, analogies, pattern-aspects, and network-aspects can be invisible


86
Chapter Two
to one person, but this is not so because the another person “sees them better”
or “has some special ability,” rather because the other person seeing them is
trained in such technique, and above all, is trained in being cautious, careful,
thorough, detailed in seeing, and comprehensive in describing.
19.  Describe perspicuously what you see! This is the second commandment
of morphology.
Then again, descriptions are always incomplete and this belongs to their core.
They are not crippled, or unfinished, but incomplete, since they are made
complete only for a particular purpose. There is a logical clarification of this
nonsense; say, a complete description would have to be complete regarding
its borders as well (seen as well as described, regarding the indescribable as
being beyond description), and in order to describe the borders of what is
described one needs to describe something which is not seen at all, and more
to that, the relations of what is seen to what is not seen, and so on, but this
is besides the point. On the other hand, it is of utmost significance that only
an incomplete description can be clear to the one to whom a phenomenon is
described. This limitation comes from the particular practical purpose of clear
descriptions, not from a regressus ad infinitum objection, or from the seem-
ingly “deep” claim that the very expression “a complete description” is “an
incomplete and non-descriptive expression” ex terminis.
One can imagine L. Wittgenstein saying something like the following: my
whole philosophy consists entirely of my private mental experiences, episodes,
events, and examples, wisely clothed in a jargon that is suited for various
dialogues and audiences. Sometimes, when I talk with overeducated philoso-
phers, I am bound to use common technical vocabulary of a particular school
of thought that they belong to in order to “get straight to the point” as they
sometimes refer to it (contrary to what non-philosophers mean by the very ex-
pression). However, sometimes, when they are really imaginative and creative,
I can use various metaphors and examples including propositions serving as
grammatical remarks, and then philosophy for a moment or two becomes what
it should be all the way, a kind of poetry spiced with a lot of humour while be-
ing at the same time astringent and strange. My whole philosophy should be
understood as a kind of poetry since that is what truly matters. My morphology
is there. There one can find descriptions for various purposes (similar notes
could be found in P. Valery’s book “Philosophy” which strangely resembles
some points both from Wittgenstein’s earlier and later works).
Now, regarding perspicuity, let us take a different example. When you
see a drawing of two shiny dice rolling on a gambling table do you think of
gambling (do you think of a definition of gambling, say, “the practice of risk-



Wittgenstein the Morphologist I
87
ing money or possessions on the result of something uncertain, for example a
card game or a race”), or do you suddenly feel lucky, do you feel happiness,
can you smell money? Then again, do you perchance feel sadness remember-
ing some unfortunate event from your own personal gambling history? Re-
search what others have to say about the drawing. Does the definition of the
gambling ever pop up? No! How utterly bizarre! What do you think, why not?
Now, compare various descriptions given by different people and summarize
them. What kind of sentences does your summary consist of, and if all people
to which it is presented “see” the point, is it then clear or perspicuous enough?
On the other hand, compare the daily patterns of a dozen people. You do not
see any similarity whatsoever. They differ regarding almost everything. How-
ever, you notice a few quite strange and idiosyncratic practices. One person
is rather nervously checking the sports page every morning at his work. You
attribute it to relaxation before work. The other has a pair of sponge dice
in her car, etc. You ascribe it to her awful aesthetical taste concerning car
interior. Now, you are observing all of them on Saturday evening. They all
went to a casino. What you regarded as marginal suddenly becomes crucial.
Now you see the similarity, now you understand the pattern. Now it is clear
and perspicuous. And at the same time you cannot point to the pattern, you
can only describe their daily activities which are similar at certain points,
and quite dissimilar at other ones. Patterns of their daily activities sometimes
overlap, sometimes are crossed, and are sometimes completely detached.
Now, you can clearly describe the pattern to someone to whom it may be of
some interest.
(19.1)  Perspicuous presentation is nothing more then a kind of summaris-
ing, clear description. Now, descriptions are an important part of
philosophical morphology as a procedure. Describing phenomenon
includes detailed descriptions of all of its relations, characteristics,
features, and aspects (propositions here function as empirical re-
marks, EP). In order to get a clear and summarising description of a
phenomenon one needs to understand a description as an instrument
for particular use, and here one should think of “a machine-drawing,
a cross-section, an elevation with measurements, which an engineer
has before him” (PI 291, BT p. 12: “One can compare understand-
ing a description with drawing a picture based on that description,”
“I understand this picture exactly. I could knead it in clay. (I could
reproduce it as a sculpture) I understand this description exactly;
I could make a drawing from it.”). One should also have in mind
a document of a patent including the summary drawing of a “use-
ful thing” and written summary or “an abstract” (illustrative topics


88
Chapter Two
regarding this matter are presented in Petroski 2000, as shown in
Figure 2.6). As an engineer (and writing patent documents as well),
Wittgenstein surely had in mind such drawings and summaries when
writing on the subject matter.
On the other hand he must have had in mind a structure of a poem
which is in a way a kind of summary as well. The third idea compris-
ing the activity of presenting is shown in his presentation of colours
in “colour octahedron” (PR). Now, a clear and summarising descrip-
tion includes propositions functioning not as empirical, but as gram-
matical remarks (sometimes referred to as “hinge-propositions,” HP)
to which different kinds of propositions are implicit, namely axis-
remarks (AP, OC 152) and these compose, are implicit, and at the
same time are essentially manifested at the core of our world-picture
and of our form of life, the way we live, act, and talk.
However, clarity and perspicuity are somewhat different. Clarity, precision,
and definability should not be mixed with perspicuity. For instance, a well de-
fined thing mostly means something that is a completely defined thing (in other
words, it is often concluded from not so well defined to undefined completely).
“A fence with a hole in it is as good as none.” (PI 99, see also 499) Say that
one is presented with two fences, A, the one without a small hole in it, and the
other, B, the one with a small hole in it (as shown in Figure 2.7).
However, there is a passage within B where a small creature can move
through it. Therefore, the land is not completely enclosed. The border is not
clear, it is opaque, it is not solid, it is porous, etc. However, there is the gram-
mar of “fence.” A fence is a kind of boundary determining a piece of land
and differentiating it from other neighbouring pieces. Therefore, a fence with
one small hole in it is a complete fence as well as the one without it. Conse-
quently, by the grammar of fence B is fence enough because it presents the
border of a piece of land sufficiently; B presents a full-blooded fence as well
as A does because it fulfils the function of a fence, it is used as a complete
fence. Fences represented by A and B as symbols are completely perspicuous
or perspicuous in the same degree, but, one may say, B is less clear, precise,
and defined than A. The hole in B is completely irrelevant in order for B to
perspicuously present a fence, and B is a proper fence as well as A. Let us
modify this example a little bit. Say that the fence is the fence of a vegetable
garden. Notice that the grammar of a fence is slightly changed. In such a
situation a hole big enough in order for a small dog to enter into the garden
through it would create an issue. However, the issue here is a grammatical,
not an empirical one. A fence now does not mean “a border demarcating a
piece of land” any more, but “a fence which prevents some animals to enter
and to destroy the vegetables in the garden” as well. One could imagine a



Wittgenstein the Morphologist I
89
Figure 2.6. Patent of a large paper clip made of spring wire (United States Patent and
Trademark Office, Patent no. 5, 329, 672).


90
Chapter Two
situation with a fence full of holes but with a sign that it is forbidden for
dogs to enter (like the ones in public parks). This is also a case of a very
perspicuous symbol of fence, and as any other symbol it mirrors our culture,
our language, and above all the way we act, in short, our Weltanschauung
according to which we act on a daily basis (here to act means to know, and to
know means to understand and to master a technique as well).
Philosophy as morphology and as grammar does not intend to solve, nor
can it solve problems sometimes regarded as philosophical but which are by
no means logical problems. Let us take an example of a heap.
The case of a heap: This case is not considered as the problem of material,
physical items of medium size, namely of their position in space and time, nor
as a problem in philosophy of science. However, it was and still is regarded as
a logical problem. Morphology dissolves the problem simply by pointing to the
very structure of the heap as a phenomenon. Say that there are four cubes of four
shades of grey. They can be in various spatial relations to each other and there-
fore composing various structures, say a layer (A), a pillar (B), creating part of
a wall (C), and creating a heap (D) in a way that three of them create a base and
the fourth is positioned on them roughly in the centre (as shown in Figure 2.8).
What one can say regarding these structures? One or two cubes cannot compose
a heap, but they can compose a small pillar, or a row. Three cubes can compose,
among other things, a small wall as well. However, it seems that four cubes struc-
tured in a way that three are the base and a fourth is put on top of them composes
a heap. As far as cubes and grains of sand are similar, four grains of sand and four
cubes as well are sufficient to compose the smallest possible heap of sand and of
cubes. Now, A is too flat to be considered a heap, in other words, it lacks height. In
that way it can be a part of a heap but it is not a heap, in fact, it is a layer of cubes.
On the other hand, B lacks basis, in fact it lacks A. However, it is a pillar by no
Figure 2.7. Two fences.



Wittgenstein the Morphologist I
91
means. C on the other hand can be a part of a wall. Now, only D seems to have the
heap structure or pattern which resembles a cone, a pyramid, or a kind of tent (it
only resembles because of the differences in the process of their composition). It
is so because heaps can be produced in essentially two ways, namely, by pouring
units of mass in one place, or by throwing mass at one place. These activities are
necessary and sufficient for the composition of a heap. Therefore, a heap has the
specific structure due to a specific structuring process, and this specific pattern of
a heap can be called the cone-pattern, produced by the heap-composition process,
and seeing that process in detail and in terms of its overall pattern means that it is
possible to describe the process clearly. Consequently, the morphological, not a
kind of solution, but a dissolution of the logical problem of a heap is to be found
in the grammar of heap, which says that any four units of any mass, say of sand,
poured in the same spot will produce a heap, and therefore four grains of sand
poured or thrown at the same spot are necessary and sufficient for the smallest pos-
sible heap of sand. This grammar of heap is nothing special, it just describes how
we humans use our words: grains, sand, a mass, heap, etc. (PI 496). The word heap
has precisely this “humble use” in an order such as “Willard, put your scattered
toys in a heap and then place them in the bag!” and similar.
Complexity and overall relativity of morphological technique in terms of
seeing patterns of phenomena on one hand, and solidity of grammatical re-
sults in terms of describing the overall pattern of an investigated phenomenon
on the other hand, seems to be understandable enough on the basis of these
examples. This tension in morphology, created mostly by tension between
the two mentioned commandments, needs to be accepted as a part of regular
morphological investigation. However, there are circumstances in which this
becomes a serious issue (say in a situation in which there is a need for change
in world-view and its most common practices). On the other hand, explicating
this issue surely goes beyond the simple answer to the simple question—what
is philosophical morphology.
Figure 2.8. Four structures of four cubes.


92
Chapter Three
Wittgenstein the Morphologist II:
Do not think, but look!
Light dawns gradually over the whole. (OC 141)
3.1. INTRODUCTION
There are a variety of ways to explicate Wittgenstein’s philosophical mor-
phology and philosophical grammar. One of the most used is by means of
interpreting PI 122 (BT 86–94), GB 133 and some related texts, through
the procedure that we applied in the previous chapter. More to that, many
authors only consider language-games as a phenomenon where the method
was strictly and completely applied (Baker, Hacker 2005, Vol. 1, Part I).
Nevertheless, PI and mentioned related works are full of applications of
morphological method to issues like consciousness, colour, certainty, math-
ematics (proof especially), religion, etc. (see Schulte 1990, 2002, Nordmann
2002, Breithaupt 2002, Baker 2004, see Chapters 7, and 8). The idea expli-
cates not just that Wittgenstein applied the method besides language-games to
practices also, but that it can be applied even to a wider range of phenomena.
This essay’s title is “Don’t think, but look!” referring to the known sentence
of section 66 of PI (the expressions are reprinted here in Table 3.1), since
precisely this section is where one should begin with an examination.
To begin with, the whole of section 66 will be quoted and arranged in
parts in order to be considered as a kind of clarification (not as a strict argu-
ment). Later on, a short commentary will be provided, and in the manner
of discussion, some ambiguous matters will be clarified. The next part will
include a somewhat detailed examination of ways how to create an overview
or perspicuous presentation of any phenomenon especially regarding Witt-
genstein’s concepts of looking-at, looking-for, seeing-as, and concepts of



Wittgenstein the Morphologist II
93
grammatical explication, clear description, and visible picturing. The present
chapter begins with an examination of sections 66-7 of Wittgenstein’s PI and
some related texts (BB, GB, PG, and CV) as fine examples of his philosophi-
cal morphology, besides P 122 and GB 133 which we extensively discussed
in the previous essay. Section 66 is separated into subsections, and the textual
context is specified and brief (connective) analyses of PI 66 and related texts
are presented. Further in the chapter some essential points of his method—
“don’t think, but look!”—will be discussed, especially regarding its contrast
Table 3.1. The text of PI 66–7 and some related texts.


94
Chapter Three
to the scientific method in GB. Finally, some applications of the morphologi-
cal method to traditional philosophical issues such as knowledge, existence,
and morality are presented.
The core thesis of the whole chapter is the following. In the twentieth
century, morphology continued to exist in many sciences and disciplines
(like art theory, psychology, biology, cultural anthropology, history, lin-
guistics, logic, mathematics, etc.) but not in philosophy (Wittgenstein is an
exception (in philosophy) that probes the rule (in other spheres)) due to a
series of unfortunate historical events. The question is why? Humans are
morphologists by nature and by nurture (culture), and morphology is at the
beginning of all sciences, arts, and techniques. This is so because morphol-
ogy is all about identifying existing similarities and dissimilarities in our
language, practices other than linguistic ones, and in the world, and it is a
method of philosophical inquiry. It is also about explicating these identi-
fied similarities and about producing descriptions of various phenomena
(especially practices of humans) in order to get “philosophical grammar”
which “perspicuously presents the phenomena” to the one to whom it is
presented (for a particular practical purpose). Morphological method is
philosophical method par excellence and by applying it one would get to
“philosophical grammar.” A part of this chapter, the most technical one
indeed, includes clarification of Wittgenstein’s notion of philosophical
grammar. The author, although being completely aware of the fact that
formalised morphological methods exists in many sciences nowadays,
tries to explicate these concepts from the point of view of Wittgenstein’s
philosophical morphology. In view of this endeavour, this chapter should
be understood as a further examination of Wittgenstein’s morphology
(based on previous investigations of his method, primarily by J. Schulte,
and of influences of Goethe and Spengler) as well as attempt of renewal of
morphology as philosophical method.
3.2. FEW REMARKS ON THE
CONTEXT OF PI 66 AND RELATED TEXTS
The whole of section 66 is closely connected to the previous one and to the
subsequent one (PI 65–7 as shown in Table 3.1). The related problem is
displayed in PI 67, BB 86–7, PG 74–6, and some other places, and solved in
a different manner, i.e. by giving different examples and descriptions. The
major context of these sections (i.e. 65–7), according to Baker and Hacker is
family resemblance, determinacy of sense, and the quest for essence (Baker,



Wittgenstein the Morphologist II
95
Hacker 2005, Vol. 1, Part I:201–26, Part II:145–91). However, the argument
in favour of “this examination” (conclusion as shown in Table 3.1) is stated
in BB 87 in a somewhat different context.
•  Regarding the conclusion, i.e. “similarities in the large and in the small,”
it is noteworthy that Wittgenstein occasionally refers to J. Nicod’s “Ge-
ometry in the Sensible World” (Baker, Hacker 2005:155). Nicod differs
between “global” and “local” similarities, and the consequence is important
for Wittgenstein since two things can be partially similar in one respect, but
dissimilar in another.
However, we consider that PI 66–7 via PI 90 is directly connected to PI 122
as the core idea of morphology in the context of topics on the nature of philos-
ophy, i.e. PI 89–133 (according to Baker, Hacker vol 1, Part II, 2005:191–285
as shown in Table 3.2). Sections 66 and 122 differ a little bit since 122 is a
statement about the very method, while 66 includes a slogan and somewhat
detailed explication of the method.
3.2.1. The topic
The topic of PI 66 is not the concept of a game, rather “the proceedings that
we call games.” Of course, the concept of a game is examined (Baker, Hacker
2005:153), but not from the point of view of the “definition,” or the “crite-
rion” of a game, rather from the viewpoint of a phenomenon of many differ-
ent games (“the proceedings that we call games” and then we have a list of
quite different games). What is given in the examination is the phenomenon
of many different games, a variety of games. In more detail, the topic is an
answer to the question—whether there is anything common to all of these
different games? Two answers are offered:
1.  there must be something in common, or they would not be called games,
and
2.  if you look at them, you will not see something that is common, but simi-
larities… and a whole series of them.
The difference is too obvious, namely between “something in common” as
presupposed by a name, its reference, and impossibility of “something in
common” as a result of observation of phenomena. Say that one is presented
with a series of games that all have in common winning and losing. The
idea (1) naturally leads one to accept a certain methodological agenda which


96
Chapter Three
“naturally” leads to the hypothesis that “winning and losing” is common to
all examined games (which miraculously confirms itself), and more to that
“mystically transubstantiates” into the “essence” of all these games. How-
ever, if one compares them in randomly constituted pairs, one could see that
this hypothesis is completely useless since similarities between such pairs do
Table 3.2. The structure of PI 89–133 (Baker and Hacker 2005).



Wittgenstein the Morphologist II
97
not have anything in common. One could observe for instance that some pairs
are quite similar regarding strategy and tactics like football and chess, while
some other ones are similar regarding the same characteristic such as rowing
and running at 1500 meters, but at the same time one could observe that these
two similarities between these two pairs are in fact quite dissimilar regard-
ing level of complexity. Therefore, there is nothing in common. However,
Wittgenstein seems to struggle with the following question—how to decide
between these two answers (1) and (2)?
3.2.2. The method
The technique suggested in favour of the answer (2) is “don’t think, but
look.” More closely, the technique of (1) is “thinking” and of (2) is “looking.”
However, what does it mean “thinking”? Does it mean:
(1.1) something like “creating a hypothesis for testing,”
(1.2)  or something like “investigating necessary conditions” (or formulat-
ing a criterion, a definition)?
In the case of (1.1) the interpretation would probably go along with PI 122 as
the crucial paragraph stating the very principle of “morphological method,”
i.e. directly against Frazer (see also GB 133), and in the case of (1.2) the in-
terpretation would be challenging, since, at least regarding some commenta-
tors, Wittgenstein used transcendental arguments concerning different topics,
perhaps this one included. On the other hand, what does it mean, “To look
at”? Looking at something is similar to:
(2.1) simple “observation” but also to something like
(2.2)  “looking at something with special assignment” (attention, care,
concern, caution, or ever mission), and “seeing” a model, or a pat-
tern. “Now it is important to note that we needn’t be aware of such
similarities thus pointed out when we are prompted to use the words
seeking, or expressions looking for, etc., in the case of trying to re-
member.” (BB 130)
Again, the connection to PI 122 is obvious, since if one wants to “see con-
nections” one must look at similarities and dissimilarities. This point is also
awkward given that (2.1) implies simple observation of phenomena (as it is
nowadays mentioned in terms of basic cultural anthropological and ethno-
graphic technique), and (2.2) implies something like a special kind of looking
at something. Again, in connection to PI 122 it seems that the method implies


98
Chapter Three
“looking at phenomena,” and “looking for similarities and differences,” which
results with “seeing connections,” and the capability of “perspicuously present-
ing a phenomenon.” Furthermore, (2.2) can be connected with “aspect seeing”
(or an ability to see the same phenomenon from different aspects, see Chapter
6), but also with quite a different method which is a part of quite a different
Weltanschauung or a kind of world-view which is needed if one wants to see
aspects (PI 122, GB 133). This is then an issue of so to say “conversion” to the
new world-view, namely to the morphological one.
•  Regarding the previous note on ethnographic technique of observation it is
common that there are two different ways of recording the data. There is
the method of creating formal field notes, and another method of recording
impressions in a personal diary, and (2.2) can be understood as the second
one (perhaps in the light of Wittgenstein’s idea (from the Preface of PI) that
PI is in fact like an album).
In the light of these methodological differences (i.e. 1.1, 1.2, and 2.1, 2.2) the
answer (1) is in fact a logical answer, since what one is looking-for is in fact
a definition as a criterion of identity, or in short, one is
• looking-at diversity and looking-for identity,
while answer (2) is morphological one, or in short, one is also
• looking-at diversity, but looking-for similarity
(regarding the question of knowledge of common properties of games see
Baker, Hacker 2005:153). What logic, and in this particular case definition,
can give one is just an identity, and what morphology can give one is simi-
larity, analogy, connections, intermediate cases, and finally a pattern. The
identity is not something that one can “see” but only construct by means of
logic and theory. Regarding this point Wittgenstein is explicitly following the
morphological method:
(2.3)  “Don’t look for anything behind the phenomena; they themselves
are the theory. (Goethe.)” (Goethe 1907: 575, Spengler 1932 I: 156,
RPP I 889)
What one is doing if one is “thinking” about the phenomenon is that one is
not looking at all. On the other hand, if one is just “observing” (“looking at”)
without trying to “look for” similarities, then one cannot “see” the connec-



Wittgenstein the Morphologist II
99
tions (one is “looking for” in this case as well, but looking for “an identical
feature” only, while dismissing non-identical ones). Nevertheless, “to look
for” is not just “looking at,” nor is it just “seeing as we interpret” (PI II 193),
it is like seeing an aspect. If this is the case, then “to see similarity” means
that one cannot be mistaken since one cannot be mistaken if one sees an
aspect and similarities (and dissimilarities of course) between parts of a phe-
nomenon (e.g. different games) which are like the rabbit aspect and the duck
aspect of the duck-rabbit head (introduced in Chapter 1, Krkač 2010:121–51).
There is an objection here, since Wittgenstein claims that seeing X as Y of-
ten includes imagining X as Y, (PI II 193–229), however, this point will be
discussed later on.
3.2.3. An application of the technique
Now, Answer 2 of PI 66 is in fact an application of the method. But this appli-
cation or an example is mentioned for a particular purpose, i.e. the purpose of
showing that if we compare games one to another, then we will find out that
there are no features common to all of them, that they are connected “only by
a complicated network of similarities” (Baker, Hacker 2005:153, as shown
in Table 3.3). This complicated network can be described in many ways,
some of which are more clear than others for some purposes. For instance,
one could say “Ball games are quite similar because of the use of a ball” and
such a remark can be of some use. However, one could observe afterwards,
say “Some ball games, like football and rugby, are more similar to chess
regarding strategy and tactics than to other ball games, say golf, cricket, or
playing with marbles.” This is what is meant by overlap of many similarities
and dissimilarities.
One should cross over many “intermediate cases” in order to see that they
are connected via these first and foremost observed cases (like tennis and
card games for instance). Regarding the solution that “there is no common
property” it should be mention that this is of essence since “overlapping of
Table 3.3. Different games and their overlap (PI 66–7).


100
Chapter Three
similarities” is what is important in morphology (“vast numbers of fibres
overlapping in a rope,” BB 87), not the fact that there is a common property
(or a feature that can be invented, there is no singe fibre “that ties the ship to
the wharf,” BB 87), and this is what gives strength to the method and indeed
to the results of application.
3.  Finally, the result of “looking at” is that “we see a complicated network
of similarities.”
But there are two questions regarding this “conclusion” or should we say
these “results”:
(3.1) First, do we really “see” a particular similarity?
Yes we do. Imagine games with characteristic features for instance, as shown
in Table 3.4. Now, many of these games can have features of others, but not
as characteristic features (their function in their pattern is completely differ-
ent). Regarding the illustration itself one can imagine a game called “guess
the game,” i.e. one in which one would have to guess the game by observing
distinctive and also minor features, and this would show so to speak “similari-
ties seeing blindness” or “similarities seeing ability (talent)” analogically to
the “aspect-blindness” or contrary to the “aspect-talent” (see PI II 213–214).
The second question is the following:
(3.2)  Does one really “see” a complicated network of similarities? “The
question ‘What do you see?’ gets for its answer a variety of de-
scriptions.—If now someone says, ‘After all, I see the aspect, the
organization, just as much as I see shapes and colours’—what is that
supposed to mean? That one includes all that in ‘seeing’? Or that here
there is the greatest similarity?—And what can I say to the matter? I
can point out similarities and differences.” (RPP I 964)
Table 3.4. Different games and their features.



Wittgenstein the Morphologist II
101
Surely, we do not “see a network” of similarities like we see for example
that two games are “similar” regarding the features of, say skill and cards
with which they are played. Nevertheless this issue is connected with two
different meanings of “seeing” (previously 2.1 and 2.2). We can see that de-
ception and delusion (in a tactical and strategic sense) is a crucial similarity
between chess and football. We can see similarity regarding the particular
moves of chessmen and of football players, and the same similarity on a
bigger scale through the entire game of chess and a football match (since
a player with the ball tries to trick (to dribble) against a player from the
rival team, as does the whole team by use of deceptive passes, movements
of players and of the whole team (consistently) try to trick the opposition,
which is very similar to the situation in chess). This particular similarity lays
the difference as well, the difference which says that changes of rhythm and
tempo are in the essence of football, while this is a minor feature in chess,
if at all. (Compare for instance the pattern of rhythm and tempo change of
the Brazilian national football team with samba and that of the Argentinean
national team with tango, and then compare these two patterns, or compare
dynamisms in van Gogh’s, Escher’s, and Munch’s drawings, lithographies,
and paintings). However, the feature of deception is not characteristic just
of some games but also of warfare (see for instance Sun Tzu 1971:41),
and some other activities (in fact it is present in certain degree in all social
activities according to some experts in game-theoretical explanations of
human behaviour). The point is that it is impossible to see in the same way
the whole network of similarities and a particular similarity. However, and
especially in the case of Wittgenstein’s concept of “presentation,” it seems
to be possible to draw (sketch, illustrate) a model of such a network. The
only such case is the case of colours presented in “Philosophical Remarks”
(as shown in Table 3.5).
In any such case, one could really see “the model” which presents “a
network” (on importance of diagrams and visual models in early and later
Wittgenstein see Biggs 2001, and Hamilton 2001). The morphology as a
method seems to be connected with “looking,” “seeing,” and “seeing-as”;
and seeing-as is, as Wittgenstein writes in PI, something between seeing and
thinking (between seeing spot as mere seeing, seeing double cross as being
half seeing and half interpreting, and seeing as pure interpreting). Therefore,
seeing-as (the duck-rabbit head for instance) would be between the double
cross case and the pure interpreting case (we see as we interpret!). But again,
do we really “see” connections? What about the following examples:
• “I see a pissoir now, and a fountain after.”
• “I see a bicycle now, and a bull’s head later.”


102
Chapter Three
This comes down to the question—what is the difference between an essence
and a prototype (Primal Plant (Uhrflanze) in Goethe, Spengler, and Wittgen-
stein? When one “thinks” of an essence in terms stated by a definition or a cri-
terion, one does not “see” anything at all, but when one “sees” the prototype,
one in fact “sees” the best example, or more precisely “a complicated network
of similarities…” (3.2) which itself manifests the prototype. The prototype
does not represent the phenomenon or a variety of games for instance, but
when one shows a prototype, then one is showing a network of similarities by
means of good example, not the particular similarity, but so to speak, the key
(“secret law,” Goethe) for seeing many different similarities arranged in the
“similar way” (because phenomena can be similar not since they have some
identical and some different features, although they can be similar because of
the arrangements or structure of features they have, and precisely that is what
makes the morphological method comparatively better that any other; it keeps
a phenomenon (artichoke, PI 164) alive and in the same time it explicates
what was before our eyes all the time).
Table 3.5. Octahedron of colours (PR, author’s drawing).



Wittgenstein the Morphologist II
103
3.2.4. Some results of an application
What is the first and foremost result of the application of the method of find-
ing similarities and analogies? The application and its result are unconvinc-
ing since if one does not “see” the purpose of philosophy as Wittgenstein
did, then one cannot “see” the use and the purpose of identifying global and
local similarities and dissimilarities, and of explicating the best similarities
in order to produce a presentation (even illustrated as a drawing, sketch, or
geometrical model) or comprehensive and clear description which clearly
presents a grammar of the use of a word, or of the way a certain practice is
done by experienced doers.
4.  The result of the application of the morphological method is that one has
a comprehensive and still clear description of a phenomenon, for example
of the use of the word “game.”
(4.1)  To achieve such a description or presentation which is perspicuous
for a certain practical purpose (teaching a child a new kind of game
for instance) is the final goal of “grammatical” investigation as a
philosophical investigation.
Beyond that, an investigation is not conceptual anymore, and eo ipso is not
philosophical. Such a description can be used in creating “hypothesis” in
scientific manner, or to direct scientific investigation, but then its perspicuity
for the previously mentioned particular practical purposes is lost, its purpose
becomes philosophically unclear.
3.2.5. Summary of analysis of PI 66 and
development of the technique
We can summarise Wittgenstein’s innermost ideas from PI 66, and related
texts (other places in PI, BB, GB, CV, etc.) regarding morphological method
by claiming the following.
5. Humans are morphologists by nature and by nurture.
(5.1)  Every technique, skill, or standard procedure begins with morphol-
ogy.
(5.2) Morphology is at the beginning of all sciences.
(5.3) Morphology is a method of philosophical inquiry.
(5.4)  Morphology deals with phenomena in respect of their “organic” unity
and change.
(5.5)  Morphology is about identifying existing similarities and dissimilari-
ties, analogies and disanalogies in our language, world and life.


104
Chapter Three
(5.6)  Finally, morphology is about explicating these identified similari-
ties and analogies and about producing descriptions of phenomena
(especially human practices) in order to get “philosophical grammar”
which “perspicuously presents the phenomena” (FOL) for various
practical purposes.
3.3. MORPHOLOGY AS A METHOD:
LOOKING-AT, LOOKING-FOR AND SEEING-AS
On the other hand, this list of claims (in item 5, i.e. from 5.1 to 5.6) should be
investigated properly. Many scholars emphasise Wittgenstein’s philosophical
morphology as the exact name of his method (note that at least two of them
are authors of his biography, namely B. McGuinness and R. Monk, and oth-
ers, like J. Schulte, at least heavily accentuate the context of his life).
6.  “A careful consideration of cases in detail leading to a demonstration of
analogies leads to a certain form of understanding of a domain, which may
be exactly the understanding that we require.” (McGuinness 2002: 450 see
also GB 131)
Again, the question is—what kind of investigation morphology (described in
5.4.–5.5.) really is?
(6.1)  First and foremost it seems to be an “observational-descriptive
investigation” since “looking at” phenomena and “describing”
them, i.e. their relations like in the form “α being more similar
to β than to γ in respect of f1” is in fact is an observation and a
description, and it is an observation of phenomena, and a descrip-
tion of similarities and dissimilarities between them. Of course,
the result may seem as a description of a certain group of similar
phenomena (say games), but in fact the result is always a set of
grammatical propositions regarding correct use of “signs” (say by
saying “Chess and football are strategic games” one can not only
show correct use of the word “chess,” but of the expression “a
strategic game” as well). However, the issue on the very nature of
the relation between “experiential propositions” as the result of an
observation, and “grammatical propositions” as the result of per-
spicuously presenting “what is seen” deserves a separate chapter
and cannot be discussed here in detail.



Wittgenstein the Morphologist II
105
(6.2)  Similarities which are seen between phenomena, or between parts
of a particular phenomenon are the only thing that can be seen if
one is trying to “look and see whether there is anything common to
all” (PI 66, answer 2). One can look at the phenomenon for one’s
entire life so to say, and could not “see” “the common,” since
“identity” (or essence) is not something that can be seen. Never-
theless, “an identity” can be imagined, constructed, postulated,
presupposed, etc. Seeing the common, and seeing the pattern, are
two different issues. Namely, the common among given phenom-
ena is simply something identical in all samples, while the pattern
is something which is differently manifested (regarding function)
in different samples. For instance, at the end of the film “Papillon”
the character played by Steve McQueen immediately “sees” not
what is common to all waves, but rather the pattern of the whole
phenomenon, specifically, a pattern composed of the rhythm and
the tempo of waves).
(6.3)  Now, if one sees similarities regarding some features of a phenom-
enon, and dissimilarities regarding other features of the same phe-
nomenon (i.e. between its parts), this can be interpreted as a kind
of “complicated net or web” which does not manifest any kind
of identity, essence, or “something common to all” parts. There-
fore, the result is that parts of a phenomenon or of many phenom-
ena are just more or less similar, but not identical or completely
different in any respect whatsoever. This does not mean to claim
that “scientific reduction” is improper in any respect, since it is
applied in science; but that it is impossible, or at least useless in
philosophy.
(6.4)  If morphology is contrary to scientific or quantitative methods,
at least in the manner that it is indisputably “philosophical,” it
surely is qualitative. Say that Wittgenstein saw some problems in
TLP and tried to solve them (at least from the period of the paper
“Some Remarks on Logical Form” onwards). Regarding the aim of
philosophy, it seems that he slightly modified the idea of “clarity”
with the idea of “perspicuity” as it was suggested at the begin-
ning of the chapter. On the other hand, regarding the method, he
seemed to put the “logic” of TLP in the broader context of “philo-
sophical grammar” in PI and related works, and logic became just
a special case or a chapter of a philosophical grammar. Now, what
is the “quality” (the new quality) of morphological method, and
what does it reveal?


106
Chapter Three
Let us go back to the already mentioned case (see Figures from 1.39 to 1.41)
and let us make it simpler like in the following one case (simpler in a way
that the number of features is more restricted than it is in the case of games,
as shown in Figure 3.1). This illustration is the same one as in the first chapter
and the only differences are colours of objects.
Now, one could try to “observe” and to “see” what is common to the ob-
jects in the 3.1. We have at least three features, namely: colour, shape, and
size. Regarding the form of expression, we can use two forms:
•  “a and b are similar regarding a particular feature …” for instance, or
• “a is more similar to d than to e regarding a particular feature ….”
Regarding shape, similar are: d-e, a-c, and b-f, regarding colour similar
are: a-d, b-c, and e-f, and regarding size similar are: a-e, b-c, b-d, b-f, c-d,
c-f, d-f. Regarding the relation of “being more similar” there is a problem,
namely, if we value all features equally, then for example e is equally
similar to f and to d, to the first regarding colour, and to the second regard-
ing shape. However, if we compare all objects from b to f, then e is more
Figure 3.1. A group of coloured objects a-f.



Wittgenstein the Morphologist II
107
similar to d and f, than to b and c, since with d and f it shares two features
(size with both, shape with d, and colour with f), but with b and c just one
feature (size). Consequently, we can count overlapping features, but we
can also make a hierarchy of features. However, these similarities we can
“see.” This relation of “being more similar than…” can be seen in simple
cases like the one in this simple illustration, but it very hard to “see” it in
more complicated daily cases (for example—which pair of people are most
similar compared to any other pair of people on some square at a certain
time, say that there are 100 people on the square?). What we see, if we
see anything at all in the second case is in fact a prototype, an example
of the most similar pair of objects (or people), and this example does not
represent an essence, rather the whole web of similarities, and of dissimi-
larities. But we can sketch the model of these relations, and although this
model does not represent the web, still when one shows such a model one
is in fact showing the web. The question is—is it possible to draw a sketch
or a model of such web, and if it is, then how would it look like in the
case of the previous illustration (perhaps something like the following il-
lustration)?
This would be a hint of such a model of a web of similarities and dissimi-
larities. Now, we can really see such a model, not just “in our mind,” but on
the paper. As shown in Figure 3.2, pairs of objects b-c and b-f are the most
similar objects, and also comparatively the most similar pairs, i.e. they are
similar in respect of colour and size in the case b-c, and in respect of shape
and size in the case of b-f. More to that, since the similarity regarding size is
the most frequent one, this seems to be to most important feature of the web.
Of course, there are dissimilarities, since e-b, c-e, and a-b are not similar in
any of the investigated features whatsoever. However, it is important that
(among investigated features) there is no feature that is common to all ob-
jects, or to all pairs of objects, rather there is a relatively simple web of rela-
tions. Therefore, if one looks for something common, one will find nothing in
common regarding all investigated objects and their features (Of course, they
are geometrical forms one could insist, but then what to do with the feature
of colour?).
•  As a result, the answer to the question—is morphology different from sci-
entific investigation—is yes, since it includes: observation, description, dis-
covering similarities (dissimilarities), analogies (disanalogies), and seeing
the web (pattern) of these relations and even creating a sketch or a model
of it (perspicuous presentation, a grammar), which makes it a conceptual
and a philosophical investigation.


108
Chapter Three
Figure 3.2. The model of similarities and dissimilarities of objects a-f (Rela-
tions: black = dis.similarity, grey = similarity in size, very light grey = similarity
in colour, medium grey = similarity in shape).



Wittgenstein the Morphologist II
109
3.4. MORPHOLOGY AS A METHOD:
DESCRIBING CLEARLY, PICTURING VIVIDLY,
AND EXPLICATING GRAMMATICALLY
Why is the model presented in Figure 3.2 both good and bad? Since we see the
objects in the first coloured illustration as “geometrical figures,” to compare
them regarding their geometrical features seems to be the right thing to do.
Nevertheless, “we see as we interpret” (PI p. 193), or “One thinks that one is
tracing the outline of the things nature over and over again, and one is merely
tracing round the frame through which we look at it.” (PI 114); therefore to
see these figures as geometrical is just one way of seeing-as. More to that,
it is a dreadful model overall since it introduces a feature which is not geo-
metrical, namely colour. It is possible to see these objects as something quite
different, for example, object d as a window in a zoological garden showing
sea animals, etc., and their combinations as something utterly strange, but
nevertheless perhaps completely meaningful to a child. For example, a child
could see these objects as a face or as a house with a tree and a small swim-
ming pool nearby as shown in Figure 3.3.
Is this illustration in 3.3 better than the one in 3.2? Yes and no. If one wants
to investigate for instance a child’s capability of recognising patterns, then the
answer like the one in Figure 3.2 would be viewed as better, but if one wants to
test a child’s combinatorial abilities, then perhaps answers like the one in 3.3
or similar would be viewed as better. The point is that all of these models are
equally good given the practical purpose for which they are produced.
•  While in the case of the observation we introduced context and observer
relativity, here in the case of perspicuous presentation we can introduce
(practical) purpose relativity.
In the same way, one aspect of the grammar of relations of these objects
is that they can be represented in both ways. Criteria are different, but if a
Figure 3.3. Faces, and a house, with a tree and a pool.


110
Chapter Three
child says that objects d and c are not just similar in size but also represent
eyes or a face, or that object b and a are not dissimilar since they are parts
of a representation of a house, the child would not be wrong. Anyway,
a child would be wrong if it is asked to compare objects a-f regarding
size, colour and shape and gives something like a description of a face
or a house with a tree and small pond. On the other hand, if any of such
criteria are not applied, then both overviews are equally good and one can
be better than the other only regarding a particular practical purpose. Of
course, the very order of objects in Figure 3.2 can be quite suggestive, as is
shown in Figure 3.4. More to that, there is no neutral order of these objects;
they always suggest a certain set of similarities, analogies, or patterns.
The point is that one who is investigating similarities and dissimilarities,
analogies and disanalogies, and patterns and networks should observe all
of these combinations in order to exhaust all possibilities, but it is difficult
for example to see the survey or the overview of relations in both previous
illustrations together. However, such an overview is possible, as we will
suggest in the last section.
Another point regarding the relation between confronted illustrations as
possible combinations of objects, as in the basic illustrations 3.1 and 3.2 is
quite important. In both of them all morphological elements are completely
present; namely,
• one is “looking-at” objects (so to speak, configurated in a certain way),
•  one is “looking-for” possible similarities,
•  and one is seeing these objects, i.e. their relations,
•  in fact “seeing (them)-as” some combination, or a series of different com-
binations.
Figure 3.4. It is easier to see a house and a tree on the right side than on the
left side.



Wittgenstein the Morphologist II
111
Objects a and b are not similar at all regarding shape, size or colour, how-
ever, they are similar, since if one puts b over a then they represent a house.
Another case is that objects e and f in the basic illustration are similar only
in respect of colour, but they can both be seen as treetops. There are however
some differences between these approaches.
7.  We can say that it is possible and practically useful to differ between
seeing-as, namely:
(7.1)  seeing similarities between objects (objects a to d, or e to f),
(7.2)  seeing similarities between pairs of objects or seeing analogies,
meaning that completely different objects could have identical rela-
tions, so that in this respect they would be similar (d is lighter than a,
or e is lighter than f), and
(7.3)  seeing patterns as seeing a pattern of a web of similarities and analo-
gies, and this model being the best example of this web for a given
purpose.
A further issue that we should deal with is somewhat odd, namely—do simi-
larities, analogies, and patterns really exist in the world, or—what is it that
we explicate by seeing-as and by applying the morphological method? Now,
are there a duck and a rabbit in the “duck-rabbit” drawing? Surely they are
not there, because, if they are “out there,” then it would be possible to “draw”
them out, and to “draw” two different, and more importantly, “one-aspectual”
drawings, one of a duck and the other of a rabbit, but surely something is “out
there.” There are lines on a paper. Lines are structured in certain way, and this
structure of lines can be seen as a duck and as a rabbit. This structure is very
delicate since a slight change even in the proportion of the whole drawing can
make it harder to recognise a duck or a rabbit (since it would be then a rabbit
with quite short ears, or a duck with a rather long beak).
•  Another type of perspicuous presentation relativity is the following,
namely, the very order of objects in the basic illustration is also quite deli-
cate, so to speak interpretation-sensitive.
Objects can be ordered in such a way that one instantly sees a face or a house,
a tree and a swimming pool, or in a way that one can see geometrical objects,
their similarities and analogies. However, this could mean that similarities are
almost instantly visible due to the given structure of a phenomenon (perspec-
tive, context, and the similar). Analogies on the other hand, seem to be a little
bit more “hidden,” i.e. in order to see them one needs to explicate relations of
similarities between objects (regarding “hidden” see Chapter 2, Section 2.5).


112
Chapter Three
Finally, a pattern seems to be completely hidden, nevertheless explicable in
various ways for various purposes. Do we see a pattern of a carpet or of a
curtain (of a daily movement of a person or a whole family, of practice, style
of life, culture, or of a labyrinth)? (When one sees the film “Shining” and sees
the mother and her son in the labyrinth, does one instantly see the pattern of the
labyrinth, or only afterwards when in the hall of the hotel its is revealed?) We
do, but sometimes instantly, since the regularity is simple (like in the case of
a carpet or a curtain), on the other hand sometimes we need to “look closely,”
with particular attention to details, and then we see some similarity, then anal-
ogy, and finally a pattern (like in the case of a particular strange practice, a
quite unknown style of life, or in the case of the labyrinth in the “Shining”).
The point is that objects are not the only things that can have patterns (on their
surface, or as a law of their structure), because actions, events, and processes
have patterns that are sometimes very complicated to become acquainted with.
For example think of the pattern of clouds (in spite of CV 48). Surely, they
have shape, and this is quite useful in areas with a lot of rainy days per year, but
rainy clouds for example have a certain shape and also a certain pattern (shade,
movement, altitude, etc.). Seeing these similarities, analogies, patterns, and the
whole network, and explicating them in the form of a series of grammatical
sentences, and through this process making the phenomenon perspicuous to the
one to whom it is presented is the whole task of the philosophy as a conceptual
investigation (of for instance the philosophical morphology of meteorology).
3.5. MORPHOLOGICAL POINT OF VIEW
Morphology deals with phenomena in respect of their “organic” unity and
change (see previously mentioned proposition 5.4). What one needs to “see”
and partly to “invent” as well is the grammar of the world, of the world-view.
The basics of such grammar are epistemology, ontology and ethics. We need
to answer the following questions: (a) what can be known, (b) what there is,
and (c) what should be done about it? Regarding (a), according to some ver-
sions of trope theory what can be known are only phenomenal tropes given
in ordinary daily experience and which we express by the use of experiential
utterances (narratives, impression/expression). In other words, what we expe-
rience are “free-floating tropes.”
•  Impression-like parts of an experience are particular ways in which tropes
force us to recognize them,
•  and expression-like parts of experience are our narratives or utterances
(imagination) by which we respond to this force and create noumenal tropes.



Wittgenstein the Morphologist II
113
From this point on one could proceed to the epistemology of know-how (in
twentieth century philosophy some hints of this were given by philosophers
such as James, Ryle, Wittgenstein, Habermas, and Foucault, see PI 150). Re-
garding the second claim (b), noumenal tropes, as being narrative constructs
from phenomenal tropes, function in two strange, different, but quite useful
ways, namely, as concrete universals, and as abstract particulars. By concrete
universals we construe narratives of non-abstract and abstract universals (mate-
rial properties, kinds, concepts, numbers, etc.). By abstract particulars we con-
strue narratives of non-concrete and concrete particulars (such as properties of
kinds, things, etc.). From this point one can proceed to some trope and process
ontology and preserve the whole vocabulary in its “humble use” (PI 97). Re-
garding (c) noumenal tropes, they are bundled in things, facts (acts), processes,
the world, and second-order narratives. From this point one can proceed to the
ethics of lege artis or standard daily procedures, or ethics of routine (a certain
version of surely not utilitarian, but of pragmatist or consequentialist ethics
perhaps). However, with a little help of our second-order narratives one can
also create a grammar of spheres such as anthropology, theodicy, cosmology,
technology, science, law, economics, politics, history, culture, art, etc. The final
result of such an investigation would be what Wittgenstein calls “philosophical
grammar.” Now, in which way, one could ask, does the morphological method
of approach to knowledge, existence, and moral values differ from other ap-
proaches? Say that for example we have the following drawing.
Figure 3.5. Duck-rabbit head (see Figure 1.43).


114
Chapter Three
This (as shown in Figure 3.5) is known as the duck-rabbit head and as is well
known, it is much discussed by Wittgenstein in PI and RPP I, II. The point with
such, and similar two-aspect drawings morphologically speaking, is not that
“seeing” aspects can be “explained” scientifically or in terms of folk psychol-
ogy, rather the point is that such cases cannot be “sufficiently described” at all
without morphology. More to that, morphological “perspicuous presentation”
is precisely the presupposed or implicit reason why one gives priority to the
scientific “explanation” than to some kind of folk psychological “explanation,”
or vice versa. The duck aspect and the rabbit aspect of the drawing are like the
face aspect and the house with a tree and a swimming pool aspect in Figure 3.3.
•  The first thing is to acknowledge that aspects such as those in the illustra-
tions are possible.
•  The second thing, which should be mentioned, is that these aspects are
presented or given in quite particular situations with a reasonably precise
practical purpose.
•  But the most important thing to point out is that these aspects are possible,
are used, are understood, and can be apprehended by others only “on the
background of” and only “on the rough ground” of a particular FOL. This
means that these aspects can be apprehended only by:
•  one who can “see” the place of these aspects in the wider net of similar cases,
•  one who can “see” that they are used for a particular practical purpose, and
•  one who can “see” that the phenomenon in question cannot be “described”
in any other way if one is going to give a “perspicuous presentation” of it,
•  although such “descriptions” / “perspicuous presentations” can be used in
completely different ways, for example for the purpose of scientific “ex-
planation.”
Nevertheless, this so to speak aspect-seeing talent (more or less developed)
regarding duck-rabbits, pissoir-fountains, bicycle-bulls, and similar, re-
garding epistemological, ontological, or ethical grammatical issues is just
a metaphor for something else which is far more important. If our overall
philosophical goal is some kind of clarity and surveyability, then any such
overall surveyable presentation or synopsis in the form of a philosophical
grammar can be produced by means of morphological method. Wittgenstein
tried to produce it regarding language, mathematics, consciousness, certainty,
religion, aesthetics, and some other phenomena. It should be noted here that
in regarding Wittgenstein’s attempts to do so, one should differ between his:
•  attempts to clarify the very notion of philosophical grammar,
•  attempts to find some grammatical propositions regarding different phenom-
ena (mathematics, language, consciousness, certainty, religion, art, etc.), and
• explicit mentioning of some grammatical propositions.



Wittgenstein the Morphologist II
115
Such examples of grammatical propositions are surely parts of philosophical
grammar, but they will be considered only as examples or illustrations if one
cannot “see” the world from the morphological point of view. Four points are
of paramount importance if one wants to apply the morphological method and
in order to produce “a grammar” of a given phenomena (see before 5–5.5.):
 8.  the morphological point of view requires a kind of “conversion” to
the new/old worldview (Weltanschauung), or world-picture (Weltbild)
which is by itself similar to the process of religious or ideological con-
version and it is accomplished practically rather than contemplatively or
theoretically (one must acquire a technique of “looking-at,” “looking-
for,” “seeing-as,” and describing clearly).
 9.  The next point consists of the vital insight which says that the reasons
(arguments, justifications, and warrants) for practice (practice of “con-
version” included) are always less justified than the practice itself which
they suppose to justify (OC 307), which leads so to speak to “the primacy
of practice, or action” (Praxis, Handlung).
10.  The third point is that one who is “converted” to the morphological
point of view “sees” the world very easily from many “perspectives” and
what seems to be the only possible perspective “before” (like the logic
of TLP), now is just one among many such perspectives (in view of the
philosophical grammar of PI), for example “one-eyed monster with both
ears on the left side of his head-aspect” becomes an aspect just like duck-
aspect, or rabbit-aspect.
11.  Finally, the converted one “sees” not just isolated analogies and dis-
