analogies, similarities and dissimilarities, but a whole net of them and
is capable of combining perspectives in different ways. There are no
“explanations” or “conclusions” in such an investigation, rather one can
come more or less close to the “grammar” of some phenomenon which
is, as well as clarity, surveyability, and perspicuity the philosophical goal
par excellence.
We could bring together these methodological insights with previously
formulated sections regarding “philosophical grammar.” Under the chapter
“knowledge” would be the epistemology of “know-how” or epistemology of
practical knowledge (see Chapter 7). This would consist of very particular
examples of ordinary and everyday practical situations and be accompanied
with routines and sentences (speech-acts, language-games), which best exem-
plify the complete absence of doubt regarding some practical matters. Epis-
temology of “know-that” or propositional knowledge (which unfortunately
is the main topic of contemporary epistemology) would be just one among
many special cases in such grammar. Under the chapter “ontology” (or the
world) would be the ontology of tropes and processes applied to everyday


116
Chapter Three
things, events, and changes. In the same way as in the chapter “knowledge,”
this would include paradigmatic examples of everyday processes. Traditional
ontological topics such as particulars and universals, time and space, sub-
stance and properties, propositions, and causation would be marginal as in
the previous case, if present at all. Perhaps the topic of appearance and reality
would be of some importance as well, but only marginally, and connected to
the issue of the very nature of philosophical grammar, not to the issue of par-
ticular grammatical propositions. Finally, under the chapter “ethics” would
have to be an ethics of everyday standard procedures and actions (and of their
exceptions as well). These propositions would be partially grammatical and
partially empirical (perhaps something like what Wittgenstein calls “hinges”
or “axis” in OC). “Pure normative ethics” would not be explicated beyond
the scope of the grammar of good. Nevertheless, the far more important point
with these “chapters” is that they would be parts of completely different top-
ics. Knowledge for example would be a part of the chapter “human action,”
or “human practices,” ontology would be a part of the chapter “processes”
which would include grammatical propositions regarding natural and artifi-
cial processes, continuous and discrete ones, etc. Finally, ethics would be a
part of the chapter “culture” which would include subchapters like technol-
ogy, economics, law, politics, art, etc. Therefore, the whole world-picture
would have to be completely different from the one “we” have and use in
philosophy (scientific or otherwise) these days.
3.6. PHILOSOPHER AS A CUSTODIAN OF GRAMMAR
Before we so to say check this application of morphological method regard-
ing at least some philosophical issues, no matter how convenient for our pres-
ent purpose they could be, some questions should be raised in order to fully
clarify the issue of the procedure itself. Namely, the question regarding the
role of philosophical grammar in these cases, especially in the case of relation
of language and reality, but then this question leads to another one which is
the one a propos the very concept of philosophical grammar, and the one to
which we must explicate some of the central actions one should perform in
order to get perspicuous presentation. These topics will be addressed in this
section. That the concept of philosophical grammar is of dominant impor-
tance for Wittgenstein’s later philosophy does not need special emphasis or
textual proof (see for instance Forster 2004:7–21). It can be said that it relates
to the whole of his later philosophy, that is to say to its nature, method, goal,
and results, namely to particular philosophical grammars (of for instance
consciousness, colours, certainty, different qualia, etc.). In another words,



Wittgenstein the Morphologist II
117
“A whole cloud of philosophy is condensed into a drop of grammar.” (PI p.
222) However, there are few questions regarding the nature of philosophical
grammar, namely:
•  How does the language-reality issue come into discussion here as a kind of
solution of the problem of grammar, or is it other way around?
•  What is the structure of grammar, or of which kind of items is it structured,
namely, does it consist of rules, descriptions, and examples, or of some-
thing additional?
•  How the grammar relates itself to the non-grammatical, i.e. phenomenal,
scientific, empirical, logical, etc.?
Philosophical grammar seems to be of utmost importance for the “appear-
ance-reality” issue since “The connection between “language and reality” is
made by definitions of words—that belong to grammar.” (PG 55) The ques-
tion here is—what kind of a connection is it, or what kinds of descriptions be-
long to the grammar? Are these descriptions simply what Wittgenstein calls
“grammatical rules” and are they expressed by what he calls “grammatical
propositions”? The following passages can help to elucidate these questions
a little bit more.
•  “That one empirical proposition is true and another false is no part of gram-
mar. What belongs to grammar are all the conditions (the method) neces-
sary for comparing the proposition with reality. That is, all the conditions
necessary for the understanding (of the sense).” (PG 45)
Therefore, it seems that the grammar sets up certain criteria for deciding is
some X appearance, or is it in reality, as it were, criteria for linking a proposi-
tion about some X and some fact. However, there are no criteria in grammar,
there are only examples and on some occasions some of them serve as criteria
(paradigmatic cases).
•  “But the essence of language is a picture of the essence of the world; and
philosophy as custodian of grammar can in fact grasp the essence of the
world, only not in the propositions of language, but in rules for this lan-
guage which exclude nonsensical combinations of signs.” (PR 54, see Alva
Noë 1994:21)
Here it seems that with the aid of philosophical grammar we can grasp the
world via rules for describing it. Therefore, rules of correct application via
exclusion of the nonsensical and toleration only of sentences with sense are


118
Chapter Three
precisely this connection. For instance, every sentence regarding one’s hands
implies that one’s hands do not disappear when one does not look at them
(OC 153, and this could be regarded as a grammatical proposition, or a cer-
tainty of our world-picture, CWP).
•  “The connection between “language and reality” is made by definitions
of words, and these belong to grammar, so that language remains self-
contained and autonomous.” (PG 55) “Like everything metaphysical the
harmony between thought and reality is to be found in the grammar of the
language.” (PG 112, Z 55) “Grammar is not accountable to any reality. It is
grammatical rules that determine meaning (constitute it) and so they them-
selves are not answerable to any meaning and to that extent are arbitrary.”
(PG 133)
Here one could ask why there is no such list of chapters of our grammar and
of some grammatical propositions. Is the proposition “Cats like milk” a gram-
matical one, or an axis one? Or the other way around (PI 122, OC 422)—are
the propositions of our world-view in fact grammatical propositions?
•  “Essence is expressed by grammar.” (PI 371) “Grammar tells what kind of
object anything is. (Theology as grammar.)” (PI 373)
These passages give affirmative answers to the questions previously
posed. For instance, in OC Wittgenstein seems to discriminate between at
least four levels of propositions of our world-view and these seem to be
examples of at least some such propositions covered by the chapter which
consists of criteria under which it is possible to claim some propositions of
our world-view with absolute (practical, and “calm”) certainty. The second
question is—what is a grammar? This question can be formulated regard-
ing many not so clear issues; namely—does philosophical grammar consist
of anything else besides rules instantiated in a series of examples? In what
way are rules related to each other if they are rules of the certain grammar
of a certain language-game? Is there a list of all the chapters of our gram-
mar? Is the structure of our linguistic expressions given in the chapters
of our grammar? One must be aware that Wittgenstein often claims that
there are no strict boundaries of our world-picture (WP); that it changes in
time (especially OC 95–9). Then, if WP is regarded as a clear description,
there are other WPs near to it; however, if it is regarded as implicit and
manifested by series of standard practices, then there are other FOL near to
it. A possibly illustrative or even quite important passage regarding these
questions is the following:



Wittgenstein the Morphologist II
119
•  “Using the octahedron as a representation gives us a bird’s-eye view of the
grammatical rules. The chief trouble with our grammar is that we don’t
have a bird’s-eye view of it.” (PR 1)
This section should be connected with PI 122 and with the overall account of
“overview” / “perspicuous presentation.” What seems to be important for the
philosophical grammar is that its chapters or maybe even groups of criteria
can represent an overview of the use of a word or even of a whole family of
words (for instance colour words, emotion words, shape words, etc.). This is
suggested in PR 69, which reads as follows:
•  “The words Colour, Sound, Number etc. could appear in the chapter headings
of our grammar. They need not occur within the chapters but that is where
their structure is given.” (PR 3–4) “I mean: what I call a sign must be what is
called a sign in grammar; something on the film, not on the screen.” (PR 69)
This passage is somewhat difficult to understand, nevertheless it seems that
rules as grammatical propositions and their elements are all parts of their
grammar, and their structure is manifested inside the chapters (PR 3–4).
•  “The rules for ‘and,’ ‘or,’ ‘not’ etc., which I represented by means of the
T-F notation, are a part of the grammar of these words, but not the whole.”
(PR 83) “Grammar as (e.g.) the geometry of negation. We would like to
say: “Negation has the property that when it is doubled it yields an affirma-
tion.” But the rule doesn’t give a further description of negation, it consti-
tutes negation.” (PG 14) “What interests us in the sign is what is embodied
in the grammar of the sign.” (PG 44)
This line of explication seems to be quite important, as a kind of criticism
of TLP that goes against the interpretation that says that the grammar is just
a new name for logic of TLP. The logic of TLP became merely one among
many equal parts of grammar in later works. For instance, the “rules” for
“and”—“or” do not have priority over the rules for “red”—“blue.”
•  “Grammatical rules determine a meaning and are not answerable to any
meaning that they could contradict. Why don’t I call cookery rules arbi-
trary, and why am I tempted to call the rules of grammar arbitrary? I don’t
call an argument good just because it has the consequences I want. The
rules of grammar are arbitrary in the same sense as the choice of a unit of
measurement. Doesn’t grammar put the primary colours together because
there is a kind of similarity between them? Or colours, anyway, in contrast


120
Chapter Three
to shapes or notes? The rules of grammar cannot be justified by showing
that their application makes a representation agree with reality. The anal-
ogy between grammar and games. Language considered as a part of a psy-
chological mechanism. I do not use ‘this is the sign for sugar’ in the same
way as the sentence ‘If I press this button, I get a piece of sugar.’ Suppose
we compare grammar to a keyboard which I can use to direct a man by
pressing different combinations of keys. What corresponds in this case to
the grammar of language? If the utterance of a ‘nonsensical’ combination
of words has the effect that the other person stares at me, I don’t on that
account call it the order to stare. Language is not defined for us as an ar-
rangement fulfilling a definite purpose.” (PG 133–8)
•  “The grammar explains the meaning of the signs and thus makes the lan-
guage pictorial.” (PG 142) “Grammar describes the use of words in the
language.” (PG 23) “What interests us in the sign, the meaning which mat-
ters for us is what is embodied in the grammar of the sign.” (PG 44) “One
would like to say: the proof changes the grammar of our language, changes
our concepts. It makes new connexions, and it creates the concept of these
connexions. (It does not establish that they are there; they do not exist until
it makes them.)” (RFM III 31)
These sections seem to be further notes on the nature and the purpose of
grammar since they describe the use and as such the meaning (given that the
“meaning is use” in a non-trivial sense) is embodied in the grammar of a sign.
“Grammar consists of rules.” (PI 497)
•  “Grammar describes use of signs, but doesn’t explain it.” “In the use of
words one might distinguish surface grammar from depth grammar. What
immediately impresses itself upon us about the use of a word is the way it
is used in the construction of the sentence, the part of its use—one might
say—that can be taken in by the ear.—And now compare the depth gram-
mar, say of the word to mean, with what its surface grammar would lead us
to suspect. No wonder we find it difficult to know our way about.” (PI 664)
This last quotation does not differ between two kinds of grammar; nevertheless
it does differ between the surface-aspect and depth-aspect of the grammar of a
certain word. In conclusion it can be added that the grammar of a certain word
or of a certain language-game is in fact its overview, i.e. at least a very clear de-
scription of its use and such a description, since it has a certain level of generality
and abstraction, could and in fact it does, provide the criterion of all particular
uses, and as it were—it constitutes them. The third question regarding grammar
is the one concerning its relation to non-grammatical items.



Wittgenstein the Morphologist II
121
•  “Physics differs from phenomenology in that it is concerned to establish
laws. Phenomenology only establishes the possibilities. Thus, phenom-
enology would be the grammar of the description of those facts on which
physics builds its theories. To explain is more than to describe; but every
explanation contains a description.” (PR 1) “If the formation of concepts
can be explained by facts of nature, should we not be interested, not in
grammar, but rather in that in nature which is the basis of grammar?—Our
interest certainly includes the correspondence between concepts and very
general facts of nature. (Such facts as mostly do not strike us because of
their generality.) But our interest does not fall back upon these possible
causes of the formation of concepts; we are not doing natural science; nor
yet natural history—since we can also invent fictitious natural history for
our purposes.” (PI II 230, RPP I 46)
•  “The grammar of the word knows is evidently closely related to that of can,
is able to. But also closely related to that of understands. (Mastery of a tech-
nique.)” (PI 155, related to grammar of to know and to be certain in OC)
These sections suggest the difference between grammar and science on one
hand, and between grammar and knowledge on the other hand. Grammar is
not science; it is more like phenomenology, which in this sense seems to be
quite close to morphology. Maybe one of the reasons or just consequences
of such an attitude toward the science is concentrated in explicating the
grammar of the word “to know” which is closely related to the grammar
of words like activity, ability, understanding, and even technique, and
know-how and less to the propositional knowledge, knowledge of facts and
laws, and know-that (OC can be completely viewed in the light of the last
quotation). In conclusion we can mention that there are just a few (Witt-
genstein’s own) examples of grammatical propositions (as “chapter titles,”
or as explicating grammatical use of certain word), like the following:
• “A complex is composed of its parts.”
• “Four metres is a length.”
• “There is no greatest cardinal number.”
• “The class of lions is not a lion, but the class of classes is a class.”
• “The chair goes on existing, whether I look at it or not.”
• “Every rod has a length.”
• “I know... only from my own case.”
• “An order orders its own execution.”
• “There is no such thing as a doubt in this case.”
• “One cannot know the future.”
• “Cats like milk,” etc.


122
Chapter Three
These examples can be seen as propositions of which our world-view consists
(our (human) form(s) of life). At least some grammatical propositions are
“axes” or “hinges,” or as they are referred to, “hinge-propositions.” Further-
more, grammatical propositions like hinge-propositions or axes (sometimes
but quite rarely used as axes-propositions) also can be:
•  Applicable to a particular person or group of persons (“I have spent most
of my life in Croatia”),
• Applicable to any person (“I have two hands,” “My name is N.N.”),
• Subjective (“I know... only from my own case.”),
• Objective (“Water boils at 100 degrees Celsius at sea level”),
• Trans-historical (“The Earth has existed for a long time”),
• And historical (any example of a discovery will do), etc.
This division of propositions in WP suggests that hinge or grammatical
propositions cannot be specified by their propositional nature or by their
content, rather by their function in our language since they are not manifest-
ing sense or non-sense. They are particular descriptions serving as principles
or criteria on the basis of which we differ between what is sense and what is
non-sense to say or even to do. It is rather demanding to put together an over-
view of anything, say shape, colour, sound, smell, taste, movement, duration,
etc. Why is that so? Since for example, it is not clear at all what we should
count as grammatical proposition of taste or duration. Regarding duration for
instance, is the proposition
• “A process has its duration”
a grammatical one, and if one is tempted to say—“but, if everything which
was said is correct, then of course,” then one should compare it to proposi-
tions like
•  “There are discrete processes that do not have duration in the same sense
as continuous processes do.”
• “One hour has sixty minutes.”
• “A football match lasts 90 minutes + additional time.” or
• “It takes a few minutes to wash hands properly.”
In order to see that the propositional content is completely irrelevant (of
course that the last one can serve as an empirical proposition, or even as part
of a clarification during a pedagogical process). What is relevant in order to
see whether a proposition is a grammatical one is to see its function as the
criterion of use of all words, and sentences of its language-game. However,



Wittgenstein the Morphologist II
123
Wittgenstein seems to overestimate the significance of explicating grammar
if he is trying to argue for such a procedure since our grammar already mani-
fests itself in our use of words, something that he realized as well. Here it
should be emphasized that explicating a grammar is closely associated with
the morphological method, therefore, there is no such procedure as isolating
the grammar from everyday language (colloquial speech) in order to make
“hypotheses and claims.” It is the other way around, since the investigation is
conceptual, we need “conceptual glasses” in order to see the same language-
game from different aspects, to see similarities, dissimilarities, analogies,
disanalogies, and patterns in order to point to its grammar which was mani-
festing itself all the time. Nevertheless, a further question remains similarly
as in the matter regarding the very nature of the FOL, namely—is there just
one grammar of for example shape, or smell, or are there more grammars? A
“form through which we look at nature” (PI: 114) can be one and the same
for all humans, but at the same time there can be numerous forms, not just
of different cultures, but of different personalities, and individuals as well.
Therefore, there must be different and numerous grammars. In light of the
idea that language-game is a (proper) part of an FOL (PI 23) the question
could be narrowed to the issue of cultural plurality.
3.7. CONCLUDING REMARKS
The major concern of this chapter was Wittgenstein’s philosophical morphol-
ogy as philosophical method, namely: investigating its nature according to
some of Wittgenstein’s texts from PI and related works, detailed analysis of
the method in terms of analysis of: observing (seeing), comparing (finding
similarities and dissimilarities), describing (producing perspicuous presenta-
tion as a part of philosophical grammar), and explicating the very concept of
“philosophical grammar” as well as some of the basic tools of morphological
method (similarity, analogy, patterns and network).
My interest was not: overall analysis of method of morphology like in
sciences (mathematics, logic, biology, cultural anthropology, or history),
arts and various techniques, comparison between Wittgenstein’s morphol-
ogy with the same method in Goethe or Spengler perhaps, or comparison of
philosophical morphology with other philosophical methods no matter how
close or distant. Besides such approaches, I also tried to show that philosophi-
cal morphology is applicable to different philosophical topics and to different
phenomena some of which traditionally are and some of which hopefully will
become of philosophical interest in the future. In light of these restrictions,
I can only spell out some conclusions already mentioned in the previous
chapters.


124
Chapter Three
12.  Morphological investigation does not include “explanations” or “conclu-
sions” rather one can come more or less close to the “grammar” (clear
description) of a phenomenon which is, as well as clarity, surveyability,
and perspicuity the philosophical goal par excellence. Observation of any
pair of phenomena as wholes, and in all their details, and comparison of
all of their characteristics can be concluded with a clear description con-
sisting of “grammatical propositions” which must be perspicuous to the
one to whom it is presented for a particular purpose.
13.  Morphology is about identifying existing similarities and dissimilarities,
and analogies and disanalogies in our language, world and practices and
about explicating these identified similarities and analogies and produc-
ing descriptions of phenomena (especially human practices) in order to
arrive at “philosophical grammar” which “perspicuously presents the
phenomena” (of an FOL) for various practical purposes. Proper overview
as a clear description of a network presupposes that all of the characteris-
tics are de facto observed and mutually compared and that none of them
is reduced, eliminated or left out (by mistake, oversight, negligence, or
similar). In doing so humans use experiential, grammatical (hinge), and
axes remarks (or propositions, see OC 95–9) (as shown in Figure 3.6)
14.  There is no complete identity or complete difference. There are no two
completely identical or completely different phenomena. Any two phe-
nomena share at least one characteristic directly or indirectly via third
phenomenon with which they are composed in a pattern or in a network.
Figure 3.6. River metaphor and three kinds of remarks (OC 94-9).



Wittgenstein the Morphologist II
125
15.  Nothing is hidden. Open to view are only phenomena given in daily ex-
perience as complicated networks consisting of many patterns, analogies
and similarities between them. There are no hidden characteristics of
phenomena. If a particular phenomenon is given in experience, then all
of its characteristics are observable and comparable since they are visible
and apparent.
16.  Humans are morphologists by nature and by nurture (culture). Every
technique, skill or standard procedure begins with morphology. Mor-
phology is at the beginning of all sciences. It is a method of philosophical
inquiry and it deals with phenomena in respect of their “organic” unity,
proceeding and change.
17.  However, the morphological point of view requires a kind of “conver-
sion” to the new worldview. The “converted” one “sees” the world very
easily from many “perspectives” and what seems to be the only possible
perspective “before” it seems to be just one among many perspectives
“now.” The converted one “sees” not just isolated analogies and dis-
analogies, similarities and dissimilarities and patterns, but a whole net
of them and is capable of combining and perspicuously presenting many
perspectives in different ways.


126
Chapter Four
Wittgenstein the Morphologist III:
A Form of Life is a Form of Culture
(with Josip Lukin)
Practices give words their meaning. (ROC III 320) I once said & perhaps
rightly: The earlier culture will become a heap of rubble & finally a heap
of ashes; but spirits will hover over the ashes” (CV 5) … of culture as an
(eternal) witness—as if an avenger of the deity. As if it were awaiting a
new incarnation (in a new culture) (PPO 55)
4.1. FORMS OF LIFE
In the “Foreword” to the 1977 edition of CV, G. H. von Wright says that
notes from “Culture and Value” “…can be properly understood and appre-
ciated only against the background of Wittgenstein’s philosophy” (CV x).
Here, we attempt to understand only one note from CV and just one part of
the background, namely Wittgenstein’s notion of culture from CV 8–9 and
forms of life (further abbreviated as FOL) mainly from PI (as forms of cul-
ture). Nevertheless, we will begin with some passages from Wittgenstein’s
later works and also with a short summary of ways of interpreting them, since
there are only a small number of instances where the notion of FOL appears
in his works (as shown in Table 4.1)
Regarding the translation, we must note that we do not accept Ans-
combe’s change in translation from “FOL” to “life-form” in 2001 3rd
edition of PI since it emphasizes biology and eo ipso naturalism which
is according to this change biological rather than anthropological (which
on the other hand seems to be closer to Wittgenstein, therefore, we agree
with Revised 2009 4th edition by P. M. S. Hacker and J. Schulte). The key
issue regarding FOL is—what is the meaning of the phrase, i.e. how is it
apprehended by Wittgenstein?



Wittgenstein the Morphologist III
127
•  Initially, it is not novel because it seems quite clear that he picked the phrase
from Spengler (1932, I, 55). He was reading Spengler’s book in the 1930s
and in the same years the core of the phrase FOL appears in his works for
the first time (PG 29).
•  Next, the word “form” is used with precise purpose, i.e. to highlight the
very method by which Wittgenstein was attracted in Spengler (as R. Haller
calls it “the method of descriptive morphology” which is in fact quite im-
portant as a method in morphology as a part of a certain kind of “regional
ontology,” or “regional grammar,” Haller 1988).
•  Finally, we will investigate whether this influence was relevant merely as
methodological, or was there also certain influence in content and results of
an application of the method, because there are many interesting questions
Table 4.1. Forms of life.


128
Chapter Four
regarding FOL: is there just one FOL or are there many of them? Is FOL
particular or universal? Is it a part of life or the whole of life? How many
FOLs are mutually connected? Etc.
Therefore it seems that FOL is at least a part of a method or the goal of a phil-
osophical investigation, nevertheless it is possible that it is an ontic category
connected to the method (perhaps as being something that is overviewed, i.e.
FOL as a web of connections between particular practices, routines, institu-
tions, etc. Here the word ontic is used almost in a Heideggerian sense in
which in most cases it means anthropological). Now we must turn back to
some additional technicalities. Firstly, there is a difference in the use of the
singular and the plural, i.e. form/forms of life and what seems to be obvious
is (as shown in Table 4.2):
(a)  use of the singular when he is writing on one FOL as the background for
many actions and also linguistic acts (PI 241, OC 358, Malcolm 2001:75, Z
Table 4.1. (Continued)



Wittgenstein the Morphologist III
129
567 (the most important), CE 397 “foundation” part, since language grows
on “foundations” which are FOL1, FOL2, etc. as “regular activities”),
(b)  use of the singular when he is writing on only one FOL as a whole of
practices of a certain community (PI 19, PI II: 174, PI II: 174, Z 569, or
even as N. Garver explicated “only one human FOL”),
(c)  use of the plural when he is writing on many FOLs as many practices of
a certain community (CE 397 “regular activity” part, RFM 95),
(d)  use of the plural when he is writing on many FOLs as different cultures,
and also different cultural backgrounds (PI II 226, commented in Kripke
1982:96–8).
Therefore, misunderstanding is possible regarding the difference
• between (a)–(b),
•  and (c)–(d), (in another words, not to differ between two meanings of the
singular, and of the plural as well),
but also regarding the distinction
• between (a)–(c),
•  and (b)–(d), (so, not to differ between the singular and the plural in gen-
eral), (on different readings see Garver 1994: 244–7, especially regarding
PI 19, and 23).
Now we can consider major interpretations of these passages. PI 19 speaks in
favour of language-games as presupposing an FOL as well as PI 241 (noted by
J. Klagge), but according to PI 23, “speaking language” is a “part” of the “FOL”
as “an activity,” so we can presuppose that there are some other parts as well and
that there is no strict identity (or pure language-game account of FOL, Garver
1994:246). CE 397 proposes a different notion, i.e. “growing” (i.e. organic
metaphor). PI p. 174 proposes that there is only “one” complex FOL which has
certain modes, (regarding Garver’s interpretation of “this complicated FOL” as
“this” one human FOL like he suggests, contrary to less complicated ones like
Table 4.2. One or many form/s of life.


130
Chapter Four
animal FOL, or plant FOL, or is it just that “hoping” is complicated in compari-
son with something simpler than it), but PI p. 226 suggests that there are many
FOL, and that they are given. These and related interpretations are sometimes
confused as well as Wittgenstein’s own lines, for example in PI p. 226 where
there is no way to decide whether he gives emphasis to social or to a natural (bio-
logical) understanding (Cavell 1989:42). Furthermore, there is no real difference
between behavioural and biological interpretations (Hunter 1986). S. Cavell in
his “The Claim of Reason” emphasized the distinction between “forms” of life,
and forms of “life” (life forms) and also the second (biological) interpretation,
but he changed his explication in support of the first (cultural) interpretation
(Cavell 1979:83, 1989:40–41). Nonetheless, Cavell’s suggestion is to merge
natural (biological) and social accounts into one. Furthermore, he also suggests
that FOL in fact “is” such a combination (Cavell 1989:44, referring to PI p. 174).
To conclude this preliminary part we can say that there are several, somewhat
competing, interpretations of the FOL concept:
(a)  Language-game account (von Wright, Schulte, Baker and Hacker) PI
19, 23, RPP I: 630 Language games are interwoven with non-linguistic
activities. (Glock 1997)
(b)  Social account, the way of life account (Bloor, Cavell 1989) PI 19 Identity
between FOL and ways of life. (Combined with biological, Cavell 1989:44)
(c)  Cultural account, anthropological, conceptual relativism (Cavell 1989,
Glock 1997) BB 134, RFM 95, culturally-natural account (Backer and
Hacker 1995) Differentiation between FOL and other cultural phenom-
ena, (b), and (c) are rather difficult to distinguish.
(d)  Behaviour-package account (Kripke 2002:96–8, Hunter) PI p. 226, RPP I
630 Identity between FOL and patterns of behaviour. (objection in Glock
1997:125–6)
(e)  Organic, biological account (Cavell 1979, Hunter, combination of or-
ganic and cultural in Haller 1988 and Simpson 1998, for objections to
Hunter see Garver 1994:241, objections 1–3) TLP 4.002, PI 185, 206,
230 Identity between FOL and biological conditions of human beings (it
must be noted that some authors identify only 4 or even 3 interpretations,
so these mentioned interpretations can be reduced).
4.2. FORMS OF LIFE ARE FORMS OF CULTURE
Without further reference to other interpretations we will attempt to make
understandable the cultural account. It seems that Wittgenstein used a whole
family of mutually similar concepts such as actions, institutions, practices,



Wittgenstein the Morphologist III
131
routines, habits, FOL, and even culture in an everyday sense (meaning arts,
and also skill and technique). Of course, he also used the expression in a more
strict philosophical sense, as for instance in CV.
1.  “Culture is like a great organization which assigns to each of its members
his place, at which he can work in the spirit of the whole, and his strength
can with a certain justice be measured with his success as understood
within the whole.” (CV 8–9, Winch’s translation, the German text is im
Sinne des Ganzen). However we must differ between Wittgenstein’s no-
tions of culture and civilization since contemporary civilization, in Witt-
genstein’s opinion, lacks culture (noted by N. Garver).
Here, it seems that we have “culture” as a whole, as a “background,” and
as something given “which assigns place,” and on the other hand “culture”
that is “constituted” by the success of its members. But, is there any kind of
relevant confirmation of such an idea of culture in Wittgenstein’s writings re-
garding form(s) of life? Now, regarding PI 19, i.e. relation of language-games
with FOL, we must mention that language is:
2.  in BB 134 “equated with culture” (Glock 1998:125),
3.  in RFM 95 equated with the “forms of culture” and in RFM 335–6 with
a “way of living.” So, if language-games are in the same relation to FOL
as well as to culture, forms of culture, and perhaps ‘way of living,’ then
FOLs are forms of culture.
That speaking of language as activity is an important part of cultural activities
seems to be an underlying idea which connects the language-game account
and the cultural account. Consequently, FOL has parts from which it is con-
stituted. These parts are language-games, actions, practices, institutions, etc.
It seems promising to recognize many of his culture-concepts in a way that
they form a bundle of similar notions ordered to create a certain overview /
perspicuous (re)presentation, i.e. from particular and more individual to uni-
versal and more social.
4.  These whatever different FOLs, as Glock suggests (1997:125),
5.  constitute “one form” of life that can be understood as a culture in the sense
previously mentioned (CV 8–9, our suggestion). More to that this “consti-
tuted form” of life (or constructed) is explicated with an amount of related
notions. Constituted “form” of life is at the same time a kind of background
(culture) and a whole of many different given “forms” of life. Many differ-
ent FOLs (actions, practices …) can be understood only against one FOL


132
Chapter Four
as a background or a whole (way of life, stream of life …), but this FOL is
(as “organization”) constituted out of these FOLs (as shown in Table 4.3).
For example, to describe a certain action (one among many FOLs) like order-
ing, obeying, or a child’s learning to brush its teeth, one needs a background
from which the process of teaching and learning (in fact acquiring an FOL), and
maintaining an FOL is possible, and this background is one “FOL” as culture.
Therefore, it seems that a form of life is a culture, i.e. a totality of commu-
nal activities in which, among other activities the language-game is embed-
ded, or we can see it as a kind of background, which has to be accepted; the
given (PI p. 226).
6.  Linguistic and non-linguistic actions are many “forms” of life and these
constitute “form” of life as a culture.
7.  Culture, which is at the same time a background on which these “forms”
of life can be understood (i.e. learned, practiced, changed, and replaced),
cannot be changed and if it can, then only without the possibility of far-
reaching changes in worldview, language, and culture (form of this change
may be suggested in OC 95–9).
Regardless of the change, a certain FOL nevertheless can be steady or not (regu-
lar activity, CE 397). So, routines for instance, as steady and regular actions, and
as institutions, can also be taken to represent the FOL concept. Furthermore,
this idea that the language-game or linguistic activity makes sense only within
the background of an FOL, as the idea that FOL are, as it were, foundations for
language-games, has two interpretations, i.e. transcendental and naturalistic.
Table 4.3. The human form of life and many cultures.



Wittgenstein the Morphologist III
133
 8.  According to the first, “FOLs as communal practices” are precondi-
tions for language-games (see previously from (a) to (e), especially (a)),
“My idea is that this mutual absorption of the natural and the social is a
consequence of Wittgenstein’s envisioning of what we may as well call
the human FOL.” […] “We might perhaps be ready to say that culture
as a whole is the work of our life of language, it goes with language…”
(Cavell 1989:44, 48),
 9.  and, according to the second, a FOL as “inflexible biological human nature”
rigidly determines how we act and react. (Glock 1997:125–6, Simpson
1989, second interpretation supported by PI 415, OC 357–60 commented
and criticized in Baker, Hacker 1995:241, see also previously (e))
Some argue that there is a third interpretation in which “natural” is important,
although not as in naturalistic interpretations, but rather as in the cultural an-
thropological interpretation, like in Garver, and in Baker and Hacker (see (c)):
10.  The basic idea of cultural naturalism seems to be the following: “From
the point of view of natural history, however, there is just one common
FOL for all humans” (natural history as a general fact, Garver 1994:260,
267 and our proposition (7)), (a) Natural is not uniformly biological. (b)
Natural is not necessary. (c) Natural is anthropological. (d) If (a–c), and
if Wittgenstein’s conception of human nature is not biological (or even a
behaviouristic, see Chapter 5), then natural is cultural, and his concept of
FOL is not biological, but cultural. (Baker, Hacker 1995:239–241) Or like
in Glock: “However, Wittgenstein’s naturalism is anthropological rather
than biological. Ordering, questioning … (PI 25). These activities, as well
as those already quoted [RPP I 630], are cultural activities, forms of social
interaction.” (Glock 1997: 126)
So, interpretation (10) is in fact “FOL contextualism” and it seems in this
context to be “culture.” This context of culture applies to linguistic and non-
linguistic activities, practices, routines, customs, and institutions (PI 199,
337; Bloor 1996).
11.  Culture as the background of action, and even more as the surroundings
of actions, gives sense (meaning, background, rough ground) to these
same actions as “ours,” meaning that they are part of our culture (this
is what Cavell means by “everydayness as home,” see also PI 206, Z
567–9); as Baker and Hacker put it: “In short, the natural history of man
is the history of a convention-forming, concept-forming, language-using
animal—a cultural animal.” (Baker, Hacker 1995:240–241)


134
Chapter Four
So there is no paradox (of not knowing the criterion of “cultural” without
knowing an “instance of culture,” and vice versa), rather, when a child learns
a certain practice (certain FOL) it becomes completely familiar with it, or he/
she bumps into the whole of culture (FOL). However, a deeper kind of para-
dox still exists in the grammatical form, and we come up with the solution
which is compatible with Wittgenstein’s ideas (suggested by T. Lampert).
(11a)  The paradox: there cannot be particular FOL without FOL as
a whole, and there cannot be FOL as a whole without at least
one particular FOL (as far as FOLs are essentially composed of
various know-hows, this paradox subsumes under Hegel’s and
Chisholm’s paradox of the criterion of knowledge).
(11b)  Solution: a particular FOL is acquired, e.g. by a child (brushing
teeth + practice brushing teeth) by means of training, particularly
by the way a child is confronted with the whole of the FOL (of
its parents for example) and is forced to construct it, and this
constructed FOL as a whole functions as “rough ground” (sur-
roundings of the each and every particular practice), i.e. it serves
for possible explication or even the interpretation of a particular
FOL on this rough ground of the whole FOL in “very particular
context” (LW I 27). Now, if needed, a child can then explicate
such a practice, or the teacher can explicate it to the child. So, now
we do not just have a particular FOL but also an explication of it.
Such a complex as it were re-constructs the whole of FOL which
now functions as the background not just of this or that particular
FOL, but also of any other particular FOL of this whole FOL, so,
as the reference for comparison of different particular FOLs of this
whole FOL, for introducing new particular FOL, and replacing,
changing, and abandoning useless ones (as shown in Table 4.4).
Accordingly, the paradox is solved by distinction between re-construction—
construction, particular FOL—particular FOL + explication/interpretation of
Table 4.4. A circularity in explicating FOL and a solution.



Wittgenstein the Morphologist III
135
it, and FOL as a whole as the rough ground—as a background. A completely
different question and maybe more interesting one is—are these cultural phe-
nomena in fact FOL phenomena, such as these patterns, forms, and weaves
of life? So, is it possible to interpret these phenomena and concepts, surely
not as metaphysical, but nevertheless ontological? And what kind of ontology
would it be? Would it be a kind of cultural ontology or ontology of culture?
4.3. EXPLICATING FORMS OF CULTURE—IN
“VERY SPECIAL CASES,” “SPECIAL CIRCUMSTANCES”
OR IN “VERY PARTICULAR CONTEXT”
What does it mean that a practice, a routine, or an institution (rule) can be
explicated (interpreted, surveyed, overviewed, etc.) in the “very particular
context” (PI, ix, xi; LW I 27), or in “special circumstances” (PI 118, OC
349, 465, 533)? First, regarding “special circumstances” and “very particular
contexts” one should notice that such context is needed if one tries to expli-
cate certain practices in order to teach someone else something, if a problem
occurs during training, etc. Therefore, if the whole FOL is to be explicated or
overviewed, then sometimes words like “thing,” “cause,” “structure,” “form,”
etc. will occur. Accordingly, the word “world” has its humble use like the
word “table” (PI 97). Or to quote:
•  The occasion determines the usefulness of the signs of pain. The concept
of pain is simply embedded in our life in a certain way. It is characterized
by very definite connections. Just as in chess a move with the king only
takes place within a certain context, and it cannot be removed from this
context.—To the concept there corresponds a technique. (The eye smiles
only within a face.) [Z 532, 533] Only surrounded by certain normal
manifestations of life, is there such a thing as an expression of pain. Only
surrounded by even more far-reaching particular manifestations of life,
such as the expression of sorrow or affection. And so on. [Z 534]” (RPP
II 149–51).
Therefore, we can imagine (construct, constitute, as well as perhaps “invent,”
see previous propositions of the paradox and solution: (11), (11a), (11b)) cer-
tain contexts in which an ontological or a grammatical remark would make
sense, e.g.
• when a child is acquiring a practice,
• when the elderly discuss the change of certain practice,
• or when a replacement of an old practice with the new one is implemented.


136
Chapter Four
Therefore, the sentence “Here is one hand” can in fact be a part of an explica-
tion of a certain practice in very special circumstances, as well as the sentence
“This appears like a wave now, but before it seemed to be a particle,” etc.
Hence, when one explicates a practice (or an event, or a process) for practi-
cal purposes and in special circumstances, or in very particular context, then
what we have is a grammar. Maybe we have to use expressions like “mate-
rial beings” for example, nevertheless, in such circumstances, we often use
expressions like “you know things like cats, chairs, stones and similar.”
Thus, this seems to be a kind of Wittgenstein’s toleration of ontology. On the
other hand he expresses disapproval of ontological investigations, namely as
completely meaningless. If this analysis is correct, we can now distinguish:
•  ontology in the sense in which Wittgenstein refused it altogether (especially
in PI), and
•  ontology in the sense in which Wittgenstein allows it (not as a consequence
of logic like in TLP, but rather as a presupposition of a grammar like in
PI, at least in the manner in which a practice is presupposed by grammar).
Due to convenience sake misunderstandings could take place regarding what
one can call “metaphysics” and “ontology.” Misunderstandings could take
place regarding:
•  the difference between Wittgenstein’s early and late works and their impli-
cations (namely TLP and PI),
•  what we are talking about, i.e. ontology and metaphysics (questions on
God, mind, free will, etc. being metaphysical, while questions regarding
the nature of material beings, causation, time, appearance and reality, etc.
being ontological) and
•  the difference between formal and material ontology (while one could be
convinced that such an explication is material ontology).
When such an explication of certain practice or even of the whole FOL reaches
its maximum, it is in its own turn complete and clear, although for practical pur-
poses, and then it is called “overview” (PI 122, regarding overview see Baker,
Hacker vol. 1 2005:307–34). Accordingly, this kind of explication, as much as
it may be practical and “local” (as opposed to theoretical and global), still has
all features of ontological inquiry. Maybe there is not just a grammar of descrip-
tion of the world, but also a grammar of the world, at least as the grammar of
certain FOL, or of any of its particular practices (for the opposite see Hacker
2007:200). We can imagine special circumstances in which a parent says to its
child “There is no tooth paste on your toothbrush, and there should be if you
want to…” Certainly, there is a grammar of such a description or of an order



Wittgenstein the Morphologist III
137
such as “Now put the toothpaste on your toothbrush.” but we could imagine a
description or an overview of the whole of the practice (or whole FOL) and we
can ask ourselves—what is this description a description of? Since we already
have overview as a description (overview which shows its grammar), we can-
not have a description of a description, or a grammar of a grammar (there is no
meta-grammar no matter if a grammar can have a surface and a kind of depth),
so it must be that an overview is an overview of something else quite different
from it, namely of a practice, i.e. of FOL, or at least of a certain part of it. The
following quotations should help to clear this ‘methodological mess.’ Namely,
Wittgenstein (in PI p. 230, RPP I 46) claims that:
•  “Indeed the correspondence between our grammar and general facts of
nature does concern us.”
•  But we aren’t pursuing “natural science” (possible causes) or “natural his-
tory” (invention of facts).
•  On the other hand we ask ourselves—in which way this correspondence
does concern us, since Wittgenstein writes only on ways in which it doesn’t
concern us? (see RPP I 78, 950)
•  Is this correspondence hidden in some kind of morphology (“new analo-
gies,” RPP I 950) of our concepts of practices, uses, FOL, as well as of
some kind of actions and FOL (linguistic and also non-linguistic), not just
their concepts?
For instance, if some order is de facto obeyed, then—what this obeying is
about? Is it about a certain practice? Is the command “Brush your teeth!”
about teeth, the toothbrush, brushing, or about certain concepts? On the other
hand, if we have a description of a description, or a grammar of a grammar,
then we have the opposite position, i.e. as P. Hacker formulates it, that,
•  “what we clarify in such investigations is not empirical landscapes before
us but our point of view, not what we apprehend but our vision” (Hacker
2007: 200).
•  And that leads him to the position: “Hence, there is no such subject as on-
tology” (Hacker 2007: 200, see also footnote 19. p. 207)
This problem, if it is a problem at all, can be solved if we distinguish between:
•  the appearance-reality issue in Wittgenstein which is finally solved in a
pragmatist manner (OC 422), and maybe even in a constructivist manner
(PI 225, RFM I 4, p.4, OC 95), and objections like Hacker’s, are directed
toward realistic interpretations of Wittgenstein (and he seems to be quite
right on that point),


138
Chapter Four
• and an issue of what an “overview” is about.
In that second sense it seems that a complete and clear overview of anything
is about practice (maybe about events and processes also), or in the end about
the world, and we should not be confused by the fact that such overviews are:
• quite everyday regarding their content,
• are made in particular contexts,
• and for practical purposes,
because this is not what makes them non-ontological, maybe just ontological
in some vague and strange sense. On the other hand, overview is an overview
of use of our words (grammar, PI 122, Baker, Hacker 2005). However, for
which purpose are words used? The only detailed example of an overview
is that of colour concepts from PR and ROC. Why there are no examples
of sounds, music, machines, shapes, or action concepts for instance? Are
such overviews realised completely in engineering, geometry, musicology,
etc.? So much on grammar, but now we must turn to the grammar of culture
which is our primary subject matter and consequently, to summarize and to
apply some of the results—it seems that the grammar of culture is impossible
because cultures are already naturalized, since we talk about cultures as a
cultural anthropology concept.
12.  Parrot Ludwig says—if parrots could talk, then we could not understand
them, but can we understand what Ludwig the parrot just said? (echoing
PI II 223, but also BRB I 64) If we can, then we must disagree with Lud-
wig the parrot, but if we disagree with Ludwig the parrot, then eventually
we must disagree with Ludwig the philosopher as well, because there is
no private language, neither for the philosopher, nor for parrots.
We also agree with the remark opposite of Ludwig the philosopher—can the
coronation of a king be wrong (unsatisfied performative in fact), i.e. corona-
tion as something that might look extremely odd to beings substantially dif-
ferent from us (PI II 226–7).
13.  Of course, one might ask—but wrong and/or odd in which way? Coro-
nation might look wrong or be a “wrong coronation,” “mistaken corona-
tion,” and “made with flaws” only to someone who knows the different
procedures of coronation, but then the question is do “we” also know that
this is then a coronation (this procedure would be similar to the testing
of a preformative regarding its conditions in J. L. Austin’s terms)? A



Wittgenstein the Morphologist III
139
coronation can also look completely odd to someone who does not know
anything about kings, queens, and coronations. We can imagine a culture
in which our coronation is in fact their baptizing, or vice versa.
In the light of these supposedly incommensurable Wittgenstein notes and
conceptions of culture, and contrary to our starting point that the ontology of
culture is impossible, it seems that Wittgenstein’s ideas imply certain ontology
as grammar, namely the grammar of FOL. The question is—in which way?
14.  There is no metaphysical language. That much seems correct at least for
Wittgenstein from PI, and especially as being explicitly critical regard-
ing at least some points from TLP. Nevertheless, ordinary language is an
activity. Ordinary language is also used for describing all kinds of activi-
ties, and moreover we can describe them in the form of rules by means
of many examples. This “rule-form” (of course not like “life-form”) is a
certain pattern and the very pattern of these activities is itself an ontology
of something else, namely of FOL which is in fact its grammar (as shown
in Table 4.5, of course without complicated details like in previous tables).
In short, these rule-forms as patterns of practices (activities, institutions,
etc.) are ontology of FOL. This, of course, serves as a possible answer to the
question—what is ontology? The first obvious answer is—it is a grammar.
This answer raises some problems in the context of the idea of practice. In
order to show this point it seems convenient to formulate it in terms of a rule-
following issue which will be addressed in more detail in the next section.
Table 4.5. An ontology of FOL as a grammar of FOL.


140
Chapter Four
15.  It is impossible even to sketch the rule-following problem here, but only
the fundamental consequence that this problem implies, keeping in mind
what was previously said, that the world-picture can be learned only by
means of practice as a rule-governed activity.
•  “The propositions describing this world-picture might be part of a kind
of mythology. Their role is like that of rules of a game; and the game
can be learned purely practically, without learning any explicit rules”
(OC 95).
Pseudo-propositions of the world-picture can be learned through practice
or FOL (this learning results with “know-how” of FOL). The practice in
the end speaks for itself (OC 139).
Wittgenstein, while being completely anti-metaphysically oriented in PI nev-
ertheless tolerates (as it seems to us) ontology as the grammar of FOL.
16.  FOL has parts, i.e. different actions, practices, institutions, language-
games, etc. These parts are not independent (they create a system). So,
they are not really parts, they are rather something like moments in Hus-
serlian terminology, or like properties of monads in Leibniz’ metaphys-
ics, or like tropes in Campbell’s theory of abstract particulars.
Thereby, considering branches of ontology, the most appropriate one for the
sake of ontology of FOL is the theory of dependence. Setting these lines of
thought aside, it seems vital to emphasize and elucidate this idea of ontology
of FOL.
17.  Ontology is grammar, and surprisingly grammar is ontology. This is not
a simple identity (like in Baker 2004), nor is it a reduction, but “a prag-
matic descent.”
A grammar is expressed via rules, i.e. by any acquired practice which is a
rule-governed activity. What appears to us as reality (things, properties, re-
lations) seems to be nothing more than a “shadow” of a grammar. In other
words, reality, e.g. an essence, is expressed in a grammar and a grammar
determines what kind of object something is (PI 371–3, PG 463–4).
•  Therefore, in a sense FOL is not the ontology of the world, nor is there
some kind of ontology of FOL, nevertheless FOL has parts, structure, etc.
The grammar is expressed in the form of grammatical remarks, e.g. “Green is
a colour.” In the light of Wittgenstein’s claim in favour of the difference be-



Wittgenstein the Morphologist III
141
tween a grammar and the nature we should mention his claim that grammatical
and empirical propositions “merge into one another” (OC 309, for clarification
see Glock 1996:155). If this is correct, then (17) seems to be also correct, but
in which way? Surely, a grammar is a grammar of a certain overview, and an
overview is an overview of certain use of our words. But in order to overview
certain use of our words we must appeal to extra-linguistic actions different
from our use of these words (to previously mentioned “correspondence,” PI p.
230, RPP I 46). Therefore, at the core, every grammar is a grammar of some-
thing non-grammatical, or on the other hand, of actions (described by experi-
ential propositions, for instance “Emma is painting a meadow”). The first part
of (17) is in accord with an anti-metaphysical stance, and the second part goes
along with the recommended interpretation that Wittgenstein introduced certain
ontology of FOL no matter how implicitly. The proposition (17) also states the
obvious, i.e. that rule-forms as certain patterns, and in this sense also the ontol-
ogy of FOL, are expressed by grammatical propositions. Particular practice is
surely expressed by experiential (empirical) propositions, but rule-forms are
expressed by grammatical propositions, or by hinge propositions (like those of
a “world-picture”). A FOL is not arbitrary, just like it is not arbitrary that water
boils at 100 degrees Celsius (despite different circumstances, for example, at
different altitudes on Earth water does not boil precisely at 100 degrees Cel-
sius). But, what is not arbitrary is, in a way, given, and that FOL are given
seems to be not just a grammatical proposition, but a natural fact as well. In
other words, grammar too is not arbitrary. Regarding the difference between
transcendental and naturalistic interpretations (see previously (8) and (9)), there
is another more important difference, namely the one between neo-Kantian “a
priori” of FOL and Hegelian “historicism” of FOL.
18.  The historicism of FOLs is in fact their history in the sense that they
make (natural) history like a life weaves its carpet (see CE 397, Leben-
steppich).
On the other hand, if we have a form, then we should have content also, and
content does not have a history. Every content suffer history and this suffer-
ing is its form. But of course, FOL does not have content like some items in
Aristotelian first philosophy for instance, or like facts of TLP ontology, i.e.
simple objects, substances.
19.  In a FOL there is no Beschreibungen / “descriptions,” because rules are
descriptions of actions, practices / Handlungen (PI 241–2). In FOL there
are no agreements on descriptions because rules belong to a framework
(PI 240).


142
Chapter Four
Wittgenstein’s notion of “agreement” (in PI 241, Übereinstimmung) is in fact
an interlocutor’s notion; rules are descriptions, but there are no descriptions.
•  Ontology is nonsense because it uses grammatical propositions that are
generally nonsensical except in “very particular contexts,” and this very
particular context is the context of explication of an FOL for particular
practical purposes.
One should, at least for the moment, distinguish between a practice and a rule
as a description of practice, as well as between an FOL and the description
of an FOL. How is it possible that the ontology of FOL is not the part of an
FOL? Part of the answer to this question is hidden in the explication of two
basic relations as answers to two following questions, the first echoing PI 23,
and the second PI 107 (see previously propositions (4)–(5)):
•  how different forms of culture “constitute” one form of culture, and
•  in which way a particular form of culture is the background (vertically) and/
or “rough ground” (horizontally, Cavell) of these many forms of culture?
Many forms of culture do not “constitute” one form of culture; in fact they can-
not constitute one form of culture, since one form of culture is a whole, gram-
matically speaking. This point could be illustrated in the following manner.
•  Bricks cannot, in a sense, constitute a house as a whole, although the house
is “constituted” of bricks. This seems to us to be applicable under “con-
stitution fallacy.” If a house is constituted as a whole made out of bricks,
windows, doors, etc. this does not imply that everything that was needed
during the process of its constitution must also be present as “a proper part”
of it when it is constituted. Of course, first we think of hammers, shovels,
etc. as parts that are needed for its constitution, but which are nevertheless
not present in the constituted house (except by the mistake, say a shovel
left in cement foundations). However, as these are necessary for a house,
so are bricks and windows, since we experience the house as a whole. For
example, when we gaze at old houses and admire them, we often forget
that once they were brand new, and probably that most of them were quite
ugly. In fact, we admire ruins, certain form without content or function (of
course, we do not want to imply a certain aesthetic attitude here).
20.  In the same way, many forms of culture (as being many different prac-
tices, routines, etc.) are necessary for a process of the “constitution”
of one culture, but when a culture is constituted these parts are not its
proper parts, since an FOL is a whole. On the other hand we “explicate”



Wittgenstein the Morphologist III
143
our experience of a culture as whole in terms of its parts as proper parts.
Accordingly, this is the answer to the first question.
This leads us to the previously mentioned “very particular context” in which
we in fact, as well with very precise purpose, explicate a culture in terms of
it parts. When a new member is introduced in a culture he/she is introduced
via learning the particular practices, any of which will do as an example of
this context. Another typical case is a change of a certain practice due to new
circumstances (inconveniences, discoveries, inventions, interpretations, and
re-descriptions, most probably Rorty-style). Sometimes these activities are
not defined in advance rather they must be invented. Sometimes certain pro-
cedures, practices, or routines must be completely explicated and changed,
and this leads us to the answer to the second question.
21.  Culture as a whole is manifested and properly represented by any of its
particular practices. Hence, if a certain practice is going to be changed, then
the whole culture will change its shape/form (the WP will change and its
language-games as well). What is explicated here is in fact the “core” of
culture, i.e. its feature of being the background of understanding of any of its
particular practice, or being “a rough ground” (naturalism), being practice as
it is usually done. Consequently this is the answer to our second question.
The difference is between culture being a background of understanding (not
of “explaining,” but of “interpreting”) of a certain particular practice, and
culture being a “rough ground,” i.e. being present, existent, living in its own
process in any particular practice as it is de facto practiced. An additional
question should be raised as well, namely—what is the relation between
grammar and naturalism? On an occasion such as this it can be mentioned
that naturalism in Wittgenstein’s use of the word (like in “natural history,” or
like in “natural science”) is connected primarily with nature, which seems to
be in the middle (like a third way) between extremes;
•  nature (natura) as essence (essentia) on one side (which is manifested in
grammar),
•  and nature as natural science (scientia) on the other (which should not be
of our interest as philosophers, at least as the prime subject matter here, not
because we should dislike science, rather because we do not have much use
of it in grammatical investigations).
But these are just presupposed (constructed as it were) in a certain viewpoint,
since an essence is given in any practice and the science is something that we
must get rid of or be freed from. Therefore, Wittgenstein’s notion of nature


144
Chapter Four
as “a natural history,” in some places seems to be in the middle between an
essence as metaphysical nature and a nature as the object of scientific data,
or aboutness of scientific (empirical) propositions. The following series of
quotations should suitably illustrate this interpretation.
•  “Commanding, questioning, storytelling, chatting, are as much a part of
our natural history as walking, eating, drinking, [and] playing.” (PI 25, see
also 415).
•  “A natural history of colours would have to report on their occurrence in
nature, not on their essence. Its propositions would have to be temporal
ones.” (ROC III 135)
•  “Indeed the correspondence between our grammar and general (seldom
mentioned) facts of nature does concern us. But our interest does not fall
back on these possible causes. We are not pursuing a natural science; our
aim in not to predict anything. Not natural history either, for we invent facts
of natural history for our own purposes.” (RPP I 46, PI p. 230, in connec-
tion with commentary on proposition (10))
Wittgenstein is more or less against both extreme understandings, against essen-
tialism as well as against philosophical “scientific” naturalism. A further issue
is the so-called rule-following problem. The description of any rule is not a part
of the rule-following activity. We do not know meanings or practices, but we
apply words, rules, and do things correctly or not. But, how do we know, or what
does it mean here “correctly”? Of course, the bottom line regarding knowledge
of a certain practice is that “to proceed correctly” means “to know-how,” or “to
have mastery of technique” (PI 150). To conclude, we have two concepts of the
world; one which is presupposed (like a kind of regulative or historical idea) by
our experiences and practices, and the other one, which is constructed out of
them for practical purposes such as learning, acquiring and even changing the
world-picture, and both of them are pragmatic concepts of the world.
4.4. ANTI-KRIPKENSTEINIAN READING OF THE
RULE-FOLLOWING ISSUE
What is crucial in Wittgenstein’s account of certainty is certitude of practices,
habits, customs, traditions, etc. (see Chapters 6, and 7). There are all kinds of
activities (like speaking a language, playing chess, building a house, standing
in front of a tree, drawing a duck-rabbit head, etc.) not just linguistic ones,
although:
•  “The rule-governed nature of our languages permeates our life.” (ROC III
303).



Wittgenstein the Morphologist III
145
After PI Wittgenstein scholars thought that only language is a rule-guided
activity, but after some other works like OC, CV, etc. as it seems we have
been confronted with the idea that there are many non-linguistic activities
which are also rule-guided (acquiring knowledge, being religious, moral, and
all everyday practices, see Stroll 2002, on the issue of religious belief see
Chapter 8). In Wittgenstein’s opinion there is no analytic definition of a rule
since the concept of a rule is a family-resemblance concept and as such is best
explained by reference to examples. Rules are:
• standards of correctness,
• there is difference between rule and its expression in a language,
•  they are general (unlike commands or orders) and they govern a multiplic-
ity of occasions,
•  rule following is an achievement-verb and it means to actually follow a
rule, not just to believe that one is following a rule, and
•  there is tremendous difference between following a rule and merely acting in
accordance with a rule, because it must be intentional, meaning that the rule
that is followed must be a part of one’s reason for doing something, not just a
cause for doing something, thus, one must intend to follow a rule (PI 207–8,
222, 232, however, the intention and the reason of an action can be completely
implicit in, and manifested by an action, see Anscombe 1957, Searle 1984).
Such reasons at the core depend on the world-picture, which contains gram-
matical and/or hinge/axis remarks, which are in virtue of being these kinds of
propositions always more certain than any reason for any action whatsoever.
However, this does not have to be explicit or prior to every action of follow-
ing a certain rule, it can be, and in fact it is, “intention in action” (Searle 1991,
perhaps a similar idea is formulated in Anscombe 2000). Therefore, we have
something like “rule-following by default,” but this is nonsense since what we
have is merely “practice X being done by default.” Rule following is merely
acting in a certain way like through customs, habits, and institutions (PI 199).
Thus, rule following is possible only within a social community, but not pri-
vately (PI 202). This point is important because the world-picture is acquired by
learning certain fundamental practices, language-games, customs, habits, and
this kind of learning is to all intents and purposes social. Of course, there is a
possibility for a solitary person to follow a rule, or to invent a rule, but this is
a possibility only because the solitary human in question was probably previ-
ously a member of a community (not necessarily a human community). After
all, Wittgenstein explicitly says that rule following activities require the context
of a social and historical way of living (PI 495, Glock 1997). There are however
two different problems about the notion of rule-following:
• understanding of rules, and
• normativity of rules.


146
Chapter Four
Because Wittgenstein in fact rejected several solutions to these problems
(mechanism, Platonism, mentalism, and hermeneutics), some commentators
thought that he adopted rule-scepticism (most notably S. Kripke). The prob-
lem is regarding the obvious gap between the rule, and its application. The
gap is situated in the space of a certain interpretation of the rule (as shown
in Table 4.6).
This problem was detected by Wittgenstein in PI (PI 186, 198). He also
made a distinction between an interpretation of a rule and an understanding
of it. Understanding is exhibited in categories of: obeying the rule and going
against it (PI 201). Therefore, the question is—how does a rule determine its
application? Kripke saw the paradox of rule-following in PI 201:
22.  “This was our paradox: no course of action could be determined by a
rule, because every course of action can be made out to accord with the
rule. The answer was: if everything can be made out to accord with the
rule, then in can be made out to conflict with it. And so there could be
neither accord nor conflict here” (PI 201).
Kripke formulated the problem very straightforwardly. The problem with rule
following is that the grasp of a rule is supposed to constrain one’s use norma-
tively (but not causally). Accordingly, the grasp of a rule cannot be equated
with one’s (fallible) application of it. The temptation is to think of the rule as
distinct from its application, and here is the point of sceptical argument. Then
we would need understanding (interpretation) to mediate rules and their ap-
plications. On one hand, we have the gap between rules and their application,
and on the other, we have understanding, which is self-contradictory because
we then have to invent meta-language or private language to be capable of
even learning natural language, or it is done ad infinitum. The possible solu-
tion would be to introduce an understanding as a part of the application of a
rule, just as intention is a part of action (intention in action). To apply a rule
then would mean to perform certain rule-governed actions and understand-
ings of it solely by their performance. In contrast with the previous scheme of
the problem, we can now see the difference and the possible way of escaping
(bypassing it in fact) the gap between rules and their applications (as shown
in Table 4.7).
Table 4.6. Rule following issue.



Wittgenstein the Morphologist III
147
Of course, the possibility of mistake still exists, but when a human S makes
a mistake, it simply means that S is doing something wrong, and that S must
correct his/her performance, and when S applies the rule correctly, it means
that S understands the rule and that his/her understanding is a part of his/her
application of the rule. The notion of grasping a rule and not the notion of
possession of an interpretation of the rule (one could be in fact “bewitched”
by an interpretation) makes us capable to remove the last border on the tabu-
lation (the left one in Table 4.7, as show in Table 4.8). Thus,
• To grasp a rule means to
○  understand a rule, and to understand a rule means to
○  be capable of applying a rule and that means to
○  follow a rule in practice (as habit, custom, and institution, as shown in
Table 4.8).
Thus, in practice, there is no difference between grasping a rule, understand-
ing a rule, being capable of applying a rule, and following a rule via certain
practice. However, certain practices raise the problem of the difference be-
tween understanding of, applying, and following a rule, namely practices of
teaching or training, but these are “exceptions to the rule.”
Now, where in this whole story is the notion of correctness of the applica-
tion of a rule, because that is the whole meaning of the rule-following notion?
Imagine that we have a chessboard. If we want to teach a child how to play
chess, then we would start by showing examples of correct movements of
some pieces, the queen for instance. After every example the child would try
to repeat these moves. Chess piece after chess piece the child learns how to
move the pieces correctly. After that we can play only with some of the pieces
… and after some time with the whole set. Therefore, when a child knows
how to play a game of chess, the child knows all of these things about chess
Table 4.7. Understanding and application of a rule.
Table 4.8. A rule, its understanding, and application is one and the same thing.


148
Chapter Four
pieces, their moves, captures and calling check and checkmate. However,
children are maybe incapable of describing rules so systematically and with
precision or even at all. What the child really knows is “how” to play a game
of chess and that means that the child can move pieces correctly, employ
some tactics, or even strategy. What it knows is a practice. For the child who
is learning how to play chess:
•  to know the rule of, for example, movements of pawns and other chessmen,
means
•  to follow the rule and that means to obey the rule blindly or by default (PI
219), which in turn means,
• that a child “can” play chess.
Accordingly, to learn how to play chess means to learn the pieces and their
moves by example and that means to acquire the ability to use pieces cor-
rectly. Learning depends on the persistence of a practice. Wittgenstein used
the notion of practice to reject the previously stated dilemma altogether by
rejecting the premise that generates it:
23.  the premise is—that understanding consists in the grasp of an interpreta-
tion and herein lies the point of slight difference between Kripke and
others (McDowell for instance).
We can see the previous solution as good one if we consider what was said
regarding rules in OC. It is clear that we can be mistaken in the application of
a rule (OC 26). However, infallibility is not the question of knowing the rule
(of pawn moves for example), rather of knowing the pawn moves by de facto
practice of moves of the pawns. The essence of pawn moves we have learned
by practice of playing chess (OC 44–5). The crucial connection between
rules (world-picture) and practice (empirical propositions) is in sections 95–9
of OC. The connection between rules, practices (as application of rules) and
world-picture is the following:
24.  “The propositions describing this world-picture might be part of a kind of
mythology. And their role is like that of rules of a game; and the game can
be learned purely practically, without learning any explicit rules.” (OC 95)
Hinge-propositions of the world-picture can be learned by practice of an FOL.
As the understanding of a rule is given in practice of its following, in the same
manner the knowledge of a world-picture is implicit in practice of its learning.
25.  “It might be imagined that some propositions, of the form of empirical
propositions, were hardened and functioned as channels for such em-



Wittgenstein the Morphologist III
149
pirical propositions as were not hardened but fluid; and that this relation
altered with time, in that fluid propositions hardened, and hard ones
become fluid.” (OC 96)
Hinge-propositions are principles of judging, principles of action, etc. How-
ever, some are more fundamental than others.
26.  “The mythology may change back into a state of flux; the river-bed of
thoughts may shift. But I distinguish between the movement of the wa-
ters on the river-bed and the shift of the bed itself; though there is not a
sharp division of the one from another.” (OC 97)
There is no strict distinction between “world-picture” propositions, practice
and FOL (this is the famous river-image in literature which could be com-
pared with the most famous Heraclitus one, no matter if Wittgenstein was
indeed claiming that Heraclitus claimed obvious nonsense, since one really
can step in the same river twice).
27.  “And the bank of the river consists partly of hard rock, subject to no al-
teration or only to an imperceptible one, partly of sand, which now in one
place now in another gets washed away, or deposited.” (OC 99)
Thus, rules are incorporated in their applications as well as their understand-
ing, and there is no problem of interpretation. However, from the point of
view of significance of empirical propositions of the world-picture the ques-
tion is—how a rule and an empirical proposition merge into one another (OC
309), how they change? When they change, there is a space for “interpreta-
tion” or grammar in such “very limited context.” The answer is that it is the
core of practice—that it masks the distinction between rules and empirical
propositions (OC 319). In a situation when a problem with a certain practice
occurs, the practice must be stopped and the rule must be, and indeed it is,
interpreted differently. What is explicated on an occasion such as this is in
fact ontology of FOL which is its grammar but again only for practical pur-
poses, i.e. with the aim of continuation of practice. This becomes obvious in
light of some sections of OC:
•  “… we did not learn this through a rule, but by learning to calculate,”
•  “But the most important thing is: The rule is not needed. Nothing is lacking,”
•  “… even when the calculation is something fixed for me, this is only a deci-
sion for a practical purpose [praktischen Zweck]”
•  “Not only rules, but also examples are needed for establishing a practice.
Our rules leave loop-holes open, and the practice has to speak for itself.”
(OC 43–9, 139)


Part Two
THREE APPLICATIONS—
CONSCIOUSNESS, CERTAINTY,
AND RELIGION


153
Chapter Five
The Nature of Consciousness:
Living human beings
(with Josip Lukin)
When philosophizing you have to descend into the old chaos and feel at
home there. (CV 74)
5.1. INTRODUCTION: CARTESIAN
PICTURE OF CONSCIOUSNESS
There are many important sides of Cartesian dualism and among them is the
epistemological perspective, which says that there is a faculty of introspec-
tion by which human beings, as conscious beings can know the contents
of their own consciousness. At this point one should differ between being
conscious of a state of affairs, say (or that one sees) “that A. Augustine’s
