completely irrelevant. If someone cannot accept such reasons, as Stroud



Anti-Sceptical Weapons I
185
cannot, then it is understandable that he tries to ask inappropriate questions
about, for example,
• Austin’s procedures which are in fact Wittgenstein’s practices, and about
•  Austin’s recognised ways, which are in fact Wittgenstein’s forms of life,
(Austin 1961:55–7, Stroud 1984: 46–7; I suppose that a lot can be said
if one compares treatment of certainty of supposedly “empirical proposi-
tions” in Austin’s works and in OC).
Here one can recall P. Unger’s distinction between absolute terms and
relative terms and the theory of relevant alternatives. Generally speaking,
if someone approaches an interpretation of the sceptical question form the
point of view of absolute terms, he will surely end in the Cartesian problem
(with The Great Gap). But if that someone approaches an interpretation of
the sceptical question from the point of view of relative terms, he will have
to presuppose a certain background of meaning of the terms in question, and
of the relevant parameters of the epistemic situation (and of course, relevant
alternatives like the “cleverly disguised mule” in F. Dretske’s famous Zebra
case). Now, I cannot see why the latter approach is in any way worse than the
former. Acknowledgement of the relevance of the context of an epistemic sit-
uation is very important. The revival of arguments from ordinary knowledge
claims from the 50’s and 60’s (in Austin, Wittgenstein, Grice, and Strawson
as heirs of Moore, Newman, and Reid) is in fact a contemporary contextual-
ism (Stine, Cohen, see Sosa 1990:171–82). It is not completely clear what
Wittgenstein’s approach to scepticism was. Of course, on the one hand his ap-
proach was similar to that of other philosophers of ordinary language. Some
even suggested that he used the transcendental argument against scepticism
(Strawson for instance).
Let us go back to Wittgenstein’s solutions now that we can see their
context. His approach in OC is similar to the one to scepticism in general in
TLP. The point is that the sceptic’s challenge—how do you know that you
are not dreaming right now? or I may be dreaming as Wittgenstein stated
it, cannot be sensibly stated and therefore there is nothing to refute (Z 369,
OC 350, 676). In Putnam’s words (from the argument that uses the same-
ness of reference for brains in a vat and thoughts of someone in the actual
world, 1981:13–4), if this is only a dream, then This is only a dream is false.
In addition, it is false, because if this is just a dream, then there is no real-
ity, and if it is so, there cannot be language entry rules and language exit
rules. However, there are some attempts of reconstruction of Wittgenstein’s
objection to the Dream Hypothesis (Stroll 2002:145). From this argument,


186
Chapter Six
Wittgenstein rejects not only the sceptical hypothesis, but Moore’s proof of
an external world as well.
6.3.1. “Calm” certainty, and the one that “still struggles” (OC 357)
The last interesting detail is Wittgenstein’s insistence on the psychological
dimension of certainty.
•  In virtue of that he speaks about calm certainty and certainty that still
struggles.
This dimension is not about evidence or practice, but indeed about our ap-
proach to the whole of the world-picture. After all, this view of certainty was
of almost no interest to contemporary philosophers. Wittgenstein suggested
that this dimension is very important thus, we must think about the possibility
of connection of this insight with the psychology of attitude change (what one
could have in mind are theories about rationalisations, changing of cognitive
parts of attitude, etc.), because it is important to understand the changes in the
world-picture. However, there is no strict argument from this dimension, even
though Wittgenstein sometimes suggests that it is also psychologically impos-
sible to doubt the certainties of the world-picture (cats like milk) and even
some propositions that are obviously incompatible with the world-picture (for
instance, that cars don’t grow from the ground). A similar objection, namely that
it is psychologically impossible to believe, and if one believes, then one is surely
insincere, was used as a counter to solipsism by B. Russell in his book “Human
Knowledge. Its Scope and Limits” (and in some other works of his as well).
After all, psychologically speaking, this is important for explication of the
phenomenon of being wonder-struck in situations in which the world-picture
is even slightly changed. How did men react when they discovered fire, when
they invented the wheel, when they saw the New World, when they realised
that it is possible to change the social and political order, when the first men
landed on the Moon, etc.? It is miraculous and frightening to see that the
picture of the world is changing and that nothing will be the same again. This
point should be connected with Wittgenstein’s idea of morphology as some
kind of “conversion” to the new world-view. In Wittgenstein’s metaphor, it
is like a river that is changing its route and changing the riverbed (which can
be taken as our common place, our everydayness as home). What is changed
or moved are not the parts, but rather the whole world-picture, the whole way
of life. It is one thing to explain the change of the world-picture and corre-
sponding practices, and a rather different thing to justify universal doubt in
its certainties when there is no special reason to do so.



Anti-Sceptical Weapons I
187
6.4. CONCLUSION: THE CONNECTIONS BETWEEN
RULES, PRACTICES (AS APPLICATIONS OF RULES) AND
CERTAINTIES OF A WORLD-PICTURE (CWP)
Therefore, we have shown:
•  the core of Wittgenstein’s pragmatic position from OC which is the practice
itself and certainties implicit to it (first part),
•  what kind this analysis of certainty is as (connective) analysis of “knowl-
edge” (of everyday hinge-propositions, second part),
• and how he refuted scepticism on the basis of this position (third part).
But, there are some more general and far-reaching consequences of this posi-
tion, not just for interpretation of his philosophy, but as well for interpretation
of some of the central questions of philosophy. There are some lessons from
OC on the so-called rule-following problem. These consequences are mainly
ontological. This means that they at the same time answer the question of
learning the WP, and the question of rule following. It is impossible even to
sketch the rule-following problem here, but the fundamental consequence of
all things previously said for this problem is that the WP can be learned only
by means of practice as a rule-governed activity. “The propositions describ-
ing this world-picture might be part of a kind of mythology. And their role is
like that of rules of a game; and the game can be learned purely practically,
without learning any explicit rules.” (OC 95) Pseudo-propositions of the WP
can be learned through practice in forms of life. As the understanding of a
rule is acquired by practice of its following, the knowledge of a world-picture
is in the same manner implicit in the practice of its learning. “It might be
imagined that some propositions, of the form of empirical propositions, were
hardened and functioned as channels for such empirical propositions as were
not hardened but fluid; and that this relation altered with time, in that fluid
propositions hardened, and hard ones become fluid.” (OC 96) Pseudo-propo-
sitions are principles of judging, principles of action, etc. However, some are
more fundamental than others. “The mythology may change back into a state
of flux; the river-bed of thoughts may shift. But I distinguish between the
movement of the waters on the river-bed and the shift of the bed itself; though
there is not a sharp division of the one from another.” (OC 97) There is no
strict distinction between the CWP, practice and the forms of life. “And the
bank of the river consists partly of hard rock, subject to no alteration or only
to an imperceptible one, partly of sand, which now in one place now in an-
other gets washed away, or deposited.” (OC 99) Thus, rules are incorporated
in their applications as well as their understanding, so there is no problem of


188
Chapter Six
interpretation or understanding of a rule or rule-following problem. However,
from the point of view of significance of empirical propositions of the world-
picture, the question is how the rule and the empirical proposition merge into
one another (OC 309). The answer is that it is the core of practice ― that
practice masks the distinction between rules and empirical propositions (OC
319). In the end, the epistemology is only a kind of overview of our everyday
practice. Moreover, this, in light of the following quotation from W. James,
can lead us to some more practical dimensions of philosophy. “The pragmatic
method in such cases is to try to interpret each notion by tracing its respective
practical consequences. Mr. Peirce, after pointing out that our beliefs are re-
ally rules for action, said that, to develop a thoughts meaning, we need only
determine what conduct it is fitted to produce, that conduct is for us its sole
significance.” (James 1998:28–9)
A world-picture is the starting point of all our beliefs (OC 208), a lot
of them in fact, and it is a system of beliefs (relations) consisting of many
“quasi-beliefs” that are “removed from the traffic” and “shunted onto an
unused siding” (OC 210), for instance that Earth exists, that New York ex-
ists, my friend there, his young trees, their buds of such and such a kind, a
telephone conversation with my friend, etc. (OC 207–9). Any such system is
the “background” and the “rough ground” of all our practices, for instance
the fact that “I have a telephone conversation with New York.” (OC 208)
There are at least three kinds of propositions mentioned in OC, namely,
experiential or empirical, grammatical, and “hinges,” sometimes referred to
as “hinge-propositions.” However, one could doubt whether they are really
propositions, but even previous to that, what is their justification. Take for
instance the hinge (a) “Cats do not grow on trees” (OC 282). The question
is how do you know the proposition (a)? Wittgenstein thinks that you do not
know it; however that it is almost absolutely certain that (a) is true. How is
this possible?
Take for instance the following empirical propositions: (b) “Felix very
much likes to sleep on ‘his’ tree.,” (c) “Felix is very stubborn cat, and very
rarely leaves ‘his’ tree.” Propositions (a), (b), and (c) are not inconsistent.
However, suppose that there is a proposition (d), which says, “Cats grow
on trees” (one can imagine a child drawing such a conclusion from (b) and
(c) based on simple induction from his/her previous experiences). Now,
propositions (b), (c), and (d) are not inconsistent as well. However, (a) and
(d) are inconsistent. In order to decide on this matter one needs to appeal to
the grammar of the words “cat” and “tree,” through something like in the
following proposition, (e) “Cats are animals, not plants.” Now, in light of (e)
or a series of similar propositions regarding the grammar of the words “cats”
and “trees,” one can decide in favour of (a) rather than in favour of (d), not



Anti-Sceptical Weapons I
189
because of particular evidence, but because (a), (b), (c), and (e) create a sys-
tem of beliefs. Therefore, the solution is obvious, namely, (a) fits within the
system, while (d) does not. The possibility that (d) is true exists, and that the
whole system is wrong as well. Nevertheless, if (d) is true, one need to change
the system a little bit, but if the whole system is wrong, then the whole world-
picture needs to be changed. More to that, a particular doubt in favour of (d) is
possible by no means, but it goes against the whole system, and does not exist
on the background of a different (its own particular) system, it is lonesome in
a way. However, this issue deserves a whole chapter.
A doubt regarding the whole system is also possible, but it is completely
unreasonable to one who is within the system. One who doubts in the whole
system needs to point to a different system in order to point to a particular rea-
son for such doubt from such a particular “background” and “rough ground”
(this kind of explication stands grammatically speaking, but not logically,
and this point could be mentioned if one wants to know the difference be-
tween logic and philosophical grammar). Regarding relations of these types
of propositions, one could say that they create a system. Imagine a monolith
(almost like the one from the movie), a source of light (without substantive
aberration), and a shadow (with different almost completely dark shades, as
show in Figure 6.1).
Hinge-propositions are in the shadow of the monolith, as it were, “shunted
onto an unused siding.” However, one can “know” such propositions on the
“rough ground” of empirical propositions, and on the “background” of gram-
matical propositions. Taken together they create a system of beliefs, a system
of relations (scaffolding, skeleton, and framework, OC 211). The system
Figure 6.1. The relation between empirical, grammatical, and hinge propositions.


190
Chapter Six
itself casts shadow differently on different types of hinge-propositions (see
Chapter 3, Section 3.6, and Glock 1996).
However, in OC sections containing references to hinges, explicit or
implicit, and sections referring to particular hinge-propositions or grammati-
cal propositions as certainties of the world-picture (CWP), are also closely
connected to sections referring to an action, behaviour, way of acting, and
rules. Therefore, hinges should be regarded less as propositions, and more as
rules for actions, or even parts of descriptions of cases of paradigm actions.
Namely, “we” act in accordance with (a), not in accordance with (d), more
to that, everything we “do” manifests that we “believe” in (a), not in (d). Our
way of life manifests our world-picture (OC 229, 395), and while it has no
further justification, way of life, “condensed” in any of its particular practice
justifies the whole world-picture.
6.5. ANNEX: WITTGENSTEIN’S PRAGMATISM AND
PRACTICAL PROPER BASIC BELIEFS
The philosopher is not a citizen of any community of ideas. That is what makes
him into a philosopher. (CV 48)
Wittgenstein indeed upheld a certain kind of pragmatism regarding the is-
sue of human knowledge, in fact certainty (probably because certainty has
levels, while knowledge does not) in a way that it is human action. This kind
of pragmatism has its (in)direct consequences on understanding of practice
and practical philosophy, and among the dimensions of practical philosophy,
on understanding social philosophy (perhaps a little less understanding of
ethical or political philosophy due to Wittgenstein’s diverse and developing
standpoint vis-à-vis ethics). In this annex, I will try to argue that it is pos-
sible to change our access to and understanding of society, and to do that
based on Wittgenstein’s pragmatism (especially from OC), and a certain
common sense understanding of the nature of practical beliefs. In other
words, it seems possible and fruitful to investigate a new way of approach to
social philosophy from the Wittgensteinian point of view, especially in light
of questions like: what are the rational commitments regarding our social
values? The task is to examine ways in which various social agents ought
to act. If we understand that a society should indeed have some political in-
stitutions (at least some kind of common practices), then such agents should
not act so as to undermine them. However, the question implicit to this one
is—What kind of social institutions do we ought to have? The last question is
not normative, but primarily it is the question of criteria and system (to have



Anti-Sceptical Weapons I
191
for instance a voting system, particular parliamentary system, an electoral
procedure and system, etc.) and its implicit normativity (as it were the norm
of a procedure). These institutions are major legal, economic, and cultural
institutions. To elaborate this idea from the Wittgensteinian point of view
seems almost like a kind of pragmatist project. The most important dream is
not that it is impossible to create practical and political philosophy as well
as a rational reflection or something similar of our practices, rather that it is
possible to create it (partially explicate it, or interpret it, an partially invent
it) from our common practices as the starting point and its final measure in
terms of its procedural nature, consequences, and values (a spirit) in light of
which routine procedures are executed.
6.5.1. The primacy of the world picture (CWP) as the
primacy of practice (P)
Perceptions, memories, and self-knowledge are believed to be fundaments
in our noetic structure / capacities (at least by some epistemologies), in the
same way habits, customs, and institutions are fundaments in our sphere of
actions and practice (at least by some philosophies of social action). How-
ever, there are other capabilities in our noetic structure such as the possibility
of comparison, connective analysis, conceptual understanding, argumenta-
tion, reasoning, etc. In the same way there are capabilities in the sphere of
our practical life such as the ability of practical conceptual understanding,
practical reasoning, reaching decisions, planning future actions, acting in ac-
cordance with decisions that have been reached, critical-creative thinking in
moments when problems emerge, etc.
It is known from human common experience that the majority of our
decisions, actions, and general everyday practices are determined by the
habits and customs that we have learned from our childhood onward. (Thus,
upbringing and education are of the utmost importance here as well.) Most
of these habits and customs are directed toward our socialisation as a form
of an easier way of survival. We follow them without questioning as well as
we follow any other rule of practice, in fact, (proto)typical case of an action.
What we follow are in fact paradigm cases of routines which do not exist,
really they are just a series of similarities between many cases which we con-
sider to belong to the same type of practice, say preparing food (most have
developed a net of such similarities, since comparing nets of similarities and
dissimilarities is what morphological procedure is all about). Therefore, our
everyday life is in major part controlled by our way of acting as a part of a
certain WP. This WP manifests itself in our ways of acting, in our everyday
practices. This emphasis is very important because it gives absolute practical


192
Chapter Six
certainty to our practical (connective, morphological) analysis and practical
reasoning, especially to the first premises of our practical reasoning (prin-
cipally deductive and that in the most intuitive forms of deduction like for
instance If P, than Q. P. Therefore, Q.). Consequently, human decisions and
actions are determined and controlled for the most part by habits or customs,
or by practical reasoning that is based on habits or customs (on this point
Wittgenstein seems to be quite close to some pragmatists like Dewey for
instance, and I suppose their methods (perhaps practices as well) of teaching
could be compared which could turn out quite interesting).
Surely, exciting questions can pop up concerning the morality of certain
habit or a custom, i.e. a particular custom can become an issue under certain
circumstances, but their primary role is to provide us with the means for mere
living (possibly just survival). Here, one should have in mind some universal
and most common habits almost as preconditions of human action, such as:
critical and creative thinking, coping with emotions, self -knowledge (in a
moral sense), decision-making, and careful, wise, and reasonable acting, etc.
We can presuppose here that we act in two fundamentally different ways in
the virtue of the decision-making process or the activation of certain readi-
ness for action if the appropriate circumstances occur.
• There are decisions previous to action
•  and decisions implicit in action. This is an application of J. R. Searle’s
distinction between intentions (Searle 1991, similar to be found in G. E.
M. Anscombe 1957, §:1).
Of course, decisions implicit in action cannot be results of reasoning because
of the nature of an action and that means that they can be only directly based
on fully adopted habit. On the other hand, a decision previous to an action
can be either based directly on fully adopted habit, or indirectly based on
adopted habit, which means to be inferred by means of practical reasoning.
This second type of decision reaching procedure is often regarded as a sign of
rationality not just of our actions, but of our systems of actions as well, such
as a system of legal or political actions for instance.
6.5.2. Practical reasoning based on the a world picture (WP)
Practical reasoning is reasoning that is directed toward the control of deci-
sions and actions and as such it is contrasted to theoretical reasoning that is
directed to the truth or to the inference of true propositions. In other words,
practical reasoning is directed to what can be done with how things are, but
not just with how they are. However, there is no sharp difference between the
theory and the practice in any given practical case.



Anti-Sceptical Weapons I
193
It is sometimes claimed that our practical reasoning depends on our theo-
retical reasoning in way that we cannot know what to do about something
if we do not know what it is, or for example how it functions. This is obvi-
ously wrong (because there is no strict logical or grammatical dependence of
know-how on know-that, see Ryle 2009, and Chapter 8). Besides, people can
often master various techniques and skills without any (previous) knowledge
regarding their body or the machines that they use. Consequently, this is
maybe true in some cases, but it is surely understood in a wrong way. It is so
because theoretical reasoning is not the basis of practical reasoning. The basis
of practical reasoning is practice itself (a fully adopted habit or custom). For
instance, a child learns to brush its teeth properly by doing so on daily basis
under parental supervision. Moreover, there is no need for knowledge regard-
ing practical reasoning, or even absolute certainty, but only “a certainty for
practical purposes.” One should think of a first premise of theoretical deduc-
tive reasoning to be true and to be known in the light of satisfying proof or at
least valid argument conditions. A first premise of a practical deduction must
be a part of our practice and that means a part of our FOL, of our worldview
and that is exclusive condition for its justification (Reid, Moore, Chisholm,
and Wittgenstein seem to be sharing similar standpoints regarding this issue).
It is in a way “explicated” from a practice. Consequently, “evidentialism” of
any kind is inappropriate to call upon in the field of thinking about practices
of humans. On the other hand, “evidence” is of utmost importance. The
first premise of practical deduction is just the shortened (more or less clear)
description of our standard procedure or routine practice under everyday cir-
cumstances (see a1, b1, and especially c1 in section 6.5.3.).
Accordingly, in the field of our practice and practical reasoning, our prag-
matic arguments can supplement epistemic reasons in determining whether
it is rational to believe a proposition or not (for a general form of such an
argument see Chapter 8, section 8.3). The idea is based upon the distinction
between:
• a P being rational to believe, and
• believing P being the rational thing to do (Nozick 1993).
•  This distinction says that some P may not be rationally believable, but that
it could be the case that believing that P might be a rational thing to do
(Nozick 1993:64–93), and in addition, believing P is a part of action, not
of thinking or believing.
Prudential rationality or reasonableness is in this way independent from an
epistemic or pure rationality (Sibley 1953). The source of reasonableness is
fundamentally our practice, our personal and communal (social) experience,
our FOL. By this I am suggesting that our practical reasoning is not restricted


194
Chapter Six
just to deductive inference, or that it can also be (in some cases) inductive
inference (Millgram 1997). By this, I am also suggesting at least two things
that can be of some interest to the topic. First, that our practice is quite re-
mote from our theory (for instance, our epistemology from our science, and
our science from its technological and industrial applications, our appliances
in fact), and that it is partly independent as well, and second, that our think-
ing about practice is independent from our thinking about theory even if the
theory is a theory of practice. By this, I am not suggesting that our practical
reasoning is independent from our descriptions of our practices since they are
indeed closely connected, rather that it is independent from our explanations
and justifications of our practices. If there is some strong dependence, then
it must be a rhetorical one (suggested perhaps in P. K. Feyerabend’s works
such as Against Method and Science in Free Society). Any description of a
practice or of a routine can be modified and used as theoretical justification
of something else; however, the ability to do something in most cases is suf-
ficient justification that one “knows-how” particular procedure. Moreover,
a description of an action can be distributed in parts, and some parts can be
used as justification of action described “properly” by other parts of the same
description serving as guides for some further type of action (and in this man-
ner creating a system of descriptions).
6.5.3. Practical reasoning based on a practice (P)
Let me for a moment go back to the issue of practical reasoning. There is
deductive and inductive practical reasoning. Deductive reasoning is based on
deductive inference from our practices, habits, customs, and fundamental val-
ues (Chang 1997). Inductive reasoning is based on our experience (personal
and communal). This type of reasoning is important because it can explain
the pressure of our own personal and communal experiences (condensed in
values) on our decisions and actions that sometimes seems to be divergent
with our worldviews. On the question of deductive practical reasoning, we
have at least two types of reasoning (Aristotelian and non-Aristotelian). Here
are some examples.
(a1) Recreational sport is good for people.
(a2) Jogging is a recreational sport.
(a3) Jogging is good for people.
This is purely theoretical reasoning concerning practical matters. Neverthe-
less, if we substitute it is good for people with would be good for me we have
practical reasoning like following type.



Anti-Sceptical Weapons I
195
(b1) Recreational sport would be good for me.
(b2) Jogging is a recreational sport.
(b3) Jogging would be good for me.
This kind of syllogism gives a doer the reason for an action, or at least the
reason for reaching a certain decision towards jogging as a rational thing
to do. Another type of deductive practical reasoning gives a doer as it were
activation (willingness, or motive) for doing something. At a glance this type
of reasoning is only composed of propositions, but upon closer examination
we can see that they are normative sentences and in that sense, pseudo-
propositions.
(c1) If I want to be in shape, I will exercise whenever I can.
(c2) I want to be in shape.
(c3) I will exercise whenever I can.
In addition, if the time is right, then the potential doer will exercise. This
type of reasoning is quite different from the two previous types because it
includes a decision. It is thus: practical reason + decision, while the first type
is only a practical reason. In Austin’s terminology, this syllogism is not con-
strued out of locutions like the first one, of illocutions like second one, but
of perlocutions, or performatives. Furthermore, the first type says only that a
certain type of action is desirable, or that there is a good practical reason for
reaching the decision on acting in this or that direction. The second type says
the same thing, but implicitly. Explicitly it says that one in fact will act in a
designated way if one will be in appropriate circumstances. The second type
says implicitly what the first type says explicitly, and the third type explicates
activation or readiness for action.
The distinction between reasoning a, b and c is important because it
stresses the independence of our practice. Type (b1–3) shows that there is
no real important practical distinction between practical argument, decision,
intention and action, as well as that there is no really important practical dis-
tinction between a rule, its understanding, and its application (or following of
it) (Wittgenstein OC 95–9). Furthermore, every case of practical reasoning
is made for practical purposes. These purposes can be extraordinary, as in
some specific practical situation or context that appears once or twice in the
lifetime of humans. On the other hand, we use practical reasoning when we
obtain a new habit or a practice, or want to change an old one partly or com-
pletely. However, through previous examples, we raised a far more important
issue, namely, the issue of “reasons for action.” This point will be clarified
in the following section.


196
Chapter Six
6.5.4. THE (CONNECTIVE) ANALYSIS OF
PRACTICAL PROPERLY BASIC BELIEFS (PPBB)
In the matter of connective analysis, we must discriminate between reasons
for action, activation; act itself, and its consequences. First, this is significant
because people sometimes act without any specific reason, i.e. their actions
are based on their worldview, or on practical reasons as standard (proto-
typical) practices. One could say that humans sometimes act pointlessly for
it surely looks like it. In that sense their actions are not strictly based on
their worldview or worldview + specific practical reasons. If such actions
are based in this or any manner whatsoever, then practically they will act the
same, for example with approximately the same level of confidence, or in
short—they will act by default (no matter if one does not see the significant
influence of worldview as the context of such an action by default). Some-
times one would call their action instinctive or even intuitive. Sometimes we
just act on a hunch; some kind of a feeling, “in the spirit of the whole” (CV).
These “reasons” for action are not necessarily irrational, because if there is
no previous practice, in other words no possibility of reason based on experi-
ence, and if the option is “living, forced and momentous” (James), then the
hunch is all we have, and it seems to be more rational to act on a hunch then
not to act at all. Secondly, it is important for practical purposes once again
because it is useful to understand who decides, for whom the decision is
made, who will act (freely or not), and who is responsible/accountable for
the consequences (good or bad, if one acts freely of course). Thus we have
individual and collective decisions. Accordingly, when one is deciding in the
name of one (himself or another), for many, or for all, the decision is always
individual. When many or all decide for one, for many, or for all, the decision
is always collective (Olson 1998). Thus, collective decision can mean, how
many decide, but it can also be understood as for whom they decide, and how
the decision was reached.
It is similar with an individual decision. The distinction can be rather
complicated when one includes an action because one can decide and act for
many (in the name of, or as a representative or, for example). The application
of these distinctions in social life, economy, law, and politics is obvious. The
most obvious result of this analysis of practice and practical reasoning is the
possibility of new grounds for practical philosophy (ethics, philosophy of:
technology, culture, politics, law, economy, etc.). Practical philosophy is in
this light very remote and independent from theoretical philosophy. Thus, now
we can renovate certain analysis of proper basicallity for practical beliefs as
motivations for actions. Analysis of proper basicallity for practical (political)
belief is the following. Practical belief is properly basic (PPBB) if it is:



Anti-Sceptical Weapons I
197
1. Based on our worldview (practice itself).
2. Evident in respect to our previous experience.
3. Defensible by practical argument, inquiry, or performance, namely:
(3.1) reasons,
(3.1.1) via which the belief is acquired,
(3.1.2) on which the belief is held,
(3.1.3) by which it may be defended,
(3.2) inquiry (primary, or secondary),
(3.3)  performance (when the judgement of a person with special skill or
experience is accepted as authoritative).
Practical beliefs that are non-basic are derived from basic ones:
4. Reached by inference (practical reasoning (deductive or inductive)).
5.  Or reached by testimony (Chicholm 1977, Plantinga 1983, 1997, Kenny
1992).
Now, the combination of this distinction with the previously highlighted
Wittgenstein categories can be somewhat confusing.
•  Basic practical (say political for instance) beliefs: (0) Practice (Praxis) is
the central and last justification of any practical belief (PPBB). (0) Implies
(1) worldview, world picture, (1.1) FOLs and (1.2) language game. On the
margin of (1), (1.2) are (2) and (3) but they are parts of (1), (1.2). Non-basic
practical beliefs are derived from the basic ones of PPBB by: (5) testimony,
or via (4) practical inference (deductive, inductive, or otherwise).
6.5.5. A defence of the overview
In this way one can clarify how it is possible to understand our WP as in-
cluded in our practice and not as something that should be explicated or
grounded in our philosophical, ethical, scientific, religious, or theological rea-
soning or findings. Thus, all our practical philosophy flows from our practice,
our ordinary daily life, our worldview, and our experience, or from beliefs
that are inferred from these practically basic beliefs (PPBB) manifested by
our routine practices. This is also appropriate for political practical beliefs. In
our worldview there exists, among other parts, the political part as well. All
of our political beliefs are grounded in our worldview, some of them directly,
and some indirectly. Our personal and communal political experiences, our
arguments (reasons), inquiry, and expert performances are also incorporated
in our worldview. Subsequently, all of our political beliefs are in one way or


198
Chapter Six
another properly basic if they are a part of our worldview. Others are derived
from them. If there is any point in talking about rationality here, one can only
say that this kind of rationality is implicit, tacit, and if explicated, then always
for practical purposes. However, I would like to leave this issue aside because
I do not want to discuss rationality of our individual or communal political
decisions. Our practical reasons that are political we can divide into three
groups: political, legal, and economic. This analysis explicates enough. For
instance, it explicates how it is possible to acquire a political part of a world
picture and to act according to it without knowing it explicitly. Political be-
liefs, ways of reaching political decisions, and customs of political action are
learned in adolescence, or at least the basics in terms of social action (there is
some evidence from the field of developmental and social psychology). What
is learned is a practice, or a practice of following a rule, and a practice of rule
following reveals the world picture.
Excursus: In the philosophy of law, this was recognised as a condition of tre-
mendous importance. As an illustration, I am only going to mention the point
of view of R. Dworkin and R. A. Posner. “That distinction would be unimport-
ant for practical purposes if the participants in a practice always agreed about
the best interpretation of it. But they do not agree… They must, to be sure,
agree about a great deal in order to share a social practice. They must share a
vocabulary… They must understand the world in sufficiently similar ways…
That means not just using the same dictionary, but sharing what Wittgenstein
called a form o f life.” (Dworkin 1986:93) On the other hand, Posner in major-
ity of his books recognises not just forms of life but also our world picture as
the fundamental ground for possibility of a “unique common world” (D. Pears’
term) which is a precondition for an interpretative community (Fish, see Posner
1990:426, also: 27, 47, 65, 74, 149–51).
Furthermore, previous analysis (or perspicuous representation if you like)
explains how it is possible that our political beliefs are in fact rational but
only implicitly, and that the explication of their rationality ends in our world-
view and finally in our way of life. In other words, appeal to practice is in
fact the appeal to an everyday, common, normal way of acting (routines,
customs, habits, institutions, and cultures). The justification of practical and
political belief (e.g. decision and/or action) thus can be grounded on practice
directly, but it can often be based on practice indirectly, through previous ex-
periences, through some quasi-arguments (practical reasoning), and present
circumstances. Ultimately, the greatest part of our political beliefs, decisions,
and actions is justified via practical reasoning. However, this reasoning is
always grounded on practice directly, or on other reasoning that is grounded
on practice. From the viewpoint of strong rationality these criteria can look



Anti-Sceptical Weapons I
199
as quite irrational, but it is the only way that we have in our practical life.
Furthermore, reasons for political belief, decision, or action are usually mixed
with non-political beliefs, decisions and actions, and with emotive attitudes
as well, etc.
This analysis also explains how it is possible for a world picture to change
in its political component or aspect. In light of Wittgenstein’s distinction
between four levels of HPs of the WP (Glock 1996) it should be added that
only a minor sub-part of the political part of worldview belongs to the first
class of HPs that are common to all men and almost all preconditions of hu-
man beings. These would be for example HPs regarding the nature of society
and social life, regarding the nature of elementary collaboration, contribution
to and benefits of society, the nature of social, economical, legal and politi-
cal systems, procedures, justice, basic institutions like “standard procedure,”
“reward,” “punishment,” etc. Let me finish with what I have not tried to do
in the present addendum.
I have not tried to use Wittgenstein’s philosophy to analyse and to make conclu-
sions regarding his political convictions (like Crary 2000:118–47).
Furthermore, I have not tried to make Wittgenstein’s philosophy relevant in
some fields of political philosophy (like the following authors: Winch (1958),
Pitkin (1972), Coates (1996), Holt (1997) and others did (like for instance many
authors did in Kitching and Pleasants (eds.) 2002 “Marx and Wittgenstein,” and
did it very well)).
Finally, I have not been concerned with an application or use of Wittgen-
stein’s philosophy in law, like Posner in fact tried to do in his epistemology and
ontology of law (Posner 1993:37–247, 2001:319–411).
I am just saying that in certain combination his remarks on the nature of
philosophy, language, reality, world picture, everydayness, and practice have
some interesting consequences for our understanding of the social “tasks” of
human beings, especially in practical (political) philosophy, and social and
political as well. Some of these consequences were examined in this annex.
As Wittgenstein writes:
•  “A philosophical question is similar to one about the constitution of a
particular society.—And it’s as if a group of people came together without
clearly written rules, but with a need for them; indeed also with an instinct
that caused them to observe certain rules at their meetings; but this is made
difficult by the fact that nothing has been clearly articulated about this, and
no arrangement has been made which brings the rules out clearly. Thus
they in fact view one of their own as president, but he doesn’t sit at the head
of the table and has no distinguishing marks, and that makes negotiations


200
Chapter Six
difficult. That is why we come along and create a clear order: we seat the
president at a clearly identifiable spot, seat his secretary next to him at a
little table of his own, and seat the other full members in two rows on both
sides of the table, etc., etc.” (BT 89)
•  “Our only task is to be just. That is, we must only point out and resolve
the injustices of philosophy, and not posit new parties and creeds.” “All
philosophy can do is to destroy idols. And that means, not making new
ones—like in the absence of an idol.” (CV 181)


201
Chapter Seven
Anti-Sceptical Weapons II:
Hinges, gyroscopes, and planets
The squirrel does not infer by induction that it is going to need stores next
winter as well. (OC 287)
7.1. SETTING THE SCENE
Analytical philosophy since 1945 has been conditioned in large part by W.
V. O. Quine’s criticism of two dogmas of empiricism ([1951] 1953:20–47),
namely the analytic/synthetic distinction and the reductionism (or the verifi-
cationist analysis of meaning), and more to that, by his proposal of a solution
that he calls “pragmatism,” which is based on his commitment to empiricism
and Duhem-inspired holism. There are consequences for epistemology as well,
namely, “just as philosophical claims about meaning require empirical sub-
stantiation, so do philosophical claims about justification of beliefs” (Baldwin
2001:76). This particular idea guided Quine from empiricism and holism re-
garding meaning (supposedly shifting toward pragmatism) to epistemological
naturalism (supposedly shifting to pragmatism as well in his essay “Epistemol-
ogy naturalized” 1969). According to the historical and sociological develop-
ment of at least one school of analytic philosophy, although some stages of this
process were somewhat improperly raised to the level of vital philosophical
heuristic instruments, one has to have a theory of meaning which leads to a so-
lution of any further issue raised, such as knowledge, morality, existence, etc.,
however, only on the syntactic, and sometimes on the semantic level as well
(regarding differences within this particular school see for instance Rorty 1967,
Glock 1997, Stroll 2000, and Baldwin 2001). This means that epistemology is
completely exhausted in a semantic conception of it, and that “[t]he traditional
philosophical discipline of epistemology dissolves into the choice of a language


202
Chapter Seven
and the setting out of the logical syntax of that language” (Skorupski 1997:79).
Therefore, for Quine, not just a theory of meaning, but a theory of knowledge
should be “scientistic, empiricist, and behaviourist” too (Stroll 2000:188). On
the other hand, something completely different was created by many members
of the Oxford school of ordinary language, and likewise by Wittgenstein at
Cambridge.
Leaving aside what members of this school have said regarding issues of
meaning and knowledge, here the focus will be situated on Wittgenstein’s
agenda regarding meaning and knowledge which should be non-scientistic,
grammatical (i.e. conceptual), and morphological. In the final analysis,
Wittgenstein’s and Quine’s conceptions of pragmatism overlap but only
accidentally. They have precisely nothing in common except the accident
of course. In the present chapter and in repudiating such a series of presup-
positions, arguments, and conclusions (spelled out mainly in Quine’s “Two
Dogmas of Empiricism” and “Epistemology naturalised”), “a more thorough
pragmatism will be espoused” (Quine 1953:20). However, this repudiation
will be made indirectly, that is to say, solely by explicating some elements
of the later philosophy of L. J. J. Wittgenstein regarding certainty and devoid
of any further reference to Quine’s analyses whatsoever. Wittgenstein wrote
the last remarks regarding the matter in question two days before he died,
April 29, 1951, later published in the book “On Certainty” (1969), without
any awareness of Quine’s paper as far as I know. Therefore, the final com-
parison and confrontation with Quine’s viewpoint will be left for readers to
tackle themselves (regarding various comparisons between Wittgenstein and
Quine concerning a theory of meaning, knowledge, and other enigmas, see
Arrington and Glock, 1996). In short, the present exposé will lead the reader
to the viewpoint opposite not just of Quine’s “epistemology naturalised” but
of the majority of 20th century epistemological agendas as well, since it will
be a kind of certainty morphologised (in view of the fact that Wittgenstein
said to Malcolm “What I give is the morphology of the use of an expression”
and “An expression has meaning only in stream of life,” Malcolm 1984:43,
75). Wittgenstein’s philosophical procedure from “Blue and Brown Books”
onwards can be summarised as a morphological one with perspicuous presen-
tation (philosophical grammar) as its most important goal or as the result of
application of such procedure.
In the present chapter the procedure itself will not be discussed more than it
is necessary (since this task is already partially accomplished, see Breithaupt
et. al. 2002, see Chapters 1 and 3). However, the very result in terms of “clear
description” will be of our innermost interest. In the following sections it will
be explicated that the analytic/synthetic distinction stands, and that it should
be broadened with (at least one) new kinds of quasi propositions or remarks,



Anti-Sceptical Weapons II
203
and that such expended distinction rests on completely different kind of
holism, namely a morphological and an anthropological one which is in its
very nature a kind of pragmatist standpoint. If such an interpretation guides
us away from Quine regarding meaning and certainty, in(to) another wor(l)
ds, to come down from “the barren heights of cleverness” to “the valleys of
foolishness” (CV 92), then so be it. This seems to be more than enough in
order to set the context in which the following sections should be understood.
As almost religiously dedicated followers of fashion regarding contemporary
writing fashions in analytic philosophy, I now turn to tedious minute (con-
nective) analyses of a few passages from Wittgenstein’s works (mostly from
OC, and few from PI) which should be less boring and irritating than my
comments and conclusions (the irony is that if one disagrees with this kind
of philosophising, then one dislikes a certain writing style, precisely the point
made by later Rorty concerning hard-core naturalist analytic philosophy, and
if one tries to claim anything more besides a reference to this particular style,
then one is surely auto-referentially inconsistent to use the phrase in a similar
cynical manner as was used by the mentioned philosopher).
•  In terms of small technical note it should be explained that I differenti-
ate between “talking about morphological and grammatical approach to
certainty” which is not necessarily “itself a morphological talk,” and “ap-
plying morphological method in the very investigation of certainty.” Both
approaches are indispensable, because a look from within enables “under-
standing,” while a look from the outside enables “criticism and reflexive
introspection regarding the subject matter.” In the present paper both ap-
proaches are combined, namely examples as an application of the method
are highlighted as (a), (b), (c)…, while results of such application (mostly
quotations from Wittgenstein and some Wittgenstein scholars) in terms of
search for philosophical grammar or perspicuous presentation of certainty
are highlighted as (1), (2), (3) … Such citations are often followed by short
commentaries highlighted with a dot as in the present case.
•  In addition, what was called HP (hinge-propositions) in the previous chap-
ter (written almost a decade ago) here will be separated into HP and AP
(axis-remarks) due to a new discovery by J. Schulte. Since there are some
doubts regarding the nature of AP, for instance are they a type of HP, or
are they are a new kind of propositions, one should not get confused. The
basic idea is the following: HPs are in fact explicated APs in proposition
form and as such they are half-grammatical and half-empirical remarks,
while if they are “implicit in actions” they are pure APs. Therefore, the
central purpose of this chapter is to more deeply connect basic ideas of
morphology, grammar, and action regarding certainty in OC and related


204
Chapter Seven
works. An additional goal is to draw some further distinctions between
propositions (in fact sentence functions) mentioned in OC, namely between
grammatical or hinge, and empirical propositions in a way to describe the
“rough ground” and “the background” of this distinction which rests in
“axis-(quasi)-propositions.”
7.2. HINGES, GYROSCOPES, AND PLANETS (I)
“Certainties of a world-picture” (further abbreviated as CWP) (see Glock
1996, Kober 1996, Pritchard 2005) are in Wittgenstein’s morphological
jargon similar to “common sense propositions” (or common sense truisms,
further abbreviated as CSP) in G. E. Moore’s epistemological vocabulary
(see Swensson 1981, Stroll 1994, Coates 1996, Williams 2004, Wright 2004).
However, there are some differences. The most obvious one is categorical in
terms of use of epistemic operators. Say that P is any CWP or CSP. Moore
would construe it as “I know that P.,” while Wittgenstein would construe it
as “We are / it is certain that P.” Since at the moment I am not engaged in
investigation of epistemic logic (perhaps I should be) there are other differ-
ences that one should focus on, namely between two, perhaps tree kinds of
propositions. For instance, propositions
(a)  “Felix is a very stubborn cat,” “Felix is extremely lazy,” or “Felix likes
to sleep on his tree”
are regarded as empirical, both by Moore and Wittgenstein. In addition, Witt-
genstein considers propositions like
(b) “Cats are animals, not plants,” “Cats purr, while dogs yelp,”
as grammatical. (Here it should be noted that I am using this expression
(namely, “the proposition like P as grammatical one”) only metaphorically,
really there are only “sentences” which can be used in order to make “gram-
matical remarks,” or to “describe a situation,” or to explicate “an underlying
and implicit conviction/convention (in terms of agreement)” for instance. It
is not my intention to draw up a list of grammatical propositions regarding
certainty. However, this task may surely be condemned to failure no matter if
Wittgenstein really speaks of “grammar” of various phenomena or families of
concepts like colour, shape, size, etc. noted by J. Schulte.) Now, beside these
two kinds, both of them give examples of rather odd propositions, namely



Anti-Sceptical Weapons II
205
Moore of propositions like “I have two hands” (for which he claims that ev-
erybody knows them), and Wittgenstein of propositions like
(c)  “Cats do not grow on trees,” “Cats like milk,” “Cars do not grow from the
ground,” or “We humans know that we have ten toes without looking at them
and counting them” (for which he claims that they are completely certain in
virtue of being a part of a system of CWP which is composed of propositions
like those mentioned under a, b, and right here, see Baker and Hacker 2005,
Vol. I, Part I:17, footnote 43 referring to hinges by saying “unquestioned and
unchallenged pivots on which our noetic structure turns”).
Wittgenstein considers all of them (the propositions under c) to be of the
same kind, however he had different doubts, for instance, if they are really
propositions? Let us for now assume that these under (c) are propositions,
sentences, or a kind of remarks. The question is then of which kind are
they? Answering this should help one to decide on the answer to the previ-
ous question. Surely, they are not purely empirical (perhaps they could be
regarded as empirical generalisations or conclusions made via simple induc-
tion; however, regarding induction OC 287 is quite illustrative), and they are
not purely grammatical (or rule-like propositions) either, since they are not
sufficiently similar to propositions under (a), nor can they be easily deduced
from propositions under (a), and more to that, grammatical propositions are
not so strange compared to (c), like for instance “A complex is composed of
its parts,” “Four meters is a length,” or “An order orders its own execution”
(all of which are Wittgenstein’s examples from various works). On the other
hand, propositions under (c) are not empirical since it seems very difficult to
imagine “serious” observations of, for instance, ground in order to find out if
cars grow from it. They are not grammatical since they do not have a feature
of being rule-like such as grammatical propositions indeed do possess. On the
other hand, they seem to be more like J. L. Austin’s performatives than con-
statives, more an issue of know-how than of know-that, more like exclama-
tions or gestures than sentences functioning as propositions (Schulte 2005).
Namely, if a child is standing under a tree looking at Felix with extraordinary
caution and if one asks the child “What’s going on?” and the child answers
(d) “(I believe that) Cats grow on trees”
then one has at least two possible comments. One should remind the child of
many other propositions the child believes and make it see the inconsistency,
in other words, show the child the whole world-picture that we humans have


206
Chapter Seven
(that the child is acquiring at the moment). The other possibility would be to
say something like the first proposition under (c). Then, (c) would be more
like a persuasion or a guarantee of a kind like for example, “I assure you that
cats do not grow on trees, please go there and do something else” (regarding
the last point see Moyal-Sharrock 2005:93). An another issue is that CWPs,
especially those under (c),
(c1)  could be somewhat different from propositions (as we already hinted; on
this point see Orr 1989, Phillips 2005:20),
(c2)  or if they are not (a) or (b) they could be a new sort of proposition, so
to say half-grammatical (half-b) and half-empirical (half-a) propositions
which can change one into another depending on the level of explication
of a practice (the solution which raises separate problems, see Table 7.1),
or simply special kinds of sentences functioning as grammatical proposi-
tions (see Stroll 1994:146, Noë 1994:1–42).
However, Wittgenstein proposes many metaphors in order to clarify their
nature and more to that their function regarding our practical daily life.
One of these metaphors is the metaphor of “hinges” according to which
CWP would be like hinges on which our whole world-picture as a door
turns. In this section, it will be suggested that the hinge-metaphor hides
in fact two different metaphors, the metaphor of hinges, and the metaphor
of a planet, and that distinguishing between them helps one to understand
the roots and the function of CWP in Wittgenstein’s philosophy (and pos-
sibly in his Weltanschauung as well). In OC propositions or “certainties of
a world-picture” are illustrated by two (perhaps three) somewhat different
metaphors, namely by the metaphor of hinges (implicitly and explicitly),
and by the metaphor of something else, perhaps of a gyroscope or a planet
(as shown in Table 7.2).
Table 7.1. Three kinds of propositions of CWP.



Anti-Sceptical Weapons II
207
EXCURSUS 1: J. SCHULTE’S PROPOSAL—
AXES ARE NOT HINGES
Regarding this distinction between (1) and (2) in Table 8.2, many Wittgen-
stein scholars do not make it even if it partially goes in the direction of their
basic interpretation (see for instance Stroll, 2005:35, especially regarding
foundationalism). However, some are on the right track. Among these the
paper by J. Schulte must be mentioned in some detail.
(0)  As far as we know, among Wittgenstein scholars the first one to distin-
guish between (1) and (2), and explicitly emphasising and commenting
on OC 152 (as “the another image”) in contrast to 341–3 was Joachim
Schulte in his paper “Within a System” (Schulte 2005:71). Schulte
writes, “Such an axis of rotation is a very different sort of thing from
the kind of hinge on which the previous image is centred. Such an axis
of rotation may be said to stand fast, but it does not do any real work;
nor does it hold fast anything which in order to be able to move would
need its support. One may say that a certain object would not move the
way it does unless it, or one of its parts, could be described as rotating
around such and such an axis. But that is a completely different kind of
statement from the claim, for instance, that a given door turns on cer-
tain hinges. And because of this difference I want to conclude that the
image of the axis of rotation applies to a different sort of proposition
from that to which the hinge image applies. It is propositions convey-
ing basic rules or information that can be compared with hinges while
sentences expressing commonplaces of the type discussed by Moore
Table 7.2. CWP metaphors.


208
Chapter Seven
and Wittgenstein are more like axis of rotation.” (Schulte 2005:71, see
also 70–73)
•  A brief commentary. J. Schulte brilliantly recognises two different
metaphors here and one should agree with the analysis completely.
Furthermore, I will agree that so to speak axis-propositions are, as
Schulte suggests, more like gestures or exclamations, and there-
fore quasi-propositions. Now, Schulte does not explicate if hinge-
metaphor is a metaphor of grammatical remark or not. If this is the
case, and if the expression “basic rules” that he uses suggests it, then
what we have besides empirical propositions (previously as (a)),
and grammatical propositions (previously as (b)), are in fact axis-
propositions (previously as (c), i.e. hinge-propositions). More to that,
hinges are a metaphor for grammatical propositions, while axis is a
metaphor for all previously under (c), in another words, what was
by a vast majority of scholars referred to as “hinge-propositions”
(including the author of these essay; see previous chapter).
(0.1)  Thus, Schulte’s interpretation (or should we say a discovery) basi-
cally claims that propositions like (c) “Cats do not grow on trees,”
“Cats like milk,” “Cars do not grow from the ground,” or “We hu-
mans know that we have ten toes without looking at them and count-
ing them” are not hinge-propositions since hinges are metaphors for
grammatical propositions, and are not propositions at all, rather more
like gestures or exclamations (Schulte 2005:71).
•  A brief commentary. Therefore, when a mother sees her son Willard,
standing in front of a tree claiming that he is waiting to see do cats
grow on trees, all she could do is to make a facial expression (saying
“I don’t get it Willard.”), to make a gesture (showing Willard to move
away from the tree), and finally, if previous interferences do not help,
to exclaim “Willard, you silly boy, cats do not grow on trees in Ohio!”
(learning or acquiring a WP is explicitly mentioned by Schulte as a
situation in which axis needs to be claimed). In my opinion there are
far-reaching consequences of this interpretation, namely, pragmatic
and indeed (something like) pragmatist consequences advocated by
scholars other than Schulte himself, among which are Putnam, Ko-
ber, Williams, Goodman, and few others. To point to some of these
consequences will be our basic task further on in the present chapter,
and perhaps to point to some general consequences for contemporary
epistemology as well completely being on the wrong tracks, espe-
cially epistemology naturalised (if it is possible of course, since it is
mostly out of range of goals of this chapter). Besides these conse-
quences there are also morphological techniques implicit in Wittgen-



Anti-Sceptical Weapons II
209
stein’s treatment (with what Schulte would probably agree), and some
of these regarding certainty will be mentioned also, at least in terms
of interpreting OC as a morphological investigation and a grammar
of certainty. The point is the following, if one wants to understand an
FOL, then one needs to understand grammatical propositions, but in
order to understand them, one needs to understand empirical proposi-
tions as well and finally, the axis of an FOL.
7.3. HINGES, GYROSCOPES, AND PLANETS (II)
To return to Table 8.2, one could combine these metaphors from Table 8.2,
and these kinds of propositions from Table 8.1, into an overall account of
CWP and it could be regarded as a first step toward perspicuous presentation
of certainty. Such presentation contains (examples as rules of) grammatical
certainties, hinges, and empirical certainties. The most obvious and basic
relation of these kinds of propositions, their metaphors, and their relation to
epistemic justification is shown in Figure 7.1.
Figure 7.1. The relation between empirical, grammatical, and hinge propositions in
two different metaphors.


210
Chapter Seven
Now, regarding these two metaphors (1 and 2 as shown in Table 7.2),
some differences should be investigated. Namely, there are references to the
difference between experiential or empirical propositions and grammatical
or rule-like propositions which should be considered and taken into account
regarding other Wittgenstein’s works (see Forster 2004). Given that there is
such difference, the issue here is—are these hinges a metaphor for grammati-
cal propositions, empirical propositions, or is it a new kind of proposition,
axis-propositions? Regarding this point, the often analysed sections OC 94–9
suggest that there is no strict difference between empirical and grammatical
propositions. To be precise, they can change one into another, for instance
in terms of the beautiful river-metaphor an empirical proposition can harden
and function as a river bed, while other empirical propositions can stay fluid
and function as the waters of a river, and vice versa. In the light of this river-
metaphor, there are at least two interesting possible interpretations of hinges.
(e)  A “hinge” could be a kind of “primordial phenomenon” (or the basic
epistemic pattern) that under particular (practical) circumstances could
be turned into a proposition (simply spelled out or explicated), namely
into a combination of these as being half-empirical and half-grammatical
proposition (hinges are so to say warnings of a kind, say “You are within
the boundaries of an FOL, it is OK,” or “Achtung! If you continue with
the present activity you will leave the territory of your FOL and the sys-
tem will collapse in approximately 10 minutes”).
(f)  On the other hand, it could be only a special case of a grammatical propo-
sition, or of an empirical proposition, or a feature of it, if a grammatical
or empirical proposition contains epistemic expressions, just as an un-
derstanding (seeing-as) of a two-aspectual drawing (say a duck-rabbit)
implies half-seeing and half-interpreting. However, there is an issue here
since “So we interpret it, and see it as we interpret it” (PI p. 193), so the
certainty of hinges implies being half-grammatical and half-empirical, but
there is another issue here as well since “One thinks that one is tracing the
outline of the thing’s nature over and over again, and one is merely tracing
round the frame through we look at it” (PI 114).
It would be like the following—a grammar, or a particular experience reveals
itself as a “hinge” via any case of explicated grammar, or of use of a word,
or of an expression which belongs to a particular epistemic jargon. However,
there could be a grammar not of words and expressions only, but of practices
as well, and therefore the interpretation (e) seems to be more plausible (re-
garding problems with hinges see Orr 1989:134–53). Now, let us turn to the
issue of the difference between hinge-metaphors.



Anti-Sceptical Weapons II
211
1.  Hinges-metaphor. The first metaphor of CWP (1 in Table 8.2, OC 341–3,
655, see Stroll 1994:134–8, Moyal-Sharrock and Brenner 2005:2) is
rather known, acknowledged, and commented upon among Wittgenstein
scholars (see references, especially Moyal-Sharrock, Brenner 2005). The
mentioned section runs as follows, “That is to say, the questions that we
raise and our doubts depend on the fact that some propositions are exempt
from doubt, are as it were like hinges on which those turn.” […] “If I want
the door to turn, the hinges must stay put.”
•  A brief commentary. A door can turn because hinges are immovable.
Hinges can be considered as a part of the door and therefore their im-
mobility is determined by understanding them as immovable parts of the
frame of a door, which we consider immovable contrary to a door which
we consider movable (see again Figure 7.1).
2.  A gyroscope or a planet-metaphor (axis metaphor). However, a different
section (OC 152) suggests a different metaphor of CWP, precisely that of
a planet, or of a gyroscope. In the referenced section Wittgenstein says,
“The axis is not fixed in the sense that anything holds it fast, but the move-
ment around it determines its immobility” (see proposition (0) and brief
commentary by Schulte 2005).
•  A brief commentary. In the case of a hinge a door frame holds a hinge
fast and a door can be opened and closed, while in this case nothing
holds the axis fast (there are no two connecting parts like hinges), but
the movement around it determines its immobility. In other words,
we do not understand the axis of a rotating body such as a planet, or
a gyroscope as being immovable in the same sense as we understand
hinges as parts of a door as being immovable. In the case of such rotat-
ing body, precisely its rotation is what determines its immovability, or
one could say, immovability is presupposed by its rotation. That which
is immovable in this metaphor is invisible, implicit, but still completely
manifested by the very rotation of the body (like the life of a system of
beliefs). There is also the element of momentum in this metaphor which
should be considered.
Regarding Figure 7.1, the hinge metaphor (1) represents a practice as a
rough ground of FOL (PI 107) and is construed in terms of a (primitive) de-
scription of a practice belonging to an FOL (mentioning features of a practice,
for instance by a child: “Fruit tree, fruits, Felix, growing…” etc.) while the
gyroscope/planet metaphor (2) represents a practice as a background (OC 94)
and it is construed as a proposition claiming something regarding particular
practice belonging to an FOL (for instance, child’s mother: “Don’t stand
there Willard, Felix don’t grow on a tree, he just likes to sleep there for a


212
Chapter Seven
long time,” etc.). Regarding (1) and (2), that is conceivably all that there is
to it. In other words, one should not give any more weight to this distinction
then there is, namely a distinction in metaphors. On the other hand, one could
say that this indicates a difference between “theories of justification,” namely
between fundationalism and coherentism. Personally, it is hard to see it this
way. However, this distinction, if it is relevant at all, can perhaps suggest the
difference between certainties of a world-picture, namely between
(g)  those CWP on the border of a system of beliefs (empirical CWP), and
those CWP closer to the “centre of rotation” of a system of beliefs (gram-
matical CWP, rules, regarding insinuation of coherentism),
(h)  and particular practices, as it were particular styles, routines, and cus-
toms, and on the other hand, the whole of standard procedures (echoing
Austin once again) or institutions, traditions and perhaps even cultures
(regarding insinuation of foundationalism).
Empirical CWP and grammatical CWP together with hinge-CWP refer to a
practice as the “background” or the context of justification in (g), while they
refer to a practice as the “rough ground” (features of a daily epistemic situ-
ations) of justification in (h). According to (g) hinges would be regarded as
propositions, and more to that as being half-empirical and half-grammatical
propositions (as shown in Figure 8.1). However, the solution (h) seems to
be correct since hinges are just interpreted as propositions and they are not
genuine propositions as in the following manner. Say that S believes that P,
while P being “If Q occurs, then it is God’s will.” Now, in Wittgenstein’s
viewpoint this would not be a genuine proposition, rather just (a part of) a
description of an action, say, “If P occurs, then S would not complain.” In
the same manner and regarding examples (c) and (d), the proposition “Cats
do not grow on trees” is just a part of a description of an action belonging to
an FOL, say “Willard is not standing in front of a tree in order to see does
Felix grow on it. He is just surprised by the fact that Felix can sleep on his
tree for a long time.”
7.4. CERTAINTY MORPHOLOGISED
However, hinges could be “out there” (implicit in our daily routines) before
and after one explicates them or in other words, before they are constructed as
“hinge-propositions” (of course, some hinge-propositions could be invented
from time to time). They seem to be implicit in our practices, or in a lack of
a certain practice; for instance and regarding the first example with Felix,



Anti-Sceptical Weapons II
213
“We do not stand under trees to check if do cats really grow on them, we
simply call them or force them to come down if for instance they are mak-
ing too much noise.” Our actions manifest what is certain for us “beyond
reasonable doubt.” The possibility (h) is in fact the possibility that regarding
certainty points to a different direction, different sphere, perhaps even beyond
pure epistemic language-games regarding the very nature of CWP since “a
foundation” in Wittgenstein’s jargon is not meant just in terms of “epistemic
grounds,” but previously to that, in terms of living in an accordance with
particular FOL one belongs to, and with its standard daily routines (“rough
ground,” PI 107). Regarding this point, namely regarding interpreting basic
certainty first and foremost as practical, and then perhaps as theoretical (in
some theoretical language-game with humble use of words like knowledge,
evidence, belief, truth, and similar of course), the following passages from PI
and OC seem to be of some relevance.
3.  “I shall get burnt if I put my hand in the fire: that is certainty. That is to
say: here we see the meaning of certainty. (What it amounts to, not just
the meaning of the word “certainty.”)” (PI 474) “The kind of certainty is
the kind of language-game.” (PI p. 224) “Ask, not: “What goes on in us
when we are certain that....?”—but: How is ‘the certainty that this is the
case’ manifested in human action?” (PI p. 225)
4.  “Now I would like to regard this certainty, not as something akin to hasti-
ness or superficiality, but as a form of life.” (OC 358) “But that means I
want to conceive it as something that lies beyond being justified or unjus-
tified; as it were, as something animal.” (OC 359) “I want to regard man
here as an animal; as a primitive being to which one grants instinct but
not ratiocination. As a creature in a primitive state. Any logic good enough
for a primitive means of communication needs no apology from us.” (OC
475) (Emphases added).
•  A brief commentary. These passages point to a strange direction, namely
to a justification without justification, that is to say that a chain of
justifications ends in and by practice, or in human action which if it is
standard (as a particular routine), then it is self-guided as well. Justifica-
tion ends not with a particular hinge-proposition, but when it bumps into
the whole of CWP, the whole of our practices (our culture, our FOL).
More to that, a justification changes its nature, it stops to be “epistemic
justification” (epistemological) and it starts to be “human justifica-
tion” (anthropological, perhaps in terms of cultural anthropology). The
other way around, our FOL, our culture, and even some of our hinge
propositions and belonging to our “system of beliefs,” or to our “frame
of reference” (Bezugssystem, OC 83, see also OC 102, 105, 126, 136,


214
Chapter Seven
140–4, 225, 232, 274, 410, see CV 8–9, and OC 594), manifest that we
are certain regarding some “standard” practices and issues because our
way of acting shows that we are certain. This seems to be a pragmatist
justification on many levels (as shown in Table 7.3).
Let us take a look at another example. One could demonstrate “I know how
to repair a flat tire” in various ways, for instance by presenting a certificate
that he or she is a trained professional, etc. However, by actually repairing it
one shows that the proposition “I know how to repair a flat tire” is justified.
In terms of the first example, by our very actions regarding cats on trees we
show that we are certain that cats do not grow on trees. Therefore, basically,
knowledge is not propositional, but practical (non-propositional), as is justi-
fication, or to continue with our own metaphor used here, it is a justification
without justification because it is a practice justified by practice. Regarding
this point the following passages from PI again could be quite illustrative.
5.  “The grammar of the word knows is evidently closely related to that of
can, is able to. But also closely related to that of understands. (Mastery
of a technique.)” (PI 155) “What people accept as a justification—shows
how they think and live.” (PI 325)
•  A brief commentary. However, this is a controversial idea, namely that
the theoretical certainty is roofed or covered by, or grounded in the prac-
tical one (even regarding mathematical certainty, see OC 212), or by
the certainty of a particular practice, and consequently that know-how
is the basic form of knowledge compared to know-that or propositional
knowledge as a derived type, and that belief-in is the basic form of belief
compared to belief-that as propositional belief.
Table 7.3. A pragmatist justification.



Anti-Sceptical Weapons II
215
Such or similar principles constitute the core of pragmatist or performative
(in J. L. Austin’s terms) “description” of knowledge and justification (a mor-
phology of such expressions, and such practices). One could find the decisive
reason for this idea in OC 307 which could be paraphrased in the following
manner:
6.  reasons for a practice are always less justified than the practice itself that
they suppose to justify (a practice is implicitly justified by being a stan-
dard practice within a system of standard practices belonging to particular
FOL, but this point raises an issue concerning morals and ethics which I
must leave for some further occasion).
Therefore, a practice is an end of a chain of justifications, but on the other
hand it is not a part of such chain, since it is merely “a decision for practical
purposes” (OC 49), and therefore, this (dis)solution of epistemological prob-
lem of knowledge and certainty surely looks like pragmatism.
7.  “Not only rules, but also examples are needed for establishing a practice.
Our rules leave loop-holes open, and the practice has to speak for itself”
(OC 139) “So I am trying to say something that sounds like pragmatism.
Here I am being thwarted by a kind of Weltanschauung.” (OC 422)
•  A brief commentary (on 6 and 7). Our CWP as a system of certainties
(implicit in practices) manifested by our way of life is justified by our
practices and practices are beyond being justified or unjustified if they
are a part of our (perhaps human) FOL. They have epistemic (or human)
power to dismiss any particular doubt (however, not an alternative FOL,
since this issue is closely connected to Wittgenstein’s implicit thesis
of relativity of rationality, regarding this complicated issue see Glock
2001, Preston 2008) simply by being practices or standard routines.
In other words, our way of life or practical certainties dismiss many
doubts simply by being practical certainties of a way of life; bizarrely
enough, our practices belonging to our FOL are epistemically “power-
ful” precisely because they are so to say “blind” (see Chapter 6). In other
words, we are able to live, act, and talk as we do precisely because we
are certain of so many things composed in an enormous system which is
like a shadow of everything we do or say. Conversely, practices can be
explicated into CWP or hinge-propositions, empirical and grammatical
ones, but again, only for particular practical purposes. Once again, this
seems to be at the core of Wittgenstein’s treatment of certainty. Besides
that, the difference between CWP and CSP (common-sense proposi-
tions, or Moore-type propositions) seems to be more obvious (as shown


216
Chapter Seven
in Table 7.4). CSP are not knowledge-claims, they are, if legitimate at
all (being claimed without particular context and practical purpose in
Moore’s case), certainties at most, and therefore CWP.
7.5. PRESENTED APPROACH CONTRASTED
WITH SOME NEW INVESTIGATIONS OF OC
This paper presents continuation of our interest in OC. Throughout the last
decade, there was not much of a discussion going on regarding OC (however,
a few papers were published and a few interesting lectures were held at vari-
ous symposia). On the other hand, from the time when OC was published
onwards, generally speaking, some major works in scholarly analysis were
available, such as T. Morawetz “Wittgenstein and Knowledge” (1978), G.
Swenson “On Doubting the Reality” (1981), G. Conway “Wittgenstein on
Foundations,” M. McGinn “Sense and Certainty” (1989), M. Kober “Gewis-
sheit als Norm” (1993), and A. Stroll “Moore and Wittgenstein on Certainty”
(1994) (for a somewhat more comprehensive list of papers and books see
bibliography). In addition, a few papers were also available (mostly early
interpretations during 70’s). However, the treatment presented should be
contrasted to some new investigations of OC. In 2005 “Readings of Wittgen-
Table 7.4. A CWP as the background system of beliefs, and as being the rough ground
of daily practice.



Anti-Sceptical Weapons II
217
stein’s On Certainty” was published (edited by D. Moyal-Sharrock and W.
H. Brenner). The book consists of an Introduction and fourteen essays on OC
divided into four parts regarding four readings of OC, namely
•  the framework reading (including papers by Stroll, Williams, Schulte, and
Moyal-Sharrock),
•  the transcendental reading (including papers by Mounce, Brenner, and
Rudd),
•  the epistemic reading (including papers by Morawetz, Prichard, and Ko-
ber), (perhaps Williams 2004 and Wright 2004 can be included here as
well), and
• the therapeutic reading (including papers by Minar, Crary, and Read).
The majority of authors in the mentioned volume develop their own inter-
pretations that were previously reached. However, as editors pointed out,
first, second and third interpretations “collapse into one” (Moyal-Sharrock,
Brenner 2005:3–4), and then what we have is to some extent a sharp differ-
ence between “grammatical” reading and “therapeutic” reading (which will
