not be discussed here). However, if this is really the case, then the present
analysis of certainty goes along with the “grammatical” reading more than
with the “therapeutic” one. There are several reasons why this is so.
•  As it was already explicated, the basic similarity is with J. Schulte’s paper of
which the present one tries to be a development in a somewhat strange direc-
tion. Regarding other papers there are some similarities as well. The first is
to be found in the similarity of this interpretation with the one defended by
Moyal-Sharrock in the context of the framework reading, namely in the part
where “belief-in” is connected with “know-how.” The second point is to be
found in similarity with some elements of the paper by Rudd, namely the idea
of the primacy of practice. Finally, the most similarities can be found regard-
ing all papers under the chapter “the epistemic reading.” However, contrary
to all papers included in epistemic reading chapters, Wittgenstein’s concept
of certainty, as it was argued, is not epistemological at all; it is basically an
anthropological (and socialised) one, meaning that it is connected with his
concepts of human being, human cultures, and ways of life.
The particular addition to these interpretations by the present one could be
summarised in terms of putting much more weight on the “pragmatist account
of certainty” as being closely connected to the idea of “a philosophical gram-
mar,” i.e. OC as a nice example of it regarding “certainty” (as it were per-
spicuous presentation of certainty as a result of morphological investigation


218
Chapter Seven
of a phenomena in question), and to the idea of an FOL as a form of culture
(see Kober 1996, and 2005:229). However, Wittgenstein’s pragmatism is
not partial or incomplete in terms of full-blooded theory, quite the contrary,
it is essentially a European regarding style meaning that the emphasis is po-
sitioned much more on a practice itself than on its consequences like in US
style (like in James for instance). Namely, it is characteristics like regular-
ity, steadiness, general acceptance, and know-how of a practice that gives
it power in terms of a source of certainty, not the fact that actions produce
convenient consequences. This point needs to be explicated in some detail
regarding basic structure of OC, and some examples of subtopics.
EXCURSUS 2: SOME ELEMENTS CONNECTING
PARTICULAR TOPICS OF OC
Here, the basics of the standpoint regarding the topic of doubt/uncertainty (D/
UC) in OC are presented and some more detailed elements will be presented
in order to make the present standpoint more available, especially in the light
of mentioned new investigations of OC, namely in order to make the present
interpretation available. First, some basics regarding the whole work OC will
be presented (as shown in Table 7.5).
These (minor) changes (regarding columns containing curved arrows and the
expression “creating a system,” and after hinge-containing “axis” propositions)
were made in the light of Schulte’s discovery, and in the light of the following
emphasis on action, practice, and life especially regarding their interconnections
Table 7.5. Basic structure of OC (repeated from chapter 6 with minor corrections,
see Table 6.1).



Anti-Sceptical Weapons II
219
with the idea of system; CWP seem to be not only a specific system of propo-
sitions belonging to an FOL. Namely, there exists a strong relation between
action, system, and rules (as shown in Table 7.6). Let us first mention some rel-
evant sections divided according to these vital elements which are indispensable
in explicating certainty in OC. However, Table 7.6 needs further clarification.
•  A brief commentary. Life in OC is explicated as something in virtue of
which a certainty of CWP is revealed; more to that, life manifests practical
certainty, and we manifest our certainty, that we are certain, for instance, via
being certain regarding the existence of everyday things (OC 7, 9). We do
not act “insecure” when we are for instance reaching for everyday things like
chairs, newspapers, pipes and tobacco, believing that they may disappear.
For instance, in our “steady” FOL, if one acts “insecure” when reaching for a
pipe and tobacco, this is surely not the case because one doubts whether the
pipe and tobacco really exist. There must be some particular reason for such
insecurity, such as that one forgot his/her glasses, or that there are two quite
similar pipes on a writing desk so one needs to look-at them more closely than
usual. Consequently, if a doubt is not connected to anything common and ev-
eryday, then it is redundant and impossible (OC 117). This is so because this
practical certainty is like an FOL, like our life (OC 358–9). Regarding the two
columns on the right side of Table 7.6, they list sections primarily expressing
that CWP (among which are hinges but not only hinges) create a system (see
Stroll, 1994:156, and for the detailed comment Schulte 2005:59–75).
•  An action is not important only for a description of WP, but for special kind
of a descriptions as well, namely descriptions as quasi-justifications. These
justifications are “invented” as “connections” between parts of WP, and one
sees this when one comes to the end of such justifications, and the end is
the “unjustified” way of action (standard or routine practice, OC 110), and
if this is the correct interpretation, then surely it is a result of morphologi-
cal procedure. This is controversial idea, namely that theoretical certainty
Table 7.6. Connections between practice and the system (of beliefs, CWP).


220
Chapter Seven
Table 7.7. An example of a main topic.
is roofed by the practical (even regarding mathematical certainty OC 212).
One could find the reason for this idea in OC 307 where it is explicated,
as we already mention, that reasons for a practice are always less justified
than the practice itself that they suppose to justify (see here proposition 6).
•  The third element in Table 10.6 is the concept of a whole or a system
manifested via forms of life, or WP respectively. For instance, the truth of
our experiential propositions belongs to our system of relations (OC 83), a
certain structure (OC 102). Finally, what connects an action with a system
are rules (see here proposition 7). However, rules are implicit in actions,
but in the same time rules are manifested by actions.
There seems to be a difference between acquiring a practice, and practicing
a certain set of routines, or standard actions, or procedures. This is visible
in for instance Wittgenstein’s explication of the practice of calculating (OC
44–9). In the end, “certainty” seems to be “a decision for practical purpose”
(OC 49). On the background of this context, sections regarding D/UC will be
presented (as shown in Table 7.7), and the new list of subtopics regarding D/
UC will be suggested (as showed in table 7.8).



Anti-Sceptical Weapons II
221
In order to better understand the previously explicated standpoint one
needs to see that each and every one of these topics are not central because of
the reader’s likely impressions, opinions, common sense, or even background
knowledge in epistemology, rather because a particular topic can be arranged
into subtopics, and every particular section can be analysed in the light of a
particular subtopic or differently, and then one will see that it belongs to the
topic. Namely, this arrangement of topics, subtopics, and particular sections
is not arbitrary; on the contrary, it is mere consequence of previous explica-
tions and commentaries. Here, only the subtopic of doubt/uncertainty (D/UC)
is presented to show this particular point, namely, the epistemic reading (from
Moyal-Sharrock and Brenner 2005) with pragmatic background and context
as our addition to the reading (as shown in Table 7.8).
This division into subsections clearly suggests that there is a serious struc-
ture, perhaps not among notes, but surely among ideas of OC, at least among
ideas regarding doubt and uncertainty (D/UC) as one of the central topics.
The topic of D/UC is central since it is completely and directly determined
by “elements” emphasised in Table 8.5, especially by the nature of CWP.
Reasonable man mentioned in the last subsection in the second row is not to
be imagined as completely rational. In fact and by standard criteria, such a
man is “irrational” (in a primitive state, something animal), but on the other
hand such a man is completely “rational” if rationality could be explicated in
terms of acting in accordance with a CWP and an FOL. A human can be ra-
tional only within the particular FOL he or she belongs to. However, different
forms of life could be incommensurable or commensurable only accidentally,
Table 7.8. An example of the division of a main topic regarding doubt/uncertainty (D/
UC) into some subtopics.


222
Chapter Seven
and this goes along with the interpretation that later Wittgenstein is advancing
a kind of relativism. Now, in the same or similar way all other sections and
subsections of main topics could be arranged. Concerning importance, or so
to speak concerning basic ideas, the clue for an interpretation of mentioned
sections are basic categories emphasised in earlier works from TLP to PI.
•  This, in the opinion of the present author, gives some weight to epistemic
reading in the context of basic ideas regarding action, and practice. How-
ever, to point to this difference, that is to say the difference between at-
tributing fundationalism, contextualism, or coherentism (which could be
easily done if one for instance, sees system-sections isolated from other
topics), and attributing pragmatism is crucial for the present interpretation.
To be honest, there are elements of continuity between PI and OC regard-
ing all of these attributed epistemological standpoints. In view of that fact
our mentioning of PI 107, 224–5, 447 (in proposition (3)), and 155, 325
(in proposition (5)) does not seem to be sufficient, except for “the war of
quotations.” Something else is needed. Therefore, in excursus 2.1 it will be
shown that the sometimes emphasised “contextualism” of OC is in fact a
marginal thing since the similarity between Wittgenstein’s mentioning of
“context” (and similar words) is only accidentally connected with the older
or perhaps contemporary contextualist ideas in epistemology. To be more
accurate, CWP as being implicit, manifested, and sometimes explicable
from their FOL are not context-dependent (since they are the last context),
rather they are practice-dependent (since the whole of standard practices of
a community is in fact it’s FOL, or its culture, see Chapter 4, section 1.2).
EXCURSUS 2.1: CWPS AS PRACTICE-DEPENDENT,
NOT AS CONTEXT-DEPENDENT
However, mentioned problems start with the use of the word “standard” (in
terms of speech or action). Therefore, let us take the following examples in
which all of the used words (printed in italics) are easily replaced with the
word standard. Furthermore, in all examples the word “know” could be re-
placed with the word “certain” in the manner in which Wittgenstein claims
that one is beyond all reasonable doubt certain that P while P being axis
proposition (or more generally a proposition is belonging to CWP).
•  Ordinarily, if Willard wants to know what the time is, he can look at a
watch (on his hand, or at the clock on a wall, a building, or on a church).
When he sees what the time is, he will no longer be questioning, or guess-



Anti-Sceptical Weapons II
223
ing, for instance, that it seemed like 14:30, when it was really 13:30. Then
he knows what the time is.
•  If Willard goes to college every morning by bus which arrives at his station
at 10:15 it is common to expect the bus to be on time every morning, or
almost every morning, because he knows that buses are sometime broken
or involved in car accidents. Willard could even adjust his expectation of
the bus arrival if there is a new circumstance which will cause a later arrival
(say a football match between Rapid (Vienna) and Sparta (Prague)).
•  Commonly, if Willard is planning a picnic tomorrow, he would like to know
the weather. Consequently, it is normal to look at the weather report on the
telly, and if it is said that the weather will be sunshine and warm, then he
knows what the weather will be like (given that he additionally understands
many things regarding geography and meteorology).
•  Usually if Willard wants to know the meaning of some foreign or inter-
national word, he could look in a dictionary, and he could find the word
and its meaning explained there. For instance, if he wants to know what
the meaning of the word “robot” is, he could find out that it comes from
the Czech (and a few other Slavic languages) word “rabota.” The word
“rabota” means “hard labour” and “robot” was invented by the Czech
writer Karel Chapek who use it for the first time in his classic SF novel
RUR (Rusum’s Universal Robots).
•  If Willard wants to know-how to cut offshoots on his fruit-tree, the typical
thing to do is to ask someone engaged in horticulture, or at least to use some
horticulture brochure.
What is common to all these examples? Many things are common to all of
them, of course. First, we have a common man who wants to know something
commonly knowable. Second, there is a desired knowledge. And third, there
is the acknowledged way of knowing. The answer is always—Oh, it is 13:30
or—You can cut offshoots in this way, or if you like in that way, etc. In all
six examples the expression “to know” means “to be certain.” Many words
highlighted in these examples are emphasised in OC as well in terms of de-
scribing calm certainty, certainty which can be regarded as a sign of a steady
FOL going on, and of standard procedure. What follows is an incomplete
however sufficient list in terms of evidence that for each and every “axis”
(sometimes in the form of a proposition, sometimes in the form of series of
key words serving as basic material for any description of a practice), applied
that the practice is certain beyond all reasonable doubt as far as situation,
circumstances, and context are normal, usual, or everyday. Therefore, the
use of the expression “to know” is in fact metaphorical since doubt cannot
be grounded. On the other hand, if the use is literal, then it is in fact a misuse


224
Chapter Seven
or even an abuse of the expression, since there is no reason to claim to know
any such proposition. However, hinges are not so vividly manifested in these
examples, one needs to explicate them. Here are some such explications:
•  “Clocks tell time.,” “Buses come to stations according to their time-
tables.,” “Weather reports for next the 24 hours are quite trustworthy.,”
“Dictionaries are reliable.,” etc.
All of these are valid, certain, and implicit in normal, everyday, and ordinary
circumstances, situations, and contexts, because they belong to an FOL and
an FOL manifests such certainties via its particular practices, routines (Witt-
genstein), or standards procedures (Austin). As it was mentioned before, here
are some examples from OC.
•  Normally, everyday, ordinary Normal linguistic exchange. (OC 260) Per-
fectly justified and everyday. (OC 553) Ordinary circumstances. (OC 445)
•  Circumstances Certain circumstance, exactly what circumstances, in nor-
mal circumstances. (OC 25–27) The circumstances, which are actually,
held. (OC 111) Under ordinary circumstances. (OC 133, 445) Novel cir-
cumstances (OC 134) In particular circumstances. (OC 255) Such-and-such
circumstances. (OC 334–335, 603) Present circumstances. (OC 552–554)
Particular circumstances, in particular circumstances, imagine the circum-
stances. (OC 620–623) Situation Specific situation. (OC 326) The need
for determination of meaning by situation. (OC 348) Context Certain con-
texts. (OC 348) In the context of a conversation—out of all context. (OC
348–50) Out of context. (OC 533) The contexts which Moore mentioned.
(OC 620–3)
Now, a short commentary on mentioned sections will be given.
•  Normally, everyday, ordinary. Wittgenstein’s appeal to that which is nor-
mal, ordinary or everyday is typical common sense idea of justification,
which is contextualist stricto sensu (the point which will be discussed later
on). Besides that, in other Wittgenstein works it was connected mainly with
language, or, if with practice, then with linguistic practice. In OC on the
other hand, the connection is mainly with non-linguistic practices (learn-
ing, doing, behaviour, habits, customs, institutions, forms of life, stream of
life, and way of life). In OC 260 he speaks of normal linguistic exchange
which is the proper place of use of the phrase I know. Normal usage is con-
nected with, and it even perhaps depends on conversational circumstances.
It means that there must be a reason for usage of the phrase (if there is no



Anti-Sceptical Weapons II
225
such reason, then it is presumptuous by all means). There must be some
need for such a statement, like I know that I am sitting in a chair now. If
there is some need, reason, then it seems to be justified and the usage of
the expression is everyday (OC 553). In OC 445 one may emphasize the
word ordinary, because in ordinary circumstances, the very “ordinariness
of circumstances” is the only reason for reliability, and after all the last
justification possible.
•  Circumstances. If one thinks that the use of the phrases like We all know
(that the Earth is round), or I know…, or This child doesn’t know, (but it
can learn…), etc. is reserved only to special circumstances, then there is a
problem. One is often unable to say what one means by pointing out These
are the special circumstances… can’t you see!, however, one can sort them
out without serious problems, or at least to describe the range of abnormal
ones (OC 27, 29, where a connection to the rule following problem is
obvious). Here, a strong difference should be made. Namely, a child can
learn that the Earth is round, and then the child knows. If someone doubts
that the Earth is round because he is from different culture with different
level of scientific development, we cannot say to him “Well, we know that
the Earth is round,” but if the doubt is grounded in a mistake, then one
belonging to our FOL could say But, we know… and these are the special
circumstances. Special circumstances are connected with, on one hand,
any normal epistemic situations of teaching for example, and, on the other
hand, with abnormal epistemic situations in which there is a possibility of a
change of a WP and consequently of our practice as well. Thus, in normal
circumstances, when one says, I know that I have never been on the Moon
(OC 111) is certain as any possible reason which one can supply for its
certainty. However, how is this manifested in our actions? Any particular
CWP (application of a rule) is also a principle (rule) of certainty. We do not
act in accordance with the principle, but our actions show that a particular
CWP is certain for us (and that “foundation walls are carried by the whole
house,” OC 248, like an axis of rotation is immovable by a rotation of the
whole rotating body, a gyroscope, a planet, or a whirligig for instance).
There must be particular circumstances for doubting (OC 255). If there are
no such circumstances and certain characteristic manifestations of doubt-
ing, the proposition in question is absolutely certain (being grammatical,
empirical, or hinge proposition) because, “Something must be taught us as
a foundation” (OC 449) and that are CWP. Here the connection to sections
on nature of doubt and indubitability is important. Context. The question
of context connects the notion of usage of the phrase “I know” with its role
in language-game. As long as there is room for doubt in language game, or
for special circumstances, there must be room for certainty as well. And the


226
Chapter Seven
CWP is presupposed in every game of doubting. Nevertheless, there is also
an epistemic context. If one is sitting in front of someone else who can see
him clearly, then it is not a context for a sentence like I am here (OC 348).
Now, a few critical and developmental remarks should be made regarding
the mentioned sections. First, the approximation of Wittgenstein’s position
would be that our knowledge is context-dependent or that only in certain
circumstances or situations we have the right to claim knowledge of some
P, but it is not so, because what is really context-dependent is only our use
of the phrase I know that…. Therefore, some epistemologists concluded that
Wittgenstein advanced contextualism regarding knowledge. Our knowing
of propositions that can be known, and in the same time cannot be seriously
regarded as false, meaning that doubts regarding these are mostly silly, is not
context-dependent, rather it is so to say practice-dependent. It means that for-
practical-purposes they are absolutely certain axis-propositions of CWP. But,
•  “The fundamental idea of contextualism is that standards for correctly at-
tributing or claming knowledge are not fixed but subject to circumstantial
variation.” (Williams 2001:159)
M. Williams classifies the contextual factors as intelligibility constraints
(meaningful questioning), methodological necessities (propositions ex-
empted from doubt), dialectical factors (dialectical environment), economic
factors, and situational (external) factors (Williams 2001:160–2). In this
sense Wittgenstein is not a contextualist, first because CWP are not real
propositions (a real proposition cannot serve as a rule), so they cannot be
subjects to the process of justification. Second, because there is no theory of
justification for-practical-purposes in OC, yet, there is a series of epistemo-
logical examples describing our practices and such descriptions serve as rules
for deciding on any other (standard) issue whatsoever. Our actions illustrate
what we are convinced of, and our convictions show what is for us absolutely
certain and as such presupposed in all of our actions. However, what do we
mean if we want to call his standpoint contextualism? S. Cohen puts this in
the following way:
•  “One of the more puzzling aspects of the issues concerning scepticism is
the stark contrast between our everyday assertions concerning what we
know and the conclusions of sceptical arguments.” (Cohen 1990:161)
Wittgenstein never saw that there are such things as “everyday knowledge
attributions.” Still, there are propositions that are absolutely certain. Thus,



Anti-Sceptical Weapons II
227
contextualist criterion of knowledge is quite remote from Wittgenstein’s
criterion for being a CWP. The similarities are, if we can say it in this way,
quite circumstantial. When E. Sosa tried to comment on Cohen’s approach
to contextualism, as it seems, he made a mistake in including Wittgenstein in
the tradition of strict contextualism (Sosa 1990:171–3). However, Sosa, fol-
lowing Cohen, distinguished between:
•  context of knowledge in respect of how sensitive one must be to the weight
of evidence in one’s possession for or against a belief that P, and
•  context of knowledge in respect of alternatives to the proposition that P
counts as relevant (Sosa 1990:173).
The second type of context-dependence is closer to Wittgenstein’s view,
because CWP can be viewed as a quasi-foundationalist criterion of relevance
of alternatives. In this context one does not have to know that a certain al-
ternative hypothesis is false (like the hypothesis that there are cleverly dis-
guised mules in the pen, Dretske 1970:1015–6). We will try to sketch here
the Wittgensteinian type of answer. In these circumstances, one could say
many things concerning the nature and function of zoos and zoo authorities,
which are part of our WP. Consequently, our actions will reveal what we
are absolutely certain of, in this particular case about zoos, etc. In normal
circumstances this kind of doubt would not be possible. However, if the cir-
cumstances were unlikely or bizarre, then such doubts must be brought out,
as well as the reason for such doubt (if there is no reason for doubt, there
could not be any doubt in a language-game). However, if there is a reason
for doubt, then surely we must have some more information about the case
and the circumstances are not as normal or standard as they seemed at first
sight, but now one could answer the sceptic’s challenge. Only after the doubt
(accompanied with the good reason) is introduced in the language (epistemic)
game, we can say that we are certain that the animals in the pen are zebras.
However, we know that P in virtue of its being grounded in CWP and its clear
grammatical implication must be grounded there as well (an axis must be still
in order for grammar and experience (composing a river) to rotate). Thus, the
closure principle is preserved, but for quite different reasons. On the other
hand, if the doubt arises in normal circumstances, then what we have to deal
with is probably a quite different WP, FOL, or culture, and then it seems to
be “a failure to communicate,” not “a failure to know,” or “to be certain.”
•  An issue of relativism and rationality in Wittgenstein cannot be discussed
here, however, in short, and as a consequence of the presented interpreta-
tion, it could be said that—we humans are basically irrational, or to put it


228
Chapter Seven
somewhat mildly—mostly irrational, however, when two or more FOLs
clash, this irrationality becomes relative to the particular CWP and therefore
“internally” rational, but “externally” irrational and relative (see here (7)).
8.5. CONCLUDING REMARKS
Now, let us return to issues opened at the beginning of the chapter.
(i)  To continue with the propositions from the beginning of the chapter, stand-
ing under a tree and observing Felix in order to see whether cats grow on
trees does not belong to our FOL, we simply never do such things, and on
the contrary what we do regarding cats on trees shows what we are certain
of, among many things regarding cats and trees (a-b) and regarding the
(manifested) “axis” which says that cats do not grow on trees (c). Is there
a situation in which a proposition like (d) would make sense? Say that a
humanoid (a Martian), Smith, personally being a member of a distant and
unknown FOL, while visiting Earth, is learning human FOL with his teacher
Homo sapiens sapiens Jones. In the course of learning he could make vari-
ous mistakes, as well as (d). Now Jones, being a good teacher, does not sim-
ply claim (c), but shows and teaches Smith many practices regarding trees,
cats, etc. and Smith eventually changes his attitude from (d) to (c).
Now, the question is—is this, namely (i), how does it really work?
Take for instance a situation in which Willard’s mother before going to
work needs to move Felix outside to the backyard. Say that she needs to catch
the attention of Felix with a sheet of newspaper which he likes to rip apart (as
shown in Figure 7.2). Now, in such a situation there are no practical doubts,
namely doubts like “Is this cat really Felix?,” “Am I really calling him?,” even
“Would he eventually come out from his hiding place?,” etc. There are only
certainties implicit in a standard practice and in a standard situation (never
mind if this particular case seems to be private idiosyncrasy (the mother’s as
well as Felix’s), certainties such as “I am his owner and he is my daughter’s
cat Felix,” “I am calling him,” “He is hiding since he knows that he has to
go outside when I go to work, but eventually he will come out from his hid-
ing place when I start to crumple newspaper since he cannot refrain himself,
he simply likes to rip it apart.” These propositions are absolutely certain for
practical purposes; moreover, they are at the same time implicit in the very
standard practice, and manifested by it. Any standard practice transparently
manifests what a doer is absolutely sure of, and of what doubt is “grammati-
cally impossible.” However, given that for later Wittgenstein any logic is a



Anti-Sceptical Weapons II
229
part of philosophical grammar, a doubt could be “logically possible.” This is
possible only in a case where standard practice does not apply if the given
situation is not standard. It could be a case that Felix is already outside, or a
case that he cannot move since he is sick, or similar. If any of these is the case
that means that the situation is not standard. Then, a particular doubt makes
sense, however only as a heuristic instrument, namely as a device by means
of which one could change from a standard practice to its (most probable)
standard exception. Whether this solves the issue of justification we are not
sure since there are further questions that could be asked, issues which are of
no interest to contemporary epistemology whatsoever (perhaps some of these
Figure 7.2. Practical certainties at work.


230
Chapter Seven
could be of some marginal interest regarding the theory of action, philoso-
phy of culture, and cultural anthropology). In other words, a further, much
more reflexive question should not be—what is your “theory” of knowledge
(theories of knowledge as being theories are surely among many things that
should not be), since there are no theories in morphology, and consequently
in morphology of certainty as well, there are only better or worse descriptions
which could be more or less perspicuous regarding the matter in question and
this could be explicated in the following manner.
(j)  Empirical and grammatical propositions are certain because they are
proper parts of our language-games (implicitly or explicitly), and because
together with hinge-propositions they constitute a system of CWP (and
a system of standard practices of an FOL as well, since language-games
are parts of forms of life they belong to, PI 23). Let us introduce a new
metaphor. This system is like a whirligig rotating, moving around, and
changing its direction, creating a certain path. Regarding our choice of
metaphors (the metaphor of a planet, gyroscope, and Wittgenstein’s meta-
phor of the axis at the beginning of the chapter, and finally the metaphor
of a whirligig here), we were guided by Schulte’s idea that “The image
of the axis of rotation suggests that what turns around it forms a single
connected structure.” (Schulte 2005:72, emphasis added). Propositions
nearer to the surface of a rotating body are spongy and soft (empirical),
propositions nearer to its axis are more firm (grammatical), while its axis
itself is of a different material which is of high porosity (hinge proposi-
tions), and which at the same time in combination with these two materi-
als, creates a solid balanced body (as shown in Figure 7.3). On the other
hand, axis-propositions, as being a proper part of CWP, are certain beyond
all reasonable doubt because they are proposition-like manifestations of
practice which are descriptions of our standard practices (belonging to
our (human) FOL) as covering justifications of anything that we do or say.
The other way around, axes are implicit in and manifested by our standard
practices as their final “justifications.” Furthermore, they could be as it
were explicated for various practical purposes (being a simple exception
to a rule, teaching or acquiring, changing, abandoning, or inventing certain
practice), and if they are, then they function like axis-propositions, and as
such together with grammatical (hinge) and empirical they constitute a
system which explicates basic reasons for any particular practical purpose.
Therefore, the basic epistemological issue is an issue of actions, not of
facts, an issue of belief-in, not of belief-that, an issue of knowledge-how, not
of knowledge-that, an issue of practical, not of theoretical certainty, and an is-



Anti-Sceptical Weapons II
231
sue of calm practical certainty, not of one which still struggles. Wittgenstein’s
dissolution of: common-sense epistemology (and perhaps the advancement of
foundationalism as well; Moore type propositions and knowledge claims; see
Morawetz 1978, Stroll 1994), scepticism (in terms of idealist’s objections;
McGinn, 1989), epistemology naturalised, and various other standpoints and
objections in OC seem to be just a by-product of dissolution of the classical
epistemological agenda in total as being entirely misleading not so much
regarding answers, but first and foremost regarding its questions. From the
last two explications, (i) and (j), it seems to follow more naturally with what
we implicitly agree (without any explicit commentary on already mentioned
basic literature regarding the topic), that Wittgenstein’s morphology and the
grammar of knowledge is the grammar of practical certainty and that it is
contextualist, socialised, externalist, and holistic (Moyal-Sharrock, Brenner
2005). However, previous to these characteristics it is also pragmatic since
the final justification is certainty “that belongs to the constitutive rules of
practice” (Kober 2005:227). On the other hand, if one could picture “the
grammar of human knowledge” as a result of morphological inquiry and
perspicuously present it, like we attempted especially through the metaphor
of the whirligig, then the light should gradually illuminate the whole which
will not be seen-as a heap (of particular issues, particular cases, and particular
standard practices), rather as a system of practices as know-hows constitut-
ing our FOL as a system in which and by which we think, speak, live, and
perform.
Figure 7.3. The relation between a system of CPW and its practices (the
river image).


232
Chapter Seven
A completely different problem which was not discussed here, and which
could be a prospective topic is whether then know-that is just a special case of
know-how, or whether belief-that is just a special case of belief-in, or whether
a theoretical certainty is just a special case of a practical one. Further issues
that are implied here are also—what is Wittgenstein’s, or generally speaking,
“a pragmatist account” of knowledge and justification in terms of certainty
(and after all—if this is the answer, then what is the question regarding mor-
phology of human knowledge; is it something like—how do we humans func-
tion as practical beings, how do we know things or perform-actions, linguistic
or non-linguistic ones)? If it is so, then a morphology of knowledge is closer
to a morphology of human consciousness, action (see Chapter 5), of a human
social and cultural life, since after all, FOLs are forms of culture (see Chapter
4). Among many such cultures there are subcultures of their scientific com-
munities, and among these, a community of psychologists which in terms of
description include perhaps a note on their methods, results, their social life
as a community, their views on their own work and achievements (so to say
“ideology”) and this is precisely the point where Quine’s “epistemology natu-
ralised” comes in, namely as an extremely unique case of a wider description
in terms of “more thorough pragmatism” nicely supported by Wittgenstein’s
“epistemology morphologised.” And hinges, descriptions of experiences, and
above all axes are of utmost importance here, since they say, for instance,
that we are quite sure that a mouse cannot come into being “by spontaneous
generation out of grey rags and dust.” (PI 52)


233
Chapter Eight
Anti-Sceptical Weapons III:
Life and religion are full of colours
I am not a religious man [that is a member of a church or a religious com-
munity], but I cannot help seeing every problem from a religious point of
view. (L. Wittgenstein in Rhees 1970:94) Religion as madness is a mad-
ness springing from irreligiousness. Religious faith and superstition are
quite different. One of them results from fear and is a sort of false science.
The other is a trusting. (CV 15, 72)
8.1. BELIEF THAT GOD EXIST VS. BELIEF IN GOD
According to the “classical” view, a belief is a propositional attitude or a state
of mind comprising “its” propositional content (for instance the content “that
M. S. E. de Montaigne’s book An Apology for Raymond Sebond is opened
at page 74 on the desk”) and an attitude (or a feeling of approval) toward
the content (propositional content serving as a guide for an action (echoing
Hume), or a certain understanding regarding the whereabouts of the book).
The object of belief indirectly speaking is a proposition, or directly speaking
that the world and a proposition could be expressed in a sentence (“The book
is on the desk.”). Moreover, a belief includes the structure of a sentence, or at
least a certain logical form mirroring that of a sentence.
•  Accordingly, S believes that P where P is “the book is on the desk” means:
S holds that the book is on the desk, and that means: S has an attitude (feel-
ing of approval) toward the content that the book is on the desk.
However, one should remember that epistemically speaking, what belief as
knowledge-condition excludes, besides truth and justification, is nothing


234
Chapter Eight
more but ignorance. A religious belief appears to be like any other sort of
belief, nothing special. However, given that religious experiences or their de-
scriptions have some special features, we could say that religious beliefs are
not about things and their relations, like a book being on the desk, or like the
God being in Heaven, or being manifested (revealed) in the world, but about
human and non-human beings or persons, their mutual relations, and their ac-
tions; so, if their object is not a proposition or the world so to say, then they
are not beliefs at all in the first place, although they can be propositionally
constructed as beliefs. Consequently,
• S believes that the book is on the desk (one could translate as).
• If S would need the book, then S would look for it on the desk. as well as,
• S believes that if P occurs, then it is God’s will (one can translate as).
• If P occurs, then S would not complain.
Accordingly, as any other belief, a religious belief also seems to be linked
with how people would act in certain situations, and by that manifest how
they understand the situation (this is in fact contrary to what is really said on
the 74th page of the mentioned Montaigne book (centuries before Kant bor-
rowed it), since it runs as follows “[Man] … annihilating his intellect to make
room for faith” and it should be something like—annihilating his theory to
make room for practice (Montaigne 1993:74). Here it seems to be appropriate
to introduce the difference between mere technique or daily practice and a
symbolic practice, for this point see Bouveresse 2008).
•  This particular possibility, namely that a religious belief is construction
or derivation from a religious (symbolic) action, or even from the whole
religious aspect of an FOL, that it is a mode of how people act, and finally
how a religious action or a practice could be reasonable is the topic of this
chapter. However, a lot of issues should be cleared up in order for one to
reach this or a similar understanding of a religious belief, because roughly
speaking, much of the mainstream of contemporary philosophy of religion
is basically misleading if one wants to advance this kind of approach.
The innermost idea is that, if a religion is in fact a FOL, then is it possible to
argue that religious symbolic actions and practices are reasonable things to do
on the background of this religious aspect of an FOL. It will be argued that, if
they are reasonable things to do, then this can be counted as a kind of pragmatic
argument for reasonableness of religious belief. R. Harré asks the questions:
•  “What is it to be religious? Is it to hold a certain kind of factual belief? Or
is it to engage in certain language games, certain practices?” (Harré 2001)



Anti-Sceptical Weapons III
235
In another words, in the chapter the second answer will be explicated as plau-
sible. It will be done by formulating descriptions for two separate theses, but
with the same conclusion. The following explication will illustrate that: belief
that God exists (further abbreviated as BTG) and that belief in God (further
abbreviated as BIG), as faith, are epistemically unjustified beliefs beyond all
reasonable doubt, and that these beliefs are (under certain conditions) hypo-
thetically, and for practical purposes, reasonably acceptable things to believe
in the same sense, and in the same degree, as many of our so-called common-
sense beliefs (such as that there are material things, other minds, past events,
free-will, essence of spatio-temporal things, universals, properties, relations,
or beliefs-in love, friendship, government, justice for all, etc.) A similar
strategy is presented in the paper by A. Plantinga “Reason and belief in God”
(Plantinga 1983), and one should agree with that critical part of his “reformed
epistemology” because it seems quite convincing, yet we should disagree
with affirmative part because of some objections that will be mentioned
later on (regarding forms of proper basic beliefs see Chapter 7, Section 7.5).
However, the scheme here is to argue for practical justification for religious
belief, while A. Plantinga argues that Christian belief may be somehow war-
ranted (noted by Jeff Jordan), so the claim here would not depend on the
criticism of Plantinga’s project (for this point see Helm 2001:101–19).
Alongside the main target of this chapter, namely the mentioned idea
regarding BIG and BTG, an investigation of reasonableness of these beliefs
will be carried out without openly connecting it to the classical compatibilis-
tic thesis of Christian philosophy.
•  The classical compatibilistic thesis is that of Augustine, Anselm, Aquinas,
many of the contemporary neothomists, and recently also of the encyclical
letter Fides et Ratio (by Pope John Paul II), and it says that faith and reason
(perhaps religion and science as well) are compatible in principle, and also
regarding many disputed topics (like creation and evolution, nature of a
human mind, and similar), and this means—without putting those beliefs
in the theological context, or even in the context of so-called theory of
theological knowledge or theological epistemology (see for instance Kern,
Niemann 1994).
The argument which will be defended is quite simple and it includes two theses:
1.  that belief that God exists (BTG) is an epistemically unjustified belief,
but that it is hypothetic-practically rationally acceptable or reasonable to
believe that God exists (BTG) on the basis of the distinction between (a)
believing P without sufficient evidence and (b) believing the same P as
being a reasonable thing to do, and (1) on the basis of


236
Chapter Eight
2.  which says that belief in God or believing God (BIG) is also an epistemi-
cally unjustified belief, but that it is hypothetic-practically rationally ac-
ceptable or reasonable to believe in God (BIG), on the basis of the same
distinction (a-b).
Briefly, I will sketch the argument supporting the general thesis that epis-
temic unjustifiability of certain kind of beliefs ((BTG), (BIG)) does not entail
reasonable unacceptability of believing those things, or having those beliefs
(as reasonable things to do). Nevertheless, it is possible that S does not know
that P, or that it is uncertain for S that P, or that S is unjustified in believing
that P, but that does not mean that S is necessarily unreasonable if he/she be-
lieves/accepts that P, because to accept P seems to be an act of a kind of guar-
antee or assurance that S will perform actions of a certain kind in appropriate
circumstances and situations (like in J. L. Austin’s expose of knowledge/
certitude as a kind of guarantee in his classical essay Other Minds).
•  By epistemic justification I mean anything which leads S to knowledge or
justification (in a causal or non-causal way, and with external or internal
epistemic capacity or ground), if a person S believes P, and if the proposi-
tion ((BTG), (BIG)) is true, or if “a hypothesis” (in W. James’s terminol-
ogy) that P is true creates more benefits than costs for S, then the hypothesis
that P is false.
•  By reasonable acceptability or reasonableness I mean anything cognitively
acceptable or humanly acceptable in the theoretical, but previously to
that in practical sense of the word as well. As such, being reasonable is
a slightly weaker notion than being rational, especially than a logical or
mathematical criteria of rationality, because something could be logically
inconsistent, although reasonable to believe on some further grounds; but it
is nevertheless more practical in a sense that the conclusion(s) of such prac-
tical reasoning includes not just S’s willingness to act, but also that S surely
will perform an action under the appropriate or standard circumstances (and
sometimes even in an absence of such circumstances) if S is not relevantly
obstructed in performing an action (see Chapter 7, section 7.5).
In short, reasonableness is practical rationality which could be explicated
from practice itself (from an instance of practice, i.e. from a child washing
its teeth, and reasonableness of a practice comes not from its benefits to the
child, rather from the fact that the child is practicing “teeth-washing” in a
regular manner, that the child acquired a part of, or the whole FOL). Accord-
ingly, it is not just the matter of Aristotelian syllogism regarding practical
matters, but more to that a syllogism with a conclusion that implies willing-
ness to act and the fact of performance of a certain standard activity.



Anti-Sceptical Weapons III
237
Furthermore, reasonableness of practical rationality is based not just on
theoretical rationality, but what’s more vital, on the FOL of a person belong-
ing to a community, and that means that a practice must speak (and undeni-
ably it speaks) for itself (OC). Hence, if BTG is a kind of theoretical illusion
derived from BIG, and if BIG is not belief at all, rather a kind of performative
as a linguistic action or as a part of non-linguistic action or an FOL, then one
should discuss only BIG as a certain type of practice, and not as a type of
belief, justification, or even knowledge.
•  Accordingly, any epistemology of religious belief is in fact a description
or a perspicuous presentation (overview) of religious practice, FOL, and as
a small part of it, of religious language-games as well (language-games in
which some sentences (CWP) serve as descriptions of the whole practice,
and at the same time as basic guides for deciding on the issue—does some-
thing belong to the FOL or not).
•  Furthermore, as know-that implies belief-that, so know-how implies belief-in.
This is a crucial technical distinction of the argument (as shown in Table 8.1).
Regarding Table 8.1, the opposite to know-that is know-how given that the
former is propositional knowledge, and the latter is not. Know-that implies
belief-that (among other things, at least in classical compatibilistic strate-
gies regarding the relation of faith and reason), and belief-that is opposite of
belief-in which is implied by know-how on the basis of the same argument.
The vital idea is that know-how implies know-that but not vice versa, at least
for the type of cases in which “knowledge” of one’s own religion, experi-
ences, and FOL is of utmost importance.
•  Consequently, and quite odd as well, it seems that know-how implies
belief-in, but that seems merely pragmatically convenient (if such conve-
nience counts), since practice (FOL) implies and manifests a worldview
(world-picture, WP) at least in the context of late Wittgenstein (especially
in OC).
Table 8.1. Some distinctions in knowledge and belief.

8.2. EPISTEMIC JUSTIFICATION OF BTG/BIG
Now, we should turn to some objections to epistemic justification of BTG
and BIG.
3.  If S knows that P (BTG) beyond all reasonable doubt, then S also has justifi-
cation (of certain appropriate kind) for believing that P. (The epistemic prin-
ciple—that knowledge entails belief, or in symbolic fashion SKP ® SBP).
4.  S never knows that P beyond all reasonable doubt (BTG), (further scepti-
cal arguments are needed).
5.  Therefore, S cannot have justification for believing that P (BTG). (However,
S can believe that P, but he/she cannot have justified belief.)
However, here we should make at least some (no matter how trivial they
could seem) remarks on proposition (4). Accordingly, the following argu-
ments would be:
6.  If P (BTG) is true, if S believes that P, if S has justification of any kind
for believing that P, and
(6.1)  if the justification for P eliminates all relevant alternative situations
in which S would belief that P in spite of the fact that P would not
be true, then S knows that P beyond all reasonable doubt. These are
necessary and sufficient conditions for knowledge-that according
to the theory of relevant alternatives argument (Stine 1976:247–61,
Cohen 1988:91–123, 1996:430–3).
7.  It is epistemically impossible for S to have such justification for P which
would eliminate all relevant alternative situations.
The criterion of relevant alternative situations should be formulated accord-
ing to the given circumstances which are relevant to the case. As arguments
for proposition (7) we could as we in fact usually do, use arguments from ac-
cidental fallibility of human cognitive device, or so called general fallibilistic
arguments or arguments from systematic fallibility, or so called arguments
from brain in a vat (Putnam 1982, however, K. R. Popper seems to advance
a kind of Socratic epistemic fallibilism against Plato’s epistemic idealism).
8.  Therefore, S does not know that P (BTG). Let us presuppose that S
claims that he/she knows that P (BTG) on the basis of certain (for S also
subjectively adequate, but inter-subjectively speaking in fact inadequate)
evidence (so that P would be evident for S (here for instance Chisholm’s
criterion of “evident” will do)), for instance on the basis of the mystical
experience of God presence (Hood et. al. 1996).



Anti-Sceptical Weapons III
239
Regarding proposition (7), psychologists of religion sometimes argue that the
possibility of hallucination, and even some other cases (like serious personal
disorders), as cases of unauthentic mystical experience are the real possibil-
ity which is usually not easy to eliminate. Considering S’s justification (the
mystical experience), we could say that if such justification is not such that
it eliminates all relevant alternative situations (for instance a hallucination
caused by natural causes) via which S would believe P (BTG, like God’s
presence), and if S would claim that he/she knows that P even if P would be
incorrect, we must conclude that S does not know that P and that S is unjusti-
fied in believing P beyond all reasonable doubt.
8.3. FALLIBILISM AND RELIGIOUS BELIEF AS ACTION
First we have an argument from the point of view of systematic fallibility
(Nozick l982).
 9.  If S now believes that P (BTG), and if S is in the process of believing at the
very moment, then P entails Q ― that S is justified in believing that no (evil)
scientist is producing all of his experiences, thoughts, emotions and all other
intentional states, or that he sends him information by computer while S is
brain in a vat in his laboratory or that the scientist in any way deludes him,
so that these delusions can force S to believe that P (BTG).
10.  It is not correct that S is justified in believing that no (evil) scientist is...
11.  Thus, it is not the case that S is justified in believing P (BTG), because
S’s justification for P does not eliminate all relevant alternative situations.
Then we have an argument based on an accidental, however regular fallibility
(Goldman l986).
12.  If always, everywhere and for every S stands that he/she could be mis-
taken about any proposition P in the moment in which S believes that
P with absolute (subjective/objective) certainty, then a real possibility
seems to exist, that is to say that every proposition (including BTG)
which we hold to be certain beyond all reasonable doubt could turn false,
and in fact is false.
13.  It seems that always, everywhere, and for every S stands that... because
human beings are essentially fallible, and we must take seriously the
possibility of a mistake (even scientific knowledge is not certain in the
absolute and non-hypothetic sense).
14.  Therefore, every proposition which we hold as certain beyond all reason-
able doubt could turn false, and BTG/BIG are no exceptions.


240
Chapter Eight
15.  If (13) is correct, then fallibilism is also correct, and epistemological dog-
matism is false. Fallibilism is rooted in the idea that any of our opinions
about the world or about anything else might turn out false (Plantinga
1983).
The common idea, as it was formulated by K. R. Popper, is rooted in the
difference between Plato’s and Socrates’ epistemological agendas. Plato had
the idea that we, as copies of perfect ideas, should look for pure and perfect
knowledge of ideas, and Socrates thought that, since we are fallible beings
(taken as an empirical fact), the only thing that we could do is to minimize
the quality and quantity of mistakes regarding our attempts to knowledge, and
regarding this Socrates was a fallibilist, and Plato was a dogmatist. We take
dogmatism in the philosophy of religion as a position which holds that one
can know some propositions about God (invisible things, or things beyond
empirical knowledge, as formulated by many classical Hellenistic sceptics,
most notably by Sextus Empiricus), if not with absolute certainty, then nev-
ertheless beyond all reasonable doubt.
16. (13).
17.  Therefore, fallibilism is in principle correct, and consequently it is epis-
temically unjustified to believe that God exists (BTG).
18.  It is possible that S believing P would be a reasonable thing to do (BTG),
even if believing that P is epistemically unacceptable (Goldman l986).
19.  If proposition (18) is false, then for S it is also unreasonable to believe
anything about: material objects, other minds, and past events, etc. upon
insufficient evidence.
20.  But, if proposition (18) is correct, then, for S to believe that material
objects exist, and that they are such and such, etc. is a reasonable thing
to do, even if it is epistemically unjustified to believe such propositions,
because they, strictly epistemically speaking, rest on inconclusive evi-
dence.
21.  (18).
22.  Thus, it is a reasonable thing to do for S to believe P on the basis of insuf-
ficient evidence, and that entails that it is also reasonable to believe that
God exists (BTG), if that belief is of the same kind as beliefs about mate-
rial objects, etc. (but are they of the same or even similar kind?), and that
kind of rationality we can call hypothetic-practical rationality, because
it could be regarded as hypothetic-practical rationally to believe such
propositions (including BTG). But if we suppose that BTG is derived
from BIG, then the problem is not solved at all, rather it is just shifted to
the problem of BIG.



Anti-Sceptical Weapons III
241
With this we conclude the argument against the possibility of epistemic
justification of BTG, and in favour of practical rationality of BTG. The next
question is on what other grounds S believes that P, since P is irrational, but
not unreasonable to believe (as an act of believing), and since evidence is in-
sufficient, what is sufficient? The line of argument now turns to the difference
between belief-that and belief-in. That, as it seems, is the core of pragmatic
arguments for religious belief since a belief is considered not as a content of
belief, rather as an act of belief in the way that, if an act of believing P is per-
formed, then it produces certain benefits for a believer or a doer/performer.
The general form of the argument reads as follows:
(a) doing P brings about, or contributes in bringing about Q, and
(b) it is in your interest that Q is obtained.
(c) So, you have reason to do P (see Jordan 2004).
Doing P in fact means “to believe in God” and obtainment of Q means that
S’s preferences will be realized (a preference to lead a religious life would
be such a preference). What are religious people as religious people doing
is in fact only manifestation of their religious belief since the hidden idea is
that beliefs are essentially manifested in actions. What is it to be religious
could nevertheless be private, inner, and subjective, but it is in the same time
and as such manifested in the actions of (a community of) religious people,
and this is what “Believing P as doing P” means (echoing S. A. Kierkegaard
and Wittgenstein both, see Putnam 1992:144–5). Regarding the “obtainment
of Q,” it could be many different things but all of them are of fundamental
interest to the doer. These different things include explicit religious language
games (like prayers), actions (rites), and style of life (certain daily ethics),
but all these different things are just manifestation of religious beliefs. This
manifestation could be called “religious FOL,” and the premise (b) says that
it is in the interest of S that this religious FOL is obtained because it implies
a certain meaningful life on the background of which S will perform better.
To perform better and to lead a more meaningful life are the interests that for
S supplement this insufficient evidence. The next thing that we should do is
to describe what it means that “believing in God” means “doing something.”
8.4. TRUST AS NON-REDUCTIVE BELIEF-IN
The problem of belief-in God (BIG) as non-propositional belief is the prob-
lem of some Christians who are not pleased with emotive justification of their
religious beliefs (as if emotions are not primitive and fundamental, and as


242
Chapter Eight
Table 8.2. Belief-in and belief-that.
such nice motives for religious beliefs and FOL). Of course, the fact that they
are dissatisfied even with good reasons (for instance, regarding arguments for
atheism), or even with good inter-subjective reasons, does not entail that the
religious beliefs have an implicit and authentic/genuine (or intrinsic) cogni-
tive dimension, nor that there could be any kind of epistemic justification for
such religious beliefs (as BIG). Here we should reject as irrelevant all forms
of justifications of belief which are previous to (or such that lead to) BIG, and
also all forms of subsequent rationalization of religious beliefs.
(a)  In the form of BIG religious beliefs as in to believe someone, we take
the form as acceptable only if to believe-in someone / something is also
acceptable.
(b)  The form to believe-in is implied in, for instance, the form to believe-in
ideal as well as the form to believe-in person, and the form to believe-
in person is implied in the form to believe someone because both forms
entail the form to have confidence-in someone (Price 1971:149–159,
Heil 1996, Kern, Niemann l994), or to have personal acquaintance with
someone (as show in Table 8.2).
Consequently, it seems that the core of religious beliefs (as the relation
between human persons and God) is not a propositional form of belief (belief-
that) but a non-propositional form of belief (belief-in) at least in its primitive
version, yet on the periphery it can look like propositional belief. If it is
possible to reduce belief-in form to belief-that form, then any kind of a per-
sonal relation between humans and God (which is, as it seems, essential for
religious beliefs, at least in Judaism, Christianity, and Islam) would be impos-
sible, as well as relations between humans (and all other relations including
humans). In a similar sense, an act of faith as an act of having confidence in



Anti-Sceptical Weapons III
243
God and as a state of confidence in God would be impossible as well (at least
for Jews, Christians, and Muslims).
(c)  Therefore, it seems that belief-that presupposes belief-in for any kind
of personal relation, and also for the relation between men and God, of
course if a human is a person, if God is a person, and if humans can es-
tablish a personal relation to God as the only humanly possible answer to
the revelation since God per definitionem can establish a personal relation
to men.
(d)  Of course there is a huge obstacle here since the question is how it is
possible to have confidence (as “personal (and/or subjective) relation”)
in someone that you never met “in person.”
Consequently, in a way the argument is begging the question since it presup-
poses that there is a personal relation between religious men and God maybe
via rites or even via personal mystical experience. Of course, this cannot lead
to the conclusion that there is a God via mystical experience (noted by Jeff
Jordan). This last line of argument was followed by philosophers like W.
James in Varieties of Religious Experience, or by experts in religion like M.
Eliade in his masterpiece Shamanism. If I have confidence in a prime minister
of a state, then I can formulate my confidence in several propositional beliefs.
(e)  For illustration, “to have confidence in the prime minister” can be
analyzed as “to be convinced, as a citizen, that the prime minister will
perform his public duties to the best of his abilities” regarding my knowl-
edge of him and norms of democratic society.
(f)  But if one collects all these propositional beliefs, they do not entail
belief-in the prime minister (or in a friend, love, God, etc.), or belief in
the institute of a prime minister, or form “to believe the prime minister,”
because, it seems, that confidence is something more than the sum of all
propositional beliefs in question. These beliefs should be of a different
kind. The trust is the difference that makes the difference.
However, there are different sorts of trust regarding the objects of belief;
(g)  non-personal trust (like a trust in some machine that it will work prop-
erly),
(h)  personal regular trust (like when you ask a stranger “What is the time?”
and you trust him/her that it is 13:45), and
(i)  personal extraordinary trust (when a person is depending on someone
else’s actions).


244
Chapter Eight
W. James in “The Will to Believe” had the idea that trust is something
between (a) and (b), and consequently between (h) and (i). The situation
is similar with the confidence between friends or with a child who has con-
fidence in its parents, or with a confidence of religious humans in God, but
only analogously and metaphorically because if God is a person, then it is a
person of a totally different kind sub specie humanitatis. If it is so, then the
following type of proposition is correct:
23.  If S believes in S1, then S can properly believe some P about S1 (for in-
stance, that God is the Creator of all things, seen and unseen...).
24.  If it is possible to find and to fix conditions under which it is for S jus-
tified to believe-in S1 (BIG), then it is epistemically justified for S to
believe-in S1.
25.  It seems that it is impossible to find and fix such conditions:
(a)  because they are too concrete and too closely related to personal
experiences
(b)  and the entire life of a religious man, as background for all of his
religious beliefs. All we can find-and-fix is always quite unimportant
for BIG.
26. Thus, for S it is unjustified to believe in S1 (BIG).
8.5. BELIEVING-IN SOMEONE / BELIEVING SOMEONE
S’s belief in S1 does not involve the explicit assertion that S1 exists, because
if this assertion is relevant at all, then it is relevant only as an implicit asser-
tion, of course, for practical purposes. For instance, I really do not know (in
a strict epistemic meaning of the word) that material objects exist, that other
minds exist, etc. and in spite of the fact that I am convinced that such proposi-
tions cannot be known at all, it does not influence my practical life in which
these propositions function only as relevant hypothesis (as axis-propositions,
crucial and implicit parts of my worldview that manifest themselves in my
practice and FOL), and for the opposite propositions it stands that believing
them is a completely irrational thing to do.
(a)  Belief-in of any kind, type or structure cannot be completely reduced, to
belief-that, because if a belief-in is authentic, then somehow it describes
the basic feature or property of human beings, a property which is funda-
mental for all interpersonal relations.
(b)  Belief-in is by no means unaccountable; however it can serve as a de-
scription of various types (including religious) of behaviour, thinking,
speaking, and acting.



Anti-Sceptical Weapons III
245
(c)  More to that, for every particular case of belief-in (of type S believes in
S1) the question Why do you believe? can be asked and it is an entirely
fair question. All the reasons why someone believes in someone else do
not answer the question—why is someone confident in someone else?
(d)  Therefore, belief in God (BIG) is epistemically unjustified in the same
way as belief in a friend, justice for all, peace, etc.
(e)  The main reason for this standpoint is located in the very nature of the
belief-in (BIG), because BIG is located outside of the theoretical sphere
and humanly possible rationality, and inside of essentially personal and
interpersonal experiences (in the religious community) in which religious
men are religious and their beliefs and actions are basically understood as
answers to God’s revelation.
(f)  This sphere of religious practice is a kind of FOL of religious men and it
stands beyond the borders of the theoretical chain of epistemic justifica-
tion, and it is, in a way, a pragmatic disconnection or the breaking of the
chain of theoretical reasons.
(g)  On the other hand, religious belief as a type of religious practice can be
the question of trust, and as well, it can be the question of a social dimen-
sion of knowledge.
•  We should agree here with Wittgenstein from OC, regarding that one
cannot properly be said to know the hinge/axis propositions. However,
J. Jordan thinks that, “for what little it is worth, I have always thought
that Wittgenstein is probably overlooking a distinction here, perhaps
knowing that P, and knowing that P with certainty (or knowing abso-
lutely that P), such that in the former sense one cannot know the hinge
propositions but one can be said to know them in the latter sense”
(noted by Jeff Jordan).
However, one could make a psychologically grounded argument which
would entail that BIG as act/state (with included so-called dynamics of faith,
P. Tillich) is a hypothetic-practically rational thing to do.
27.  If S does the act A consciously (regarding the consequences of an action
in moral and non-moral sense) and authentically (here: regarding rules
of religious rites and ceremonies), then S is reasonable in doing the act
A (act of BIG).
28.  S does the act A...
29.  Therefore, S is reasonable in doing the act A (BIG).
Of course, this argument is correct only if one could formulate plausible cri-
terion for conscious and authentic acts (rites and ceremonies) with respect to
religious acts (of BIG) as such and besides that, the question is—does this
