Chapter Eight
hypothetic-practical rationality entail epistemic rationality of BTG, because if it
does, then there is no need for there to be anything conscious and authentic about
BIG in order for it to be genuine. I believe that the argument (23)–(26) shows
that (hypothetic-practical rationality) reasonableness of BIG cannot be reduced
to epistemic justification of BTG. This is the argument for standpoint (2).
8.6. W. JAMES’S ARGUMENT AND KNOW-HOW
If both previous arguments are valid and correct, then we can draw certain
conclusion from them:
30. that BTG and BIG are
(a)  epistemically unjustified beliefs beyond all reasonable doubt, but that
they could be
(b)  practically rational or reasonable (things) to believe in.
Here it should be added that I do not accept the results of the project of re-
formed epistemology (Plantinga, Wolterstorff, and others) because it claims
that we can stay in the theoretical sphere with epistemic rationality for reli-
gious beliefs with the dubious concept of proper basic beliefs and without
conclusive argument for existence of God (Plantinga l983, 1997, comment in
Kenny l992). Instead, I have tried to argue
(a)  for the possibility of rational acceptability of religious beliefs
(b)  as practical reasonableness of religious beliefs as reasonable things to do
(BTG, BIG)
(c)  where, for example, to believe in God, under certain conditions, is for S
the only reasonable thing to do,
(d)  because in general, impossibility of epistemic (adequate-theoretical)
justification (of BIG) does not entail hypothetic-practical irrationality
(of BIG),
(e)  and there are or could be certain motives and reasons for action, and also
a certain normative background (such as this is better to believe than
that, and in the religious aspect of an FOL it is not an issue if should
one believe-in or not, because if one could choose to believe-in on not to
believe-in, one would probably choose not to, and the point is that one
cannot choose, because if one is in such an FOL, then all these issues are
already settled, implicit and vividly manifested in all particular practices
of an FOL).



Anti-Sceptical Weapons III
247
We could see this conclusion (30) in the light of W. James’s solution:
(f)  that there are occasions in which for S it is rationally and according to
James also morally permissible to believe a proposition (BTG, BIG) even
in the absence of adequate evidence supporting that proposition if believ-
ing P is part of a genuine option for S (James 1959:11).
This kind of essentially practical rationality was much underrated because
non-propositional beliefs were not the main subject of traditional (and also
foundational) epistemologies. On the other hand, BIG could be a reasonable
thing to do. Of course, this idea is based upon the distinction between:
31. a proposition P being rational to belief and
32. believing P being a rational thing to do.
Accordingly, a particular proposition may not be rationally believable, none-
theless, to believe such a proposition may be the rational thing to do, (regard-
ing “beliefs as rule for actions” see Rorty 1979, 1991:65, 18, commentary
in: Allen 2000:220–36, Nozick 1993:64–93). The ground of this distinction
regarding religious belief is in fact well known as T. Aquinas’s distinction
between:
(a)  the very act of believing/faith (fides qua creditur),
(b)  and the content or what is believed (fides quae creditur).
Hence, it is rational to believe certain propositions in the prudential manner,
but not in the strict epistemic manner. James’s principle according to this
distinction is:
33.  For any person S and proposition P, S can permissibly believe P if P is:
intellectually indeterminate / indeterminate on intellectual grounds (what
BTG and BIG undoubtedly are), and is part of a genuine option (com-
mented in Jordan l997:356).
The central notion in the paper “The Will to Believe” is trust as the relation
between human agents and human and divine agents, and this type of relation
makes religious belief as belief-in “non-reductive” regarding reduction of
belief-in to belief-that, i.e. non-propositional beliefs to propositional beliefs.
A notion of trust is very simple but even as such it seems to be inappropriate
for epistemological analysis. In human relations, and in average or everyday
circumstances, to trust someone means to believe that a person performed,


248
Chapter Eight
is performing, or will perform an action in accordance with a standard pro-
cedure regarding the matter in question, or in special circumstances that a
person will for example keep a promise to do something (recently K. Lehrer
developed some interesting ideas regarding trust, especially self-trust).
(c)  Thus, if it is possible to describe the core of belief-in as believing-some-
one and that as trusting-someone, then it seems quite clear why it cannot
be reduced to belief-that.
Further differences regarding James’s notions of belief are all connected to
belief-in as non-reductive, and in a way to knowledge as know-how as a non-
reductive type of knowledge regarding the very reduction to the know-that
(propositional knowledge).
•  However, if it is correct that belief in is not reducible to belief that, it seems
that the former implies the latter. If belief in does imply belief that, the whole
argument will be affected (noted by Jeff Jordan). In order to bypass this
problem one should differ between reductive and non-reductive belief-in.
Therefore, one could also differ between reductive and non-reductive belief-
that, since it seems obvious that some beliefs-that do not imply beliefs-in,
for instance, if S believes that there are X planets in Y solar system, or that
most philosophers are lazy, or that Brown very much likes to drive his Ford.
Key concepts of W. James’s approach to BIG are:
• hypothesis as anything that is believable,
•  live hypothesis as any hypothesis that is a real possibility for S where the
measure of “real possibility” of hypothesis is S’s “willingness to act,”
•  and an option as choice between two live hypotheses, and an option could
be:
○ genuine (S cannot be indifferent),
○ live (like being atheist or religious for instance),
○  forced (S must make a choice),
○ and momentous.
James claims that BIG is such option. There is however one more intrigu-
ing consequence of James’s distinction if we apply it to religious beliefs as
fundamentally non-reductive beliefs-in.
(d)  Namely, if believing P for S is a reasonable thing to do, then although P is
unjustified belief, S surely by doing that (i.e. via believing P) displays know-



Anti-Sceptical Weapons III
249
how of some sort (at least of a certain type of action, and implicit in action
via manifestation by de facto performing an action is willingness to act).
(e)  This is a sort of Wittgensteinian position which says that consciousness,
willingness, emotions, beliefs, etc. are manifested in our actions, practice.
There is no strict evidence in James’s paper “The Will to Believe” regard-
ing this notion of religious belief, but James starts his paper (lecture) with
a notice that what he is going to talk about is “something like a sermon on
justification by faith” (James 1897:1).
34.  Therefore, this is not a justification of faith (perhaps such thing is overall
impossible), i.e. religious belief, rather a justification of “attitudes” and
“actions” by faith. Perhaps one could say that this is in fact an argument
for BIG, but of a special kind (as shown in Table 8.3).
Therefore, it is possible to say that believing in God (BIG) is a reasonable
thing to do without opening the question about epistemic justification of such
a belief (it is similar for BTG).
(a)  Furthermore, even if it is impossible to know that God exists (it is enough
for S to have some indications), and if it is impossible to say, or to ex-
plicate why men believe in God (psychology and sociology of religious
Table 8.3. An overview of arguments for BTG / BIG (with the emphasis on pragmatic
arguments).


250
Chapter Eight
belief will do), that does not imply that it is always, everywhere, and for
every S to believe that God exists, and to believe in God as an irrational
thing to do.
(b)  On the contrary, sometimes it is the only rational thing to do, and if it so,
then that is the practical core of rationality of religious belief.
8.7. WITTGENSTEIN’S PRAGMATISM (NOT FIDEISM)
Wittgensteinian fideism is one of the major standpoints among Wittgenstein
scholars regarding his views on religion and religious belief. This instantly
opens the question—was he irrationalist, or perhaps anti-rationalist?
•  The problem of rationalism in Wittgenstein, or even the problem of ratio-
nality is quite a different issue. Nevertheless, it should be briefly noted that
while Wittgenstein seems to be irrationalist, or even against rationality,
he is rationalist in some matters regarding religion, for example in regard
of the fact that religious actions are part of religious forms of life and that
they are as such rule-governed activities (on the matter of his irrationalism
and especially voluntarism see Glock 2001:213–216 about Wittgenstein’s
emphasis on action and will against theory and intellect, see also Labron
2006:46–74).
Nowadays we can hear different voices, namely those who claim that what
seems to be correct interpretation is so to speak an “FOL account of religious
belief” which is presented as compatible with fideism.
•  What fideism says is in fact that a religious belief is the matter of believing,
of faith, not of knowledge, for example not of proving God’s existence.
Moreover and positively, fideism also stresses rationalist efforts as some
kind of a heresy.
Wittgenstein may be irrationalist on one hand, since he emphasizes that any
religion is (like many other things) a matter of action and motivation, not
of theory and intellect, but he cannot be fideist on the other hand, since he
claims that religion is in fact FOL consisting of actions as rule-guided activi-
ties. Consequently, he could be a pragmatist in a way (generally, surely not
like James, but in the matter of religious belief much closer to Pascal, and
Kierkegaard for instance), but surely not a fideist. Personally, I favour this
“FOL interpretation,” and I will try to give some indications in its direction.
More to that, like other pragmatic arguments it seems that this account of re-



Anti-Sceptical Weapons III
251
ligious belief could be counted as a pragmatic argument in favour of reason-
ableness of religious belief (Pascal, Kierkegaard, James, etc.). It is counted
as a “pragmatic argument” and as “a kind of pragmatism” because there is
no better expression, not because one should believe that he was pragmatist.
Norman Malcolm captures the essence of Wittgensteinian fideism when he
writes:
•  “Religion is a form of life; it is a language embedded in action—what
Wittgenstein calls a language-game. Science is another. Neither stands in
need of justification, the one no more than the other.” (Malcolm 1976:212)
○  The Urtext of Wittgensteinian Fideism is Winch’s essay “Understanding
a Primitive Society.” (Commented on by B. Matthews in his excellent
paper: “Wittgenstein, Religion and Lebensform: Or Why Wittgenstein is
not a Wittgensteinian Fideist”).
35.  Rationality of religious beliefs (religious worldview, “religious point of
view” (Wittgenstein’s remark to Drury, in: Rhees 1970)), i.e. believing in
God as reasonable thing to do, is essentially manifested in morphological
structure and grammatical representation of religion which consists of:
(a)  language games (even the text of the Apostolic Creed),
(b) actions (rites), practices (mysticism, ordinary spirituality, morality),
(c)  and FOLs of religious people, and if it is an issue at all, surely is an
issue of practical rationality or to put it somewhat more mildly—
practical reasonableness, or practice.
As it seems, Wittgenstein underlines is this deep difference between belief
and unbelief in a way that a man who believes something regarding religion
seems to a nonbeliever like a person from a different country or a distant and
unknown culture (FOL), perhaps our contemporary Western culture (on this
point see Phillips 1996:201–17).
Of course, another interesting question is whether this difference is a dif-
ference within a certain culture, i.e. between believers and nonbelievers, or
is it a difference between two cultures. On the other hand, Wittgenstein often
says that religion in general is for him an “intellectually” distant phenom-
enon, but not “practically.” For instance, proving God’s existence means to
add certain intellectual analysis and foundation to religious belief, and not to
give a reason or an argument for God’s existence.
A person may be convinced that God exists only by a certain kind of
upbringing, by shaping life in such and such a way, by being involving in
certain FOL (similar opinion is ommented in Phillips 1996).
Moreover, one cannot easily reduce religious beliefs like BIG to proposi-
tional beliefs like BTG, but of course one can derive BTG from BIG as the


252
Chapter Eight
description of an action, or of an FOL. If this reduction would be possible,
then epistemology of religious belief would be possible, and as would truth,
justification, and knowledge of God. But this would be an “illusion of justifi-
cation” (Wittgenstein) and knowledge, unless one construes knowledge as a
kind of guarantee regarding our attitudes and actions (Austin).
36.  Because Wittgenstein puts so much stress on behaviour many scholars
were led to the fideist standpoint. However, Wittgenstein’s concept of
behaviour is in fact a concept of action of the whole of living human be-
ings (reasoning included). Therefore, there cannot be strict relation here.
After all, he is using the word “Handlung” which is properly translated to
English as “action,” not as “behaviour.”
(a)  Furthermore, while the inner part of a human being is hidden it is never-
theless essentially manifested in forms of action, and with this position
he is completely against behaviourism and consequently against fideism
(Richter 2004, Glock 1996, Hacker 1999).
(b)  Maybe the conclusion that he was fond of fideism comes from his em-
phasis on faith against superstition, not so much against rationality. He
regarded religious superstition as “a sort of false science” (CV 72), and
faith as a matter of true religiousness.
Moreover, he thought that rationalist philosophers of religion (engaged for
instance in search for proof of God’s existence) are also superstitious. On the
other hand, in various places he writes that religiousness (perhaps like (classi-
cal) art) can make a better man, not in view of the fact that atheists are worse
than religious people, but almost in James’s terms mentioned before, namely,
leading a better (more fulfilled) life.
37.  The purpose of religious language games is not to display beliefs, or
even to be the justification for such beliefs, rather to describe, to give an
overview, for example to comfort, so there are only practical purposes, or
practices themselves, descriptions, or perspicuous presentations of how
things are done in religious FOL.
Wittgenstein says on various occasions (see LC 77 for example) that believing
is a matter of action and willingness. For instance, to believe in God means to
act in accordance with a commandment, not to have a special kind of belief,
or a belief with special content. And “God has commanded” means—it must
be done, or otherwise God will punish anyone who does not do it.



Anti-Sceptical Weapons III
253
This was as it seems Wittgenstein’s point regarding religious belief espe-
cially in “Culture and Value” and this is similar to his position in “Lectures
and Conversations on Aesthetics, Psychology, and Religious belief” and in
“Remarks on Frazer’s The Golden Bough.” His position as “the FOL ac-
count” of religious belief and religion could be interpreted from his known
and obvious position of “understanding of religion from the outside” (Glock
1996:321–3).
•  Religious statements do not describe any kind of reality, and do not make
knowledge claims. Rather, they express commitment to an FOL.
• The term “God” also expresses such a commitment.
• Proving God’s existence is a kind of superstition.
• Religious language is sui generis a language-game.
•  What gives meaning to religious beliefs is their role in practice of the be-
liever (CV 85).
38.  These points, especially the last one can constitute “a pragmatist” ac-
count of a religion, and perhaps in some quite far consequence, a cultural
account of a religious aspect of a language game and an FOL as well.
From such a standpoint certain morphology could be applied in the
(morphological) research of religions, and perhaps an overview of some
elements could be achieved (in the light of the fact that such method may
be essential for contemporary projects of a comparative study of world
religions as it is sometimes labelled).
8.8. ANNEX: THE MORPHOLOGY OF
KNOW-THAT AND KNOW-HOW
The morphology of use of words in some obvious cases can be broadened
to actions like in case of verbs. However, there are some cases traditionally
important to philosophers like thinking, human acting, knowing, etc. which
are good examples as well. In this section the expression “to know x” and
the very activity of knowing will be examined by means of morphological
method. If one observes human knowledge one immediately notices that
human knowledge does not have a unique pattern. This particular diversity
comes from basic difference in the very concept of knowledge. This differ-
ence is between:
• know-how and
• know-that.


254
Chapter Eight
The first is also called non-propositional or practical knowledge, and the
second propositional or theoretical knowledge. For instance, knowing how to
ride a bicycle is know-how, and knowing of which parts and their relations
a bicycle is composed of is know-that. Criteria for know-how are obviously
completely different since know-how includes for example a belief-in as a
rule for action, and action as know-how manifests belief-in (and this is in fact
a belief-minus, see Allen 2000:228). Obviously, for humans it is possible to
have know-how without know-that and vice versa. However, there are many
other “types” of knowledge which should not be forgotten, like the following:
•  Know-why (causes, reasons, motives, drives), know-who, know-where,
know-when, know-of, etc.
These types of knowledge obviously include somewhat different aspects
then two previously mentioned types no matter if they can be included under
know-that type.
•  Know-who. For example, to know-who can mean knowledge of a person in
relation to some situation like in “I know that John was in the living room
before me,” but it can mean knowledge of a person in terms of person’s
character like in “I know John quite well and I can say that he is a person of
fairly high integrity.” This later meaning of “know-who” obviously cannot
be subsumed under know-that without serious harm to its use, while with
the former it can be done without obvious problems.
•  Know-of. Another example is “know-of.” Namely, one can know-of some
book, event, or a person like in “I know of him” and this is obviously one
distinct meaning of the expression. However, one can know-of one’s own
inner world, say one’s own emotions, motives, drives, intentions, thoughts,
plans, expectations, etc. like in “Before I was not sure, but now I know of
my emotion regarding this matter, that is I completely dislike it..” This last
use as well as in the case of know-who cannot be reduced to know-that
without serious problems. Normally, we differ between say “I know that
anger is this feeling and I can describe it,” “I know how to manage my
anger,” and “I know I am angry now.” This last utterance looks somewhat
odd since regularly it seems to be sufficient to say “I am angry” and the
utterance “I know I am angry now” could serve as a kind of clarification of
a not sufficiently clear emotion to oneself or to some other person.
These two examples show that the use of the expression “to know” is much
richer then the first differentiation really suggests, almost as the differen-
tiation misses some great portion of usage which is obviously quite normal,



Anti-Sceptical Weapons III
255
standard, and has important practical purpose. More to that there are cases
which show that there is no sharp difference between know-how and know-
that (few of these are represented in Table 8.4).
Now, what is interesting here is not the possibility of reduction of know-
that to know-how or vice versa (procedures which were agenda of some
philosophers, or indeed some schools). On the other hand, what seems to be
of some interest here are intermediate cases. Any such case would have to
manifest characteristics of both types of knowledge.
•  In this respect, knowledge would be any “content” which is discovered via
investigation, or any “skill” which is acquired by imitation, or invented
(created), and both of these can be routinely used, applied, and transferred
to other members of our culture (FOL).
The question here is—are there any possible intermediate cases which would
be best explicated by such description.
•  Say that one safely stops her car on a highway because of a flat tire. What
one needs in such situation is knowledge of repairing a flat tire. Now,
knowledge of repairing a flat tire may consists of many things: (a) it can
be a part of theoretical knowledge about cars, but (b) it can be a part of
practical skills consisting of ability to repair many broken things on cars
including flat tires as well. However, in both of these extreme cases some-
thing is implied, namely a kind of knowledge of a manual which consists
Table 8.4. Basic morphology of knowledge.


256
Chapter Eight
Table 8.5. The place of intermediate knowledge.
of knowledge of how to repair a flat tire. If both (a) and (b) will result with
an actual repair of a flat tire, more or less handy depending on ratio of
know-that and know-how about cars and tires, and if such actual repair +
describing what one actually does while she repairs a flat tire, is the only
criterion of such half-know-that/half-know-how knowledge, than this is
such intermediate case.
There are many intermediate cases (driving a bike, a car, making lunch, making
a tent, drawing, sculpturing, painting, playing football, rowing, playing some
card games, playing billiards, in fact most of games are intermediate cases, but
some basic cultural (-anthropological) skills as well), namely all of cases which
include at least some know-how, or at least some know-that. All of intermedi-
ate cases accordingly have the similar structure which obviously makes them
quite dissimilar to the pure cases of know-that and of know-how. The question
here is—can this structure be described clearly? Perhaps the following structure
would be such description of this type of knowledge (as shown in Table 8.5).
The Table 8.5 simply situates this intermediate knowledge (for historical
reasons it is not convenient to label it as “a middle knowledge”) between more
or less pure know-that and know-how. Now, regarding the very nature of this in-
termediate knowledge it should be said that the basic characteristics of it include:
• Being implicit in cultural routines,
• Manifestation of such knowledge by practicing it,
•  Possibility of explication of such knowledge in various special cases in-
cluding:



Anti-Sceptical Weapons III
257
○ Upbringing of younglings into a FOL,
○ Clash with another FOL,
○ Change of a practice, and
○ Replacement of a practice with the new one.
Pure know-that and know-how do not have these characteristics at least as
relevant or such that one should create relevant similarity of this type of
knowledge with any of these drastic cases. Therefore, these characteristics
create a pattern of intermediate knowledge. However, this pattern creates a
nice network together with know-that and know-how. On the opposite sides
on borders of this knowledge-network there are characteristics and patterns
which do not share any direct similarity; for instance theoretical knowledge in
physics, mechanics, inorganic chemistry and mining does not have any direct
connection or similarity with a skill of production of stone spearheads. How-
ever, if a tribe due to some circumstances moves to a new environment in
which there are no stones which are similar to these at previous environment,
then some intermediate knowledge is necessary in order to test new materials
and to find the most suitable one for spearheads. There are some other char-
acteristics of intermediate knowledge which do not belong to it internally but
externally, such as the very use of such knowledge.
•  Many experts in various fields (such as Hayek in economics, or Wittgen-
stein, Ryle, Austin, Hintikka, Rorty, Perry, Pollock and other in philoso-
phy, epistemology, and epistemic logic, and some other experts in social
psychology applied in, for instance, examination of consumer behaviour)
claimed that there is particular type of knowledge which is a mixture of
know-how and know-that which enables members of a culture to quickly
accustom to new circumstances which are as it were unforeseeable. Some
of mentioned experts, such as F. A. Hayek, even claimed that this type of
knowledge is fundamental and primordial because it enables one to act
under uncertainty, lack of information, and previous experiences which is
often a case in modern world.
•  There is another external characteristic as well, namely what one who has
such knowledge is able of, because one learns certain techniques, skills,
arts, and crafts, is knowledge of routines in terms of daily standard actions
in various activities, but one also knows how to act in extraordinary situa-
tions, and both of these is possible because one adapts, one is inventive, and
witty in new and quite unknown circumstances and environment.
These differences can lead one to some further differences in the concept of
knowing (as shown in Table 8.6).


258
Chapter Eight
Now, Table 8.6 shows similarities and connections between different under-
standings of knowledge. One can also present dissimilarities and disconnec-
tions between the same concepts. More to that there are even more concepts.
This particular table raises many questions regarding our conception of know-
ing, compare for instance knowing why of crucial elements of Caravaggio’s
painting “Conversion on the way to Damascus” with knowing why fires start
during hot summers in costal areas with the little rainy days per year, and that
with knowing why the whether is so unpredictable, and that with knowing why
people in bigger groups are less motivated to help an individual in need then
people in smaller groups (it seems that W. James in his “The Will to Believe” in
fact discovered this mechanism of self-fulfilling prophecy, nowadays so com-
mon in social psychology textbooks, and perhaps interesting for general public
because of its nowadays so often misuse in financial and banking sectors).
Such comparisons can give a completely different and much broader picture
of knowledge and of belief as well. Such picture could seem to be completely
unorganized to one who does not see all of the patterns of knowledge and belief
creating quite clear network of this or similar kind.
8.9. CONCLUDING REMARKS
Therefore, we can conclude with an FOL account of religious belief as reli-
gious action.
•  Religious beliefs are not “about something,” they do not have a property
of “aboutness,” and maybe they are not beliefs at all, since there is cer-
tain enormous difference between belief-that and believe-in, and between
belief-in and trust (Harré 2001).
Table 8.6. Similarities between different conceptions of knowledge



Anti-Sceptical Weapons III
259
○  Believing (in) God is not a matter of belief, and consequently of knowl-
edge, but rather of action and of FOL (Wittgenstein CV 50, 82; Glock
1996).
○  Believing-in God, and consequently believing–God as a practice (lin-
guistic and non-linguistic) is a part of FOL (as well as believing in
everyday objects, past events, other minds, beauty, justice, etc. are parts
of other FOL).
•  FOLs are in fact the whole of certain systems of practices, and each case of
a certain practice explicates its rule according to which it is performed by
simply being a certain case of certain practice, and by simply being done.
More to that, by being performed, a grammatical nature of practice is mani-
fested (for instance “We pray to God like this”).
○  Believing-in God as an FOL is like any other FOL, or like any other
aspect of an FOL, i.e. its rule is implicit in it as it is practiced, and the
very practice reveals the rule and via the rule also derived is practical
reasonableness of the practice (in terms of the weaker notion of rational-
ity mentioned at the beginning).
•  Consequently, epistemology of religious belief ends up in a theory of (reli-
gious) action (practice), or in cultural psychology/anthropology of religion
(like in W. James, or M. Eliade) in view of the fact that religiousness is
first and foremost an FOL, i.e. a way of living, a certain system of actions,
linguistic and non-linguistic. (Perhaps this point can be extended to the
whole epistemological project as Rorty suggests. “Anybody who beliefs
snow is white does not know much about snow. […] Such knowledge is not
impeccably justified belief. It is not a belief at all. It is non-propositional,
perceptual, kinaesthetic knowledge.” Allen 2000:228).
In short, FOL account says that religious beliefs as well as religious language-
games are sort of religious practices, and the whole system of these practices
is the religious aspect of an FOL as certain context or background. Reason-
ableness of religious practices (beliefs-in, language-games, rites, ceremonies,
etc.) as a weaker notion of rationality is implicit in these practices, and these
practices manifest their reasonableness (or, believers manifest their reason-
ableness), simply by being practiced on the foundations of a certain religious
FOL, because any practice in the end must speak for itself (in terms of being
the “rough ground”).
Any particular religious action (believing-in God by praying, baptizing,
etc.) could be a reasonable thing to do or not to do only in the context of
the whole religious FOL. Furthermore, the religious aspect of an FOL is an
aspect of an FOL like any other … scientific, political, industrial …, i.e. a
certain set of practices, routines, and of course extraordinary actions which


260
Chapter Eight
could be regarded as such only on the background of the whole FOL and/or
culture (perhaps as its aspect if one could consider a FOL as “multi-aspec-
tual” phenomenon).
On the other hand, certain practices, routines, and extraordinary actions
surely constitute the religious aspect of an FOL, but in order to bypass con-
stitution fallacy one should claim that these are not parts of it once the form
is constituted (upbringing is finished, so to say).
The life of a religious person, even doubts about religious issues (like for
instance “Is God really watching over the world if thing like this could hap-
pened”), is the whole on which any particular action makes certain sense, and
it is a kind of background of understanding of any particular action. However,
any such particular action, is not eo ipso justified or not, reasonable or not—it
is simply understandable. In a way it is beyond being justified or unjusti-
fied, “as something animal” (PI 217, OC 358-9). Religious FOL, as being
“something animal,” as an FOL, or as the practical (calm) certainty, should
not be regarded as primitive in terms of the primitive religious beliefs, rather
in terms of the primitive religious practices.
Nevertheless and in terms of criticism of Wittgenstein, one should not
forget the dynamics of our own FOL, being religious FOL or not, i.e. how
we acquired it, how it was changed in various ways during our lives, how we
doubt it, how it overlapped with different FOLs from time to time (influenc-
ing them and being influenced by them in the same time), and above all how
it appears to give meaning to our own lives, at least sometimes, at least for
some time, because…
•  “Every soul has religion, which is only another word for its existence. All
living forms in which it expresses itself—all arts, doctrines, customs, all
metaphysical and mathematical form-worlds, all ornament, every column
and verse and idea—are ultimately religious, and must be so.” (Spengler
vol I, 1965:385)
